On Bruhat–Tits theory over a higher-dimensional base
Vikraman Balaji, Yashonidhi Pandey

Abstract




Abstract.
Let
k
be a perfect field. Assume that the characteristic of
k
satisfies certain tameness assumptions. Let
\mathcal{O}_{n}:=k\llbracket z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}\rrbracket
and set
K_{n}:=\Fract(\mathcal{O}_{n})
. Let
G
be an almost simple, simply connected affine Chevalley group scheme with a maximal torus
T
and a Borel subgroup
B
. Given a
n
-tuple
{\bf f}=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n})
of concave functions on the root system of
G
as defined by Bruhat–Tits, we define
n
-bounded subgroups
{\tt P}_{{\bf f}}\subset G(K_{n})
as a direct generalization of Bruhat–Tits groups for the case
n=1
. We show that these groups are
schematic
;
i.e.
they are valued points of smooth
quasi-affine
(resp.
affine
) group schemes with connected fibres and
adapted to the divisor with normal crossing
z_{1}\cdots z_{n}=0
in the sense that the restriction to the generic point of the divisor
z_{i}=0
is given by
f_{i}
(resp. sums of concave functions given by points of the apartment). This provides a higher-dimensional analogue of the Bruhat–Tits group schemes with natural specialization properties. Under suitable assumptions on
k
, we extend all these results for an
(n+1)
-tuple
{\bf f}=(f_{0},\ldots,f_{n})
of concave functions on the root system of
G
, replacing
\mathcal{O}_{n}
by
{\mathcal{O}}\llbracket x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rrbracket
,
where
\mathcal{O}
is a complete discrete valuation ring with a perfect residue field
k
of characteristic
p
. In particular, if
x_{0}
is the uniformizer of
\mathcal{O}
, then the group scheme is adapted to the divisor
x_{0}\cdots x_{n}=0
. In the last part of the paper, we give applications in characteristic zero to constructing certain natural group schemes on wonderful embeddings of groups and also certain families of
2-parahoric
group schemes on minimal resolutions of surface singularities that arose in an earlier paper by the first-named author.

Keywords.
Bruhat–Tits group scheme, parahoric groups, concave functions, wonderful compactifications, degenerations

2020 Mathematics Subject Classification.
14L15, 14M27, 14D20

1. Introduction

Let
G
be an almost simple, simply connected affine Chevalley group scheme. Let
\mathcal{O}
be a complete discrete valuation ring (DVR) with a perfect residue field
k
. Let
\mathcal{O}_{n}:=k\llbracket z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}\rrbracket
and let
K_{n}:=\Fract\penalty\ \mathcal{O}_{n}
; more generally, we consider
{\mathcal{O}}\llbracket x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rrbracket
when the residue characteristic
p
satisfies some tameness assumptions; see Section
1.1
. The object of this paper is to define and study an optimal class of subgroups
{\tt{P}}\subset G(K_{n})
which we call
\tt n
-bounded subgroups
. These are natural generalizations of the classical bounded groups and parahoric groups due to F. Bruhat and J. Tits,
who dealt with the case
n=1
(
cf.

[
BT84a
,
BT72
]
).

We show that these
n
-bounded groups are
schematic
in the sense of Bruhat and Tits;
i.e.

there exist smooth, finite-type group schemes over
\spec(\mathcal{O}_{n})

with connected fibres, such that the group of sections gives back the
n
-bounded groups. We will call these group schemes
nBT
-group schemes; see Definition
1.4
. The
nBT
-group schemes we construct are always
quasi-affine
, with a large, and perhaps most significant, class being
affine
; see Theorem
1.3
. This provides natural analogues of the Bruhat–Tits group schemes over
\mathcal{O}_{n}
with good specialization properties.

In
[
BT84a
, Section 3.9.4]
one finds general remarks on how one could possibly generalize
[
BT84a
, Theorem 3.8.1]
to a base of dimension
2
. Somewhat later in the mid-nineties, there was an approach to Bruhat–Tits buildings over higher-dimensional local fields made by A. Parshin
[
Par94
]
with possible motivation from questions in arithmetic. Our approach has its origins in geometry stemming from the study of degenerations of the stack
\text{Bun}_{G}
of
G
-bundles over smooth projective curves; see
[
Bal22
]
. Unlike Parshin, our aim is not towards a generalization of the notion of a Bruhat–Tits building. Rather, we follow the general spirit of the Bruhat–Tits approach,
cf.

[
BT84a
]
, namely, to define subgroups of
G(K_{n})
characterizable by group schemes over
\spec(\mathcal{O}_{n})
.

Recall that a
parabolic vector bundle
in the sense of C. S. Seshadri is a vector bundle with additional data defined on pairs
(X,D)
, where
X
is a
smooth quasi-projective scheme
and
D
a
simple normal crossing divisor
(see the appendix).
Hitherto, these have been
objects
of study primarily occurring as points in certain moduli spaces. In the present paper, the notion of a parabolic bundle helps us get a technical tool to address the issue of extending affine group schemes across subsets of codimension bigger than
1
. These connections first came up in the paper
[
BS15
]
, where under the assumptions of characteristic zero and for group schemes which are generically split (see Definition
1.1
), it was shown,
cf.

[
BS15
, Theorem 5.2.7]
, that
any
parahoric
Bruhat–Tits group scheme over a complete discrete valuation ring
\mathcal{O}
can be realized through “invariant direct images”, i.e. obtained by taking Galois invariants of Weil restriction of scalars of a reductive group scheme, on a ramified cover of
\mathcal{O}

(
see Section
A.2

)
. It is easily checked
(
see Section
9

)
that this can be made to work under some mild tameness assumptions on residue field characteristics for arbitrary complete DVRs
(
see Section
1.1

)
. This result and its underlying philosophy form the cornerstone of some of the basic results of this paper
.

Our first step is to define
n-parahoric
subgroups of
G(K_{n})
. Let
T
be a maximal torus contained in a Borel
B
of
G
over
k
. Let
X(T)\,=\,\Hom(T,\mathbb{G}_{m})
be the group of characters of
T
and
Y(T)\,=\,\Hom(\mathbb{G}_{m},T)
be the group of all one–parameter subgroups of
T
.
Let
\mathfrak{g}
denote the Lie algebra. We denote by
\Phi^{+},\Phi^{-}\subset\Phi
the sets of positive and negative roots with respect to
B
. Let
S=\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{\ell}\}
denote the set of simple roots of
G
, where
\ell
is the rank of
G
. Let
\alpha^{\vee}
denote the coroot corresponding to
\alpha\in S
. We also choose an
épinglage
or a
pinning
of
G
;
i.e.
in addition to
S
, we also choose for every
r\in S
, a
k
-isomorphism
u_{r}
from the additive group
\mathbb{G}_{a}
to the unipotent group
U_{r}
of
G
associated to the root
r
. Let
\{u_{r}\}_{r\in\Phi}
be their canonical extensions satisfying the Chevalley relations.

Let
\mathbb S:=S\cup\{\alpha_{0}\}
denote the set of affine simple roots. Let
\mathcal{A}_{T}
denote the affine apartment corresponding to
T
. It can be identified with the affine space
{\mathbb E}:=Y(T)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}
together with its origin
0
.

For a point
\theta\in\mathcal{A}_{T}
and a root
r\in\Phi
, let
r(\theta):=(r,\theta)
. Set

(1.1)

m_{r}(\theta):=-\lfloor{r(\theta)}\rfloor.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be a complete discrete valuation ring with residue field
k
, with uniformizer
z
and field of fractions
K
. Classically (see
[
BT72
]
), in terms of generators a parahoric subgroup
{\tt{P}}_{\theta}
of
G(K)
is defined as

(1.2)

{\tt{P}}_{\theta}:=\left\langle T(\mathcal{O}),U_{r}\left(z^{m_{r}(\theta)}\mathcal{O}\right),r\in\Phi\right\rangle.

More generally, for an
enclosed
bounded subset
{\Omega}
in the affine apartment
\mathcal{A}_{T}
(
cf.

[
BT72
, Section 2.4.6]
or
[
Cou11
, Section 3]
), set

(1.3)

m_{r}(\Omega):=-\left\lfloor\inf_{\theta\in\Omega}r(\theta)\right\rfloor=\left\lceil\sup_{\theta\in\Omega}-r(\theta)\right\rceil.

Note that there is a notion of a
closure
of a bounded subset, and the function
m_{r}(\Omega)
does not change by taking closures. So unless otherwise mentioned, in this article we always work with enclosed bounded subsets. A bounded subgroup
{\tt{P}}
of
G(K)
associated to a bounded subset
\Omega
of
\mathcal{A}_{T}
is defined as follows:

(1.4)

{\tt{P}}:=\left\langle T(\mathcal{O}),U_{r}\left(z^{m_{r}(\Omega)}\mathcal{O}\right),r\in\Phi\right\rangle.

Following
[
BT72
,
BT84a
]
, we define a larger class of bounded groups as follows.

Definition 1.1
.

A subgroup
{\tt Q}\subset G(K)
will be called
schematic
if there exists a smooth
\mathcal{O}
-group scheme
\mathfrak{G}
with connected fibres and of finite type with
\mathfrak{G}_{K}\simeq G\times K
such that
{\tt Q}=\mathfrak{G}(\mathcal{O})
.

Throughout this paper we work under the assumption that all of the
n
-Bruhat–Tits group schemes we study are connected and the generic fibre
G_{K}
is a product
G\times_{\spec(\mathbb{Z})}\spec(K)
; we call this the “generically split case”
.
As we have mentioned above, parahoric subgroups are all
schematic
. However, Bruhat and Tits study a larger class of schematic subgroups of
G(K)
which are defined by
concave functions
(in fact, they work with the class of quasi-concave functions).

Let
\tilde{\Phi}:=\Phi\cup\{0\}
.
Recall, see
[
BT72
, Section 6.4.3, p. 133]
,
that a function
f\colon\tilde{\Phi}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is said to be
concave
if whenever
r_{i}\in\tilde{\Phi}
are such that
\sumop\displaylimits_{i}r_{i}\in\tilde{\Phi}
, then

(1.5)

f\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{i}r_{i}\right)\leq\sumop\displaylimits_{i}f(r_{i}).

In this paper we generalize the theory for all concave functions over higher-dimensional bases. We hasten to add that Bruhat–Tits theory works for general connected reductive groups, while we make the rather simplifying assumptions on
G
in the spirit of
[
BT84a
, Section 3.2, p. 52]
that it is almost simple, simply connected and
generically split
. Even under these assumptions, the problem seems sufficiently complex.

A natural class of
2
-parahoric group schemes (see Definition
1.4
) came up naturally in the work of the senior author while constructing flat degenerations of the stack of
G
-torsors on smooth projective curves, when we allow the curve to degenerate to stable curves (
cf
.
[
Bal22
]
). A special
2
-parahoric group scheme comes up in the paper
[
PZ13
]
(see Section
9.4
below and also
[
Lou23
]
). We note that
BT
-group schemes on discrete valuations rings arising from general concave functions have appeared in the papers of J.-K. Yu
[
Yu01
,
Yu15
]
and Reeder–Yu
[
RY14
]
. Further, a very special class has found applications in the papers of Moy–Prasad (see
[
MP94
, Section 2.6]
) and Schneider–Stuhler
[
SS97
]
.

Even in the most geometric contexts,
i.e.
without the complications of rationality questions, the larger class of concave functions are needed to express
[
Bal22
]
or the present paper (see Theorem
11.1
and Remark
11.2
). Indeed, the description of the
closed fibres
of the
n
-parahoric group schemes (see Definition
1.4
) over
\mathcal{O}_{n}
, at points of depth bigger than
1
, needs perforce the Bruhat–Tits theory of bounded groups associated to concave functions; see Theorem
1.5
. The interesting feature is that the concave functions which appear in this situation
do not in general arise from bounded subsets of the apartment
but are from the most general class considered by Bruhat and Tits (see Examples
3.10
,
3.12
and
3.11
, Equation (
3.50
) and Remark
11.2
).

In view of
[
BT84a
, Section 3.2, p. 52, second paragraph and Section 3.2.15]
, we believe that Bruhat and Tits may have already envisioned the theory over higher-dimensional bases (
cf.

[
BT84a
, Section 3.9.4]
) as well. We revisit this in Section
5.9

1.1. Assumptions on the residue field
\boldsymbol{k}

In this introduction we assume that
k
is
algebraically closed
. We however note that if we assume only perfectness of
k
together with a few assumptions on the existence of roots of unity (see Section
2.2
), the results of the present paper continue to hold.

We will assume throughout that the characteristic
p
of
k
is coprime to the order of the centre of
G
and the coefficients of the highest root.

•

This assumption suffices when the concave function is of type I, with no further restrictions on the type of the group
G
, except when
G
is of type
A_{n}
, where
p
does not divide
n+1
.

•

For groups of type
A_{n},G_{2},F_{4},E_{6}
, this suffices for concave functions of type II as well.

•

Let
h_{G}
be the Coxeter number of
G
. Let
m(G)
be the dimension of the minimal faithful representation of
G
. When the concave function is strictly of type III, we further need
p
to be greater than
h_{G}
as well as be coprime to
m(G)
. The second condition more precisely means the following:
p
is coprime to
7
for
G_{2}
, coprime to
13
for
F_{4}
, coprime to
3
for
E_{6}
, coprime to
14
for
E_{7}
, coprime to
31
for
E_{8}
and finally coprime to
2n+1
for other classical groups of rank
n
.

•

For the schematization of
n-Moy--Prasad
groups, we refer to Section
8.1
.

1.2. Higher dimensions and statement of main results

We begin with a few key definitions in terms of which the present paper is organized.

Types of Concave functions

Definition 1.2
.

(1)

For
j=1,\ldots,n
, let points
\theta_{j}
in the apartment
\mathcal{A}_{T}
be given. Let
{\bf f}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}=\{f_{\theta_{j}}\}_{j=1}^{n}
be the set of concave functions associated to them by the function
r\mapsto m_{r}(\theta)
(see (
1.1
)). These will be called
n-concave
functions of
type I
.

(2)

For
j=1,\ldots,n
, let
bounded subsets

{}_{j}\subset\mathcal{A}_{T}
be given. Let
{\bf f}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}=\{f_{{}_{j}}\}_{j=1}^{n}
be the set of concave functions associated to them by
r\mapsto m_{r}(\Omega)
(see (
1.3
)). These will be called
n-concave
functions of
type II
.

(3)

Let
{\bf f}:=(\ldots,f_{j},\ldots)\colon\Phi\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an
n-concave
function on
\Phi
defined by concave functions
\{f_{j}\}
(see (
1.5
)). These will be called
n-concave
functions of
type III
.

As mentioned earlier, there are examples of concave functions of
type III
which are not of
type II
(this is stated in
[
BT72
, Section 6.4.4]
; however, see the claim in Example
3.10
below for explicit examples) and similarly of
type II
not of
type I
.

Bounded groups in higher dimensions

We begin by stating our results in the equicharacteristic case. Recall that
\mathcal{O}_{n}:=k\llbracket z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}\rrbracket
, and let
K_{n}:=\Fract(\mathcal{O}_{n})
. For each set of
n
rational points
\boldsymbol{\theta}=(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{n})\in\mathcal{A}^{n}
, in terms of generators we define an
n
-parahoric subgroup of
G(K_{n})
as

(1.6)

{\tt{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}:=\left\langle T\left(\mathcal{O}_{n}\right),U_{r}\left(\prodop\displaylimits_{1\leq i\leq n}z_{i}^{m_{r}(\theta_{i})}\mathcal{O}_{n}\right),r\in\Phi\right\rangle.

More generally, for
\boldsymbol{\Omega}=({}_{1},\ldots,{}_{n})
, where the
{}_{i}\subset\mathbb E
are
enclosed
bounded subsets (
cf.

[
BT72
, Section 2.4.6]
), we can analogously define
n
-bounded subgroups of
G(K_{n})
:

(1.7)

{\tt{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}:=\left\langle T\left(\mathcal{O}_{n}\right),U_{r}\left(\prodop\displaylimits_{1\leq i\leq n}z_{i}^{m_{r}({}_{i})}\mathcal{O}_{n}\right),r\in\Phi\right\rangle.

Even more generally, let
f_{i}\colon\Phi\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be concave functions, see (
1.5
), and let
{\bf f}:=(\ldots,f_{i},\ldots)\colon\Phi\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the
n-concave
function defined by them. To the
n-concave
function
{\bf f}
we can associate an
n
-bounded subgroup of
G(K_{n})
as follows:

(1.8)

{\tt{P}}_{\bf f}:=\left\langle T\left(\mathcal{O}_{n}\right),U_{r}\left(\prodop\displaylimits_{1\leq i\leq n}z_{i}^{f_{i}(r)}\mathcal{O}_{n}\right),r\in\Phi\right\rangle.

Similarly, one may define the
n
-bounded Lie subalgebra
\Lie(\tt{P}_{\bf f})
of
\mathfrak{g}(K_{n})
.

As in Definition
1.1
, we can call a subgroup
{\tt Q}\subset G(K_{n})
a
schematic
subgroup if there exists a connected, smooth
\mathcal{O}_{n}
-group scheme
\mathfrak{G}
of finite type with
\mathfrak{G}_{K_{n}}\simeq G\times_{\spec(\mathbb{Z})}K_{n}
such that
{\tt Q}=\mathfrak{G}(\mathcal{O}_{n})
.

We remark that an important aspect of Bruhat–Tits group schemes over discrete valuation rings is the structure of a
big cell
which extends the big cell of the generic fibre (
cf.

[
BT84a
, Section 3.1.3]
and Theorem
1.3
(
5
) below). This aspect also generalizes to the
n
-Bruhat–Tits group schemes.

We now state our main theorems in the equicharacteristic case. This corresponds to the layout of the paper.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
p
satisfy the hypothesis of Section
1.1

(
or more generally those of Section
2.2

)
.
For an
n
-concave
function
{\bf f}
of each type in Definition
1.2
, there exists a smooth group scheme
\mathfrak{G}_{\bf f}
of finite type on
\mathbb A^{n}_{k}
with connected fibres, whose restriction to
\spec(\mathcal{O}_{n})
comes with an isomorphism
h_{K_{n}}
of the generic fibre
\mathfrak{G}_{K_{n}}
with
G_{K_{n}}
and such that the following hold:

(1)

The group scheme
\mathfrak{G}_{\bf f}
is
affine
when
{\bf f}
is of
type I
, and in general it is
quasi-affine
in
types II and III
.

(2)

The
n
-bounded subgroup
\tt{P}_{\bf f}
is
schematic
. More precisely, we have an identification
\mathfrak{G}_{\bf f}(\mathcal{O}_{n})=\tt{P}_{\bf f}
.

(3)

We have a natural isomorphism of
\Lie(\mathfrak{G}_{\bf f})(\mathcal{O}_{n})
with
\Lie(\tt{P}_{\bf f})
.

(4)

Let us denote the canonical images under
h_{K_{n}}
of the maximal torus
T_{K_{n}}
and the root groups
\{U_{r,K_{n}}\}_{r\in\Phi}
in the generic fibre
\mathfrak{G}_{K_{n}}
by the same notation.

There exist closed subgroup schemes
\{\mathfrak U_{r,\bf f}\subset\mathfrak{G}_{\bf f}\}_{r\in\Phi}

(
resp.
\mathcal{T}_{\bf f}\subset\mathfrak{G}_{\bf f}
)
on
\spec(\mathcal{O}_{n})
, with generic fibre isomorphic to the root groups
\{U_{r,K_{n}}\}_{r\in\Phi}

(
resp.
T_{K_{n}}
)
.

(5)

There exists a big cell
\mathfrak B_{\Phi}
of
\mathfrak{G}_{\bf f}
such that for any choice of total ordering on
\Phi^{+}
(resp.
\Phi^{-}
), the morphism induced by multiplication

(1.9)

\left(\prodop\displaylimits_{r\in\Phi^{+}}\mathfrak U_{r,\bf f}\right)\times\mathcal{T}_{\bf f}\times\left(\prodop\displaylimits_{r\in\Phi^{-}}\mathfrak U_{r,\bf f}\right)\longrightarrow\mathfrak{G}_{\bf f}

is an open immersion with image
\mathfrak B_{\Phi}
. Moreover,
\mathfrak B_{\Phi}
restricts to the big cell
\mathfrak B_{K_{n}}
of the generic fibre
\mathfrak{G}_{K_{n}}
over
\spec(K_{n})
.

(6)

More generally, on a base which is a smooth quasi-projective variety with a divisor with simple normal crossings, given a data of type I concave functions at the height
1
primes associated to the components of the divisor, in Theorem
5.4
, it is shown that there exist a smooth affine group scheme with connected fibres on
X
together with a big cell structure which interpolates this datum on the divisors.

(7)

Let
{\bf f}
be a special
n
-concave function of type III such that each term
f_{i}
is a sum of concave functions of
type I
. Then the associated group scheme
\mathfrak{G}_{\bf f}
is in fact
affine
; see Corollary
5.9
.

The pair
(\mathfrak{G}_{\bf f},h_{K_{n}})
is uniquely determined up to
a unique isomorphism, by properties (
4
) and (
5
).

Definition 1.4
.

The group scheme
\mathfrak{G}_{\bf f}
is called the
nBT
-group scheme associated to an
n
-concave function
{\bf f}
. An
n
-parahoric group scheme is an
nBT
-group scheme associated to an
n
-concave function of
type I
.

Theorem 1.5
.

The group scheme
\mathfrak{G}_{\bf f}
associated to the
n-concave
function
{\bf f}
has the following structure at points of depth bigger than
1
:

(a)

Let
{\tt I}\subset\{1,\ldots,n\}
be a non-empty subset. For a general point
\xi\in\cap_{i\in{\tt I}}H_{i}
, where the
H_{i}
are the coordinate hyperplanes in
\spec(\mathcal{O}_{n})
, the fibre

\mathfrak{G}_{{\bf f},\xi}
is isomorphic to the closed fibre of the Bruhat–Tits group scheme
\mathfrak{G}_{{\tt f}_{\tt I}}
on
\spec(\mathcal{O})
associated to the
1-concave
function
{\tt f}_{\tt I}\colon\Phi\to\mathbb R
given by
r\mapsto\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in{\tt I}}f_{i}
.

(b)

More precisely, let
\mathbb{A}_{\tt I}\subset\mathbb{A}^{n}
be the “subdiagonal” obtained by setting the coordinates
x_{i}=t
for
i\in\tt I
. Let
\spec(A_{\tt I})
be the local ring at the generic point of the divisor
t=0
in
\mathbb{A}_{\tt I}
. Then the restriction of
\mathfrak{G}_{\bf f}
to
\spec(A_{\tt I})
is isomorphic to the Bruhat–Tits group scheme
\mathfrak{G}_{{\tt f}_{\tt I}}
associated to the concave function
{\tt f}_{\tt I}
.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be a complete DVR in mixed characteristics.
Extending the methods for group schemes over
\mathbb A^{n}_{k}
to the case
\mathbb{A}^{n}_{\mathcal{O}}
, in Section
9
we prove the following.

Theorem 1.6
.

For an
(n+1)
-concave
function
{\bf f}=(f_{0},\ldots,f_{n})
, where
f_{0}
is prescribed at the uniformizer of
\mathcal{O}
, the main theorems, Theorems
1.3
and
1.5
, hold over
\mathbf{A}_{\mathcal{O}}={\mathbb A}^{n}_{\mathcal{O}}
under the tameness assumption of Section
1.1

(
in fact under the assumptions in Section
2.2

)
.

1.3. An outline of the strategy of proof

We now outline briefly the strategy of the proof in this paper. The general strategy is an
induction on the order of complexity
of concave functions on root systems. The simplest one is a function
f_{\theta}
, see (
1.1
), which is a concave function of type I; the next in the order is the concave function
f
(see (
1.3
)),
associated to a bounded subset
\Omega
in the apartment,
i.e.
of type II. The third in the order of complexity is the general concave function
f
of type III which need not arise as an
f
.

In other words, we build the family of
nBT
-group schemes
\{\mathfrak{G}_{\bf f}\}
on
\spec(\mathcal{O}_{n})
associated to an
n-concave
function
{\bf f}\colon\Phi\to{\mathbb R}^{n}
in three tiers. This is carried out by a bootstrapping process. The first step, in Section
5
, is to build the
n
-parahoric group scheme (see Definition
1.4
) associated to a collection of
n
points in the affine apartment,
i.e.
an
n-concave
function
{\bf f}=\{f_{\theta_{j}}\}
of
type I
. This case falls into a general method of proof where the ideas arising from the notion of parabolic vector bundles play a key role. It has its origins in the paper
[
BS15
]
, and the end result is somewhat surprising in that, unlike what Bruhat–Tits expect, namely only a
quasi-affine
group scheme (see
[
BT84a
, Section 3.9.4]
and Section
5.9
below), one in fact gets an
affine

n
-parahoric group scheme on
\spec(\mathcal{O}_{n})
along with its cell structure. The pair of the
n
-parahoric group scheme and its cell structure become the base for the subsequent constructions. We then naturally move on to the case when instead of points in the apartment, we are given a collection of
n
bounded subsets in the affine apartment and so work with an
n
-tuple of
concave
functions arising from these bounded subsets. The construction (in Section
6
) of the corresponding
nBT
-group scheme on
\spec(\mathcal{O}_{n})
builds on the construction of an
n
-parahoric group scheme. We assume in Section
6
that
G
is one of
A_{n},G_{2},F_{4}
or
E_{6}
, where results from
[
BT84b
]
and
[
GY03
,
GY05
]
play the important role of reducing schematic constructions over DVRs for enclosed bounded subsets to their extremal vertices. The proof here is somewhat involved, especially in extending the cell structure and the schematic group law across subsets of codimension
2
.
The end result is that, much as expected, we get a
quasi-affine

nBT
-group scheme associated to the
n-concave
functions of
type II
. Finally, in Section
7
we turn our attention to the case of
G
with no constraints and also a general
n-concave
function. This case is then reduced to the previous case,
i.e.
of
n-concave
functions of
type II
. This reduction follows from the
not so obvious
observation, see Proposition
3.21
, that an
optimal concave function
on groups of type
A_{n}
always arises as one coming from a bounded subset of the apartment. This remark together with the power of the main results of
[
BT84a
]
help us to realize the
nBT
-group scheme associated to an
n
-tuple of concave functions; these group schemes are
a fortiori
quasi-affine as expected. A surprise, and a possible opening, is offered by a special class of concave functions of type III, which occur as sums of concave functions of type I. Here, our methods yield the
affineness
of the group scheme; see Corollary
5.9
. This leads us to a natural question, namely “Characterise
n
-tuples of optimal concave functions for which the associated
nBT
-group schemes are
affine
.”

Recall that in the equicharacteristic case when the residue field characteristic satisfies mild tameness assumptions, by
[
BS15
, Theorem 5.2.7]
, all parahoric subgroup schemes are recovered by the invariant direct image functor (
i.e.
obtained by taking Galois invariants of Weil restriction of scalars). Following
[
BS15
]
closely, in Section
8
we show that Moy–Prasad group schemes over DVRs which generalize parahorics can be obtained by the invariant direct image together with the simplest types of dilatations. Over higher dimensions, this generalizes suitably using dilations results of
[
MRR20
]
. We use results of Bruhat–Tits
[
BT84a
]
to show in Section
9
that the generalization of
[
BS15
, Theorem 5.2.7]
to mixed characteristics reduces to a verification on the big cell which was done in Section
8
, following
[
BS15
]
closely.

1.4. Other related results and proof strategies

In
[
Lan96
]
Bruhat–Tits group schemes are constructed by using extension of
birational group laws
and the theorem of Artin–Weil; see
[
SGA3
, Exposé XVIII]
. The possibility of such an approach is briefly mentioned in
[
BT84a
, Section 3.1.7]
. In the very recent paper
[
Lou23
]
, smooth and separated group schemes over arbitrary Noetherian bases has been constructed; see
[
Lou23
, Theorem 3.2.5]
. This last result assumes that the
schematic root datum
extends to the base ring
A
. However, in general proving the key properties such as
quasi-affineness
of the group schemes does not seem immediate by these approaches. In the present paper, for
k\llbracket z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}\rrbracket
or
\mathcal{O}\llbracket z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}\rrbracket
, where
\mathcal{O}
is a discrete valuation ring, in the course of our proofs for each of the three types of concave functions, we actually show that the schematic root datum extends.

In the context of the present paper on higher-dimensional bases, the first construction goes back to
[
BT84a
, Section 3.9.4]
, where for
A=k[x,y]
or
A=A_{1}[[z]]
for
A_{1}
a Dedekind ring, a
2BT
-group scheme is constructed by generalising
[
BT84a
, Theorem 3.8.1]
.

In the special setting of a
2
-concave function
{\bf f}=(0,f)
over
A=A_{1}[[z]]
, for
A_{1}
a Dedekind ring, we refer to
[
PZ13
]
and Section
9.4
below. The very recent paper
[
Lou23
]
also gives a completely different proof of the key
affineness result
of these group schemes over
A=A_{1}[[z]]
; further, it goes on to construct and study affine Grassmannians for these group schemes.

1.5. Other directions

In a slightly different direction, recall that Moy–Prasad groups (see
[
MP94
, Section 2.6]
) can be realized as schematic subgroups arising from
BT
-group schemes arising from a small variant of the concave functions in
[
BT72
]
. As a natural extension of our approach, in Section
8
we define
n
-Moy–Prasad groups and the corresponding schematization. Their schematization over discrete valuation rings was derived as a consequence of a general approach by Yu in
[
Yu15
]
. To adapt these groups to our theory, we need to realize the Moy–Prasad groups as
invariant direct images
, see Notation
5.2
, of certain group schemes from ramified covers (see
[
BS15
]
); see Section
8
. This involves generalising the notion of a
unit group
to one corresponding to the variant of concave function mentioned above. As a consequence, we show that
n
-Moy–Prasad group schemes are affine just like the group scheme

\mathfrak{G}_{\bf f}
when
{\bf f}
is of
type I
.

As further applications of this approach, we construct higher
BT

group schemes
on certain basic spaces. More precisely, we construct and describe

•

an
\ell
BT
-group scheme on the De Concini–Procesi wonderful compactification
{\bf X}
(see
[
DCP83
]
) of the adjoint group
G_{\mathrm{ad}}
, where
\ell=\rank(G)
;

•

an
(\ell+1)
BT
-group scheme on the loop “wonderful embedding”
{\bf X}^{\mathrm{aff}}
of the adjoint affine Kac-Moody group
G^{\mathrm{aff}}_{\mathrm{ad}}
, constructed by P. Solis; see
[
Sol17
]
– the novelty is that the base here is an
ind-scheme
;

•

a family of
2BT
-group schemes on the minimal resolution of singularities of normal surface singularities in the context of
[
Bal22
]
.

Part 1.  The bounded groups

2. Towards the
n
-parahoric Lie algebra bundle

The first two subsections are in vigour throughout this article. The remaining ones recall a “loop” approach to parahoric groups for application in Sections
4
and
10
.

2.1. Lie data of
\boldsymbol{G/k}

In this subsection we assume the group-theoretical notions of
G
already introduced in the introduction and give only those that we need further in this article. Let
\mathbf{a}_{0}
be the unique Weyl alcove of
G
whose closure contains
0
and which is contained in the dominant Weyl chamber corresponding to
B
. Under the natural pairing between
Y(T)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
and
X(T)\otimes{Z}\mathbb{Q}
, the integral basis elements dual to
S
are called the fundamental co-weights
\{\omega^{\vee}_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in S\}
. Let
c_{\alpha}
be the coefficient of
\alpha
in the highest root. The vertices of the Weyl alcove
\mathbf{a}_{0}
are exactly
0
and

(2.1)

\displaystyle\theta_{\alpha}:={{\omega^{\vee}_{\alpha}}\over{c_{\alpha}}},\quad\alpha\in S.

We will call the lattice generated by the
\theta_{\alpha}
the Tits lattice.

Let
\SL(m(G))
be the minimal faithful representation of
G
. We fix a maximal split torus
T_{\SL}
of
\SL(m(G))
. Let
\mathcal{A}_{T_{\SL}}
denote its apartment. We fix an alcove
\mathbf{a}
in
\mathcal{A}_{T_{\SL}}
.

Definition 2.1
.

For any
\theta\in Y(T)\otimes\mathbb Q
, let
d_{\theta}
be the least positive integer such that
d_{\theta}\cdot\theta
lies in
Y(T)
. For
\theta\in Y(T_{\SL})\otimes\mathbb{Q}
, let
d_{\theta}
be defined similarly.

Thus, if
e_{\alpha}
is the order of
\omega^{\vee}_{\alpha}
in the quotient of the co-weight lattice by
Y(T)
, it follows that for
\theta_{\alpha}
, the number
d_{\alpha}:=d_{\theta_{\alpha}}
is

(2.2)

d_{\alpha}=e_{\alpha}\cdot c_{\alpha}.

An affine simple root
\alpha\in\mathbb S
may be viewed as an affine functional on
\mathcal{A}_{T}
. Any non-empty subset
\mathbb I\subset\mathbb S
defines the facet
\Sigma I\subset\overline{\mathbf{a}_{0}}
where exactly the
\alpha
not lying in the subset
\mathbb I
vanish. So the set
\mathbb S
corresponds to the interior of the alcove, and the vertex
\theta_{\alpha}
of the alcove corresponds to
\alpha\in\mathbb S
. Conversely, any facet
\Sigma\subset\overline{\mathbf{a}_{0}}
defines a non-empty subset
\mathbb{I}_{\Sigma}\subset\mathbb S
. For
\emptyset\neq\mathbb I\subset\mathbb S
, the barycenter of
\Sigma I
is given by

(2.3)

\theta I:=\frac{1}{|\mathbb I|}\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha\in\mathbb I}\theta_{\alpha}.

2.2. Assumptions on the residue field
\boldsymbol{k}
and points in the apartments compatible with
\boldsymbol{k}

The following assumptions will be in vigour throughout the article. We assume
k
is perfect to be able to apply
[
BT84a
]
. Let
p:=\charr(k)
.

Characteristic Assumptions.
These are needed for the existence of a Kawamata cover (see Section
A.1
). We will assume that
p
is coprime to
d_{\alpha}
; see Equation (
2.2
). Notice that since
G
is simply connected, this assumption is implied by Section
1.1
. Indeed, since
Y(T)
equals the coroot lattice,
Z_{G}
identifies with the quotient of the co-weight lattice by
Y(T)
. Further, whenever we work with a finite set of rational points
\{\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{n}\}
of
\mathcal{A}_{T}
or
\mathcal{A}_{T_{\SL}}
, we assume that for
1\leq i\leq n
,
p
is coprime to the integers
d_{\theta_{i}}
; see Definition
2.1
. Let
h_{G}
denote the Coxeter number of
G
. Let
m(G)
denote the dimension of the minimal faithful representation of
G
. When the concave function is strictly of type III, we further need
p>h_{G}
as well as
p
to be coprime to
m(G)
.

Assumptions on the existence of primitive roots of unity.
These are needed to make Section
A.2
work. The residue field
k
is such that for every facet
\Sigma
of
\mathbf{a}_{0}
(see Section
2.1
), there exists a rational point
\theta\in\Sigma
such that
d_{\theta}
(see Definition
2.1
) is coprime to
\charr(k)
and
k
contains the primitive
d_{\theta}^{\mathrm{th}}
roots of unity.

•

For a general
G
, this suffices when the concave function is of type I.

•

For
A_{n},G_{2},F_{4},E_{6}
, this suffices for type II as well.

•

When the concave function is strictly of type III, in addition to the condition for
\mathbf{a}_{0}
, we need to assume the analogous condition for
\mathbf{a}
(see Section
2.1
).

Whenever we work with a finite set of rational points
\{\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{n}\}
of
\mathcal{A}_{T}
or
\mathcal{A}_{T_{\SL}}
, we assume that for
1\leq i\leq n
,
k
contains the primitive
d_{\theta_{i}}^{\mathrm{th}}
roots of unity.

We observe that any facet
\Sigma\subset\mathcal{A}_{T}
contains a rational one-parameter subgroup
\theta
of
T
which is a convex combination of the vertices of
\Sigma
in a way that for
d\in\mathbb{N}
coprime to
p
, the multiple
d\theta
becomes a one-parameter subgroup of
T
. We see this as follows. Since the alcove
\mathbf{a}_{0}
is a fundamental domain for the action of the affine Weyl group, we may suppose that
\Sigma
is a facet
\Sigma_{\mathbb{I}}
of
\mathbf{a}_{0}
. Say
\mathbb{I}=\{\theta_{0},\ldots,\theta_{j}\}
are the vertices of
\Sigma_{\mathbb{I}}
and
\theta_{0}=\theta_{\alpha}
. Then since the
d_{\alpha}
defined in (
2.2
) are always strictly greater than
1
, it follows that we may take

(2.4)

\theta=\frac{\theta_{0}+\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{j}\left(d_{\alpha}^{i}-d_{\alpha}^{i-1}\right)\theta_{i}}{d_{\alpha}^{j}}.

We remark that if
L
denotes the LCM of the coefficients of the highest root and
\ell
is the rank of
T
, then for type I, it suffices that
k
has
\big(|Z_{G}|L\big)^{\ell}
primitive roots of unity. For type III,
k
must further have primitive
({m(G)^{m(G)}})^{\mathrm{th}}
roots of unity.

2.3. Loop groups and their parahoric subgroups

We have included this section, where we introduce the language of loop groups to describe parahoric groups. We however add that this terminology is restricted to Sections
4
and
10
of this paper.

The loop group
\LG
is the group functor which associates to a
k
-algebra
R
the group
G(R(\!(z)\!))
; this is representable by an ind-scheme over
k
. Similarly, the loop Lie algebra functor
L\mathfrak{g}
is given by
L\mathfrak{g}(R)=\mathfrak{g}(R(\!(z)\!))
. We can similarly define the positive loops (also called
jet groups
in the literature)

L^{+}(G)
to be the subfunctor of
\LG
defined by
L^{+}(G)(R):=G(R\llbracket z\rrbracket)
. The positive-loop construction extends more generally to any group scheme
\mathcal{G}\rightarrow\spec(A)
and any vector bundle
\mathfrak{P}\rightarrow\spec(A)
whose sheaf of sections carries a Lie bracket.

Let
L^{\ltimes}G=\mathbb{G}_{m}\ltimes\LG
, where the
rotational torus

\mathbb{G}_{m}
acts on
\LG
by acting on the uniformizer via the
loop rotation action
as follows:
u\in\mathbb{G}_{m}(R)
acts on
\gamma(z)\in\LG(R)=G(R(\!(t)\!))
by
u\gamma(z)u^{-1}=\gamma(uz)
. A maximal torus of
L^{\ltimes}G
is
T^{\ltimes}=\mathbb{G}_{m}\times T
. An
\eta\in\Hom(\mathbb{G}_{m},T^{\ltimes})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
over
R(s)
can be viewed as a rational one-parameter subgroup (
1
-PS),
i.e.
a
1
-PS
\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow T^{\ltimes}
over
R(w)
, where
w^{n}=s
for some
n\geq 1
. In this case, for
\gamma(z)\in L^{\ltimes}G(R)
, we will view
\eta(s)\gamma(z)\eta(s)^{-1}
as an element in
L^{\ltimes}G(R(w))
. Hence, by the condition

(2.5)

\lim_{s\rightarrow 0}\eta(s)\gamma(z)\eta(s)^{-1}\text{ exists in }L^{\ltimes}G(R)

on
\gamma(z)\in L^{\ltimes}G(R)
, we mean that there exists an
n\geq 1
such that for
w^{n}=s
, we have

(2.6)

\eta(s)\gamma(z)\eta(s)^{-1}\in L^{\ltimes}G(R\llbracket w\rrbracket).

This is the underlying principle in
[
BS15
]
in a more global setting.
Let
\eta=(a,\theta)
for
a
a strictly positive rational number and
\theta
a rational
1
-PS of
T
. Then, we have

(2.7)

\eta(s)\gamma(z)\eta(s)^{-1}=\theta(s)\gamma\left(s^{a}z\right)\theta(s)^{-1}.

We note further that for any
0<d\in\mathbb{N}
, setting
\eta=(\frac{a}{d},\frac{\theta}{d})
we have

(2.8)

\eta(s)\gamma(z)\eta(s)^{-1}=\theta\left(s^{\frac{1}{d}}\right)\gamma\left(s^{\frac{a}{d}}z\right)\theta\left(s^{\frac{1}{d}}\right)^{-1}.

So for
\eta=\frac{1}{d}(1,\theta)
, observe that the statement “
\lim_{s\rightarrow 0}\eta(s)\gamma(z)\eta(s)^{-1}
exists” is a condition which is equivalent to

(2.9)

\theta(s)\gamma(s)\theta(s)^{-1}\in L^{\ltimes}G(R\llbracket w\rrbracket).

In other words, the condition of the existence of limits is independent of
d
, and we may further set
z=s
in
\gamma(z)
. More generally, if
a>0
, the conditions for
(a,\theta)
and
(1,\frac{\theta}{a})
are equivalent. We may write this condition on
\gamma(z)\in L^{\ltimes}G(R)
or
\LG(R)
as

(2.10)

\text{``}\lim_{s\rightarrow 0}\theta(s)\gamma(s)\theta(s)^{-1}\text{ exists in }L^{\ltimes}G(R)\text{ or }\LG(R)\text{ if }\gamma(z)\in\LG(R)\text{''}.

For
r\in\Phi
, let
u_{r}\colon\mathbb{G}_{a}\rightarrow G
denote the root subgroup. If for some
b\in\mathbb{Z}
and
t(z)\in R\llbracket z\rrbracket
, we take
\gamma(z):=u_{r}(z^{b}t(z))\in Lu_{r}(R)
and
\eta:=(1,\theta)
, then

(2.11)

\eta(s)u_{r}(z^{b}t(z))\eta(s)^{-1}=\theta(s)u_{r}((sz)^{b}t(sz))\theta(s)^{-1}=u_{r}(s^{r(\theta)}(sz)^{b}t(sz)).

So for
\eta=(1,\theta)
, the condition that the limit exists is equivalent to

(2.12)

r(\theta)+b\geq 0\iff\lfloor r(\theta)+b\rfloor=\lfloor r(\theta)\rfloor+b\geq 0\iff b\geq-\lfloor r(\theta)\rfloor.

We note the independence of the above implications of the number
d
occurring in the equation
\eta:=\frac{1}{d}(1,\theta)
.

Let
\pi_{1}\colon T^{\ltimes}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{m}
be the first projection. For any rational
1
-PS
\eta\colon\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow T^{\ltimes}
, we say
\eta
is positive if
\pi_{1}\circ\eta>0
, negative if
\pi_{1}\circ\eta<0
and non-zero if
\pi_{1}\circ\eta
is either positive or negative. In this paper, we will only need to work with
\eta
which are positive.

Any non-zero
\eta=(a,\theta)
defines the following positive-loop functors from the category of
k
-algebras to the category of groups and Lie algebras:

(2.13)

\displaystyle{\tt{P}}_{\eta}^{\ltimes}(R):=\left\{\gamma\in L^{\ltimes}G(R)\;\Big|\;\lim_{s\rightarrow 0}\eta(s)\gamma(z)\eta(s)^{-1}\text{ exists in }L^{\ltimes}G(R)\right\},

(2.14)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{P}}_{\eta}^{\ltimes}(R):=\left\{h\in L^{\ltimes}\mathfrak{g}(R)\;\Big|\;\lim_{s\rightarrow 0}{Ad}(\eta(s))(h(z))\text{ exists in }L^{\ltimes}\mathfrak{g}(R)\right\},

(2.15)

\displaystyle{\tt{P}}_{\eta}(R):=\left\{\gamma\in\LG(R)\;\Big|\;\lim_{s\rightarrow 0}\eta(s)\gamma(z)\eta(s)^{-1}\text{ exists in }\LG(R)\right\},

(2.16)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{P}}_{\eta}(R)=\left\{h\in L\mathfrak{g}(R)\;\Big|\;\lim_{s\rightarrow 0}Ad(\eta(s))(h(z))\text{ exists in }L\mathfrak{g}(R)\right\},

(2.17)

\displaystyle\text{Thus,}\penalty\ \penalty\ \tt{P}_{\eta}:=\tt{P}_{\eta}^{\ltimes}\cap(1\times\LG)\hskip 10.4999pt\text{and}\hskip 10.4999pt{\mathfrak{P}}_{\eta}:={\mathfrak{P}}_{\eta}^{\ltimes}\cap(0\oplus L\mathfrak{g}).

A
parahoric
subgroup of
L^{\ltimes}G
(resp.
\LG
) is a subgroup that is conjugate to
\tt{P}^{\ltimes}_{\eta}
(resp.
\tt{P}_{\eta}
) for some
\eta
. In this paper we will mostly be using only the case when
\eta=\frac{1}{d}(1,\theta)
. In this case, letting
L\mathfrak{g}(R)=\mathfrak{g}(R(\!(s)\!))
as in (
2.10
), we may reformulate (
2.17
) as

(2.18)

{\mathfrak{P}}_{\eta}(R)=\left\{h\in L\mathfrak{g}(R)\;\Big|\;\lim_{s\rightarrow 0}Ad(\theta(s))(h(s))\text{ exists in }L\mathfrak{g}(R)\right\}.

Let
\mathfrak{t}
denote the Cartan subalgebra associated to
T
, and let
\mathfrak g_{r}\subset\mathfrak g
be the root subspace associated to
U_{r}
. Then in terms of generators, the Lie algebra functor associated to the group functor
\tt{P}_{\theta}
(see (
1.2
)) is given by

(2.19)

\Lie({\tt{P}}_{\theta})(R)=\left\langle\mathfrak{t}(R\llbracket z\rrbracket),\mathfrak{g}_{r}\left(z^{m_{r}(\theta)}R\llbracket z\rrbracket\right),r\in\Phi\right\rangle.

By the conditions (
2.13
) and (
2.17
), using (
2.12
), we may express
\tt{P}_{\eta}
in terms of generators as in (
1.2
) and its Lie algebra functor
\Lie(\tt{P}_{\eta})
as in (
2.19
)
.
Thus, for any
\eta
of the type
(1,\theta)
, we get the equality of Lie algebra functors

(2.20)

\Lie(\tt{P}_{\eta})={\mathfrak{P}}_{\eta}.

This can be seen by using (
2.14
) and (
2.17
) and then replacing the conjugation in (
2.7
) by
\Ad(\eta(s))
.

For a rational
1
-PS
\theta
of
T
with
\eta=(1,\theta)
, we will have the notation

(2.21)

{\mathfrak{P}}_{\theta}:={\mathfrak{P}}_{\eta}.

2.4. Pro-groups and pro-Lie algebras

Recall that to each facet
\Sigma I\subset\overline{\mathbf{a}_{0}}
, Bruhat–Tits theory associates a parahoric group scheme
\mathfrak{G}_{\mathbb I}
on
\spec({\mathcal{O}})
, which is smooth and affine with connected fibres and whose generic fibre is
G\times_{\spec(k)}\spec(K)
. Let
L^{+}(\mathfrak{G}_{\mathbb I})
be the group functor

(2.22)

L^{+}(\mathfrak{G}_{\mathbb I})(R):=\mathfrak{G}_{\mathbb I}(R\llbracket z\rrbracket).

This is represented by a pro-algebraic group over
k
which is a subgroup
of the loop group
\LG
. For
\eta=(1,\theta)
, where
\theta
is any rational
1
-PS lying in
\Sigma I
, we therefore have the following identifications:

(2.23)

L^{+}(\mathfrak{G}_{\mathbb I})(k)=\tt{P}_{\eta}\hskip 10.4999pt\text{and}\hskip 10.4999pt\Lie(L^{+}(\mathfrak{G}_{\mathbb I})(k))={\mathfrak{P}}_{\eta}.

2.5.
n
-parahoric loop groups in higher dimensions

In this subsection we give a loop-theoretic reformulation of the definitions in Section
2.3
to the higher-dimensional base. As always, let
\mathcal{O}_{n}:=k\llbracket z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}\rrbracket
and let
R
denote an arbitrary
k
-algebra. We denote the field of Laurent polynomials by
K_{n}=k(\!(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\!)=k\llbracket z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}\rrbracket[\ldots,z_{i}^{-1},\ldots]
. The
n-loop
group
L_{n}G
on a
k
-algebra
R
is given by
L_{n}G(R):=G\big(R(\!(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\!)\big)
. Similarly, the
n-loop Lie algebra

L_{n}\mathfrak{g}
is given by
L_{n}\mathfrak{g}(R):=\mathfrak{g}\big(R(\!(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\!)\big)
.

Let
L^{\ltimes}_{n}G:=\mathbb{G}_{m}^{n}\ltimes L_{n}G
, where the
rotational torus

\mathbb{G}_{m}^{n}
acts on
L_{n}G
by acting on the uniformizer via the
loop rotation action
. This goes as follows: an element
\mathbf{u}:=(\ldots,u_{i},\ldots)\in\mathbb{G}_{m}^{n}(R)
acts on
\gamma(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\in L_{n}G(R)=G(R(\!(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\!))
by

(2.24)

\mathbf{u}\gamma(z)\mathbf{u}^{-1}:=\gamma(\ldots,u_{i}z_{i},\ldots).

A maximal torus of
L_{n}^{\ltimes}G
is
T^{\ltimes}=\mathbb{G}_{m}^{n}\times T
. An
\mathbf{\eta}\in\Hom(\mathbb{G}_{m},T^{\ltimes})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
over
R(s)
can be viewed as a rational
1
-PS. For example, if
\eta_{i}=\big((0,\ldots,a_{i},\ldots,0),\theta_{i}\big)
for
(0,\ldots,a_{i},\ldots,0)\in\mathbb{Q}^{n}
and
\theta_{i}
a rational
1
-PS of
T
, then as before, we have

(2.25)

\eta_{i}(s)\gamma(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\eta_{i}(s)^{-1}=\theta_{i}(s)\gamma(z_{1},\ldots,s^{a_{i}}z_{i},\ldots,z_{n})\theta_{i}(s)^{-1}.

For
\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{n}
as before, we can make sense of the condition on
\gamma(z)\in L_{n}^{\ltimes}G(R)

“
\lim_{s_{i}\rightarrow 0}\prodop\displaylimits\eta_{i}(s_{i})\gamma(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\prodop\displaylimits\eta_{i}(s_{i})^{-1}
exists in
L_{n}^{\ltimes}G(R)
”.

Similarly, if
{\boldsymbol{\theta}}=(\theta_{1},\theta_{i},\ldots,\theta_{n})\in(Y(T)\otimes\mathbb{Q})^{n}
denotes an
n
-tuple of rational
1
-PSs of
T
and if
m_{r}(\theta)=-\lfloor r(\theta)\rfloor
as in (
1.1
), then in terms of generators, we have an
n-parahoric
group
{\tt{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(R)
associated to
\boldsymbol{\theta}
as in (
1.6
).

Defining
\eta_{i}:=\big((0,\ldots,1,\ldots,0),\theta_{i}\big)
as before, we can describe the
n
-parahoric groups as follows:

(2.26)

\displaystyle{\tt{P}}_{\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{n}}(R):=\left\{\gamma(z)\in L_{n}^{\ltimes}G(R)\;\Big|\;\lim_{s_{i}\rightarrow 0}\prodop\displaylimits\eta_{i}(s_{i}).\gamma(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}).\prodop\displaylimits\eta_{i}(s_{i})^{-1}\text{ exists}\right\}

(2.27)

\displaystyle\text{and in fact}\penalty\ \penalty\ {\tt{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(R)={\tt{P}}_{\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{n}}(R)\cap(1\times L_{n}G)(R).

Thus facets correspond to
n
-tuple of usual facets.
Further, let
\lambda\in\mathcal{A}_{T}
. Then

(2.28)

\lambda(z_{i}){\tt{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\lambda(z_{i})^{-1}={\tt{P}}_{(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{i}+\lambda,\ldots,\theta_{n})}.

So conjugacy classes of parahorics correspond to
n
-tuples of non-empty subsets of
\mathbb{S}
.

We can similarly describe the
n
-parahoric Lie algebras. In particular,

(2.29)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{P}}^{\ltimes}_{\eta_{i}}(R)=\left\{h\in L_{n}\mathfrak{g}(R)\;\Big|\;\lim_{s_{i}\rightarrow 0}\Ad\left(\prodop\displaylimits\eta_{i}(s_{i})\right)(h(z))\text{ exists in }L_{n}^{\ltimes}\mathfrak{g}(R)\right\},

(2.30)

\displaystyle\text{Thus,}\penalty\ \penalty\ {\mathfrak{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(R):={\mathfrak{P}}_{\eta}^{\ltimes}(R)\cap(0\oplus L_{n}\mathfrak{g})(R).

2.6. Standard parahoric subgroups

The
standard parahoric subgroups
of
G(K)
are parahoric subgroups of the distinguished hyperspecial parahoric subgroup
G(\mathcal{O})
. These are realized as inverse images under the evaluation map
\ev\colon G(\mathcal{O})\to G(k)
of standard parabolic subgroups of
G
. In particular, the standard
Iwahori subgroup

{\mathfrak I}
is the group
{\mathfrak I}=\ev^{-1}(B)
.

3. Examples and Remarks

The first example is illustrative of the fact that the operations of imposing regularity conditions and setting variables to be equal do not commute.

Example 3.1
.

Let
G=\SL_{2}
,
\mathcal{O}=\mathcal{O}_{2}
and
K:=K_{2}
. Let
{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}=(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})\in\mathbb{Q}^{2}
.
Then

(3.1)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}s_{1}^{\theta_{1}}s_{2}^{\theta_{2}}&0\\
0&s_{1}^{-\theta_{1}}s_{2}^{-\theta_{2}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}X_{11}(s_{1}z_{1},s_{2}z_{2})&X_{12}(s_{1}z_{1},s_{2}z_{2})\\
X_{21}(s_{1}z_{1},s_{2}z_{2})&X_{22}(s_{1}z_{1},s_{2}z_{2})\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}s_{2}^{-\theta_{2}}s_{1}^{-\theta_{1}}&0\\
0&s_{2}^{\theta_{2}}s_{1}^{\theta_{1}}\end{pmatrix}

(3.2)

\displaystyle\hphantom{\Bigg(}=\begin{pmatrix}X_{11}(s_{1}z_{1},s_{2}z_{2})&X_{12}(s_{1}z_{1},s_{2}z_{2})\penalty\ s_{1}^{2\theta_{1}}s_{2}^{2\theta_{2}}\\
X_{21}(s_{1}z_{1},s_{2}z_{2})\penalty\ s_{1}^{-2\theta_{1}}s_{2}^{-2\theta_{2}}&X_{22}(s_{1}z_{1},s_{2}z_{2})\end{pmatrix}\subset SL_{2}(K(s_{1},s_{2})).

If we put the regularity condition “
\lim_{s_{i}\rightarrow 0}\text{exists}
”, we get

(3.3)

{\tt{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{O}&\mathcal{O}z_{1}^{-\lfloor 2\theta_{1}\rfloor}z_{2}^{-\lfloor 2\theta_{2}\rfloor}\\
\mathcal{O}z_{1}^{-\lfloor-2\theta_{1}\rfloor}z_{2}^{-\lfloor-2\theta_{2}\rfloor}&\mathcal{O}\end{pmatrix}.

Now setting
t:=z_{1}=z_{2}
,
B:=k\llbracket t\rrbracket
and
{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\in(0,\frac{1}{2})^{2}
, we get

{\tt{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}^{\diag}=\begin{pmatrix}B&B\\
t^{2}B&B\end{pmatrix}.

On the other hand, setting
t:=z_{1}=z_{2}
and
s:=s_{1}=s_{2}
in (
3.2
), we get

(3.4)

\begin{pmatrix}X_{11}(st)&X_{12}(st)\penalty\ s^{2\theta_{1}+2\theta_{2}}\\
X_{21}(st)\penalty\ s^{-2\theta_{1}-2\theta_{2}}&X_{22}(st)\end{pmatrix}.

Now putting the regularity condition “
\,\lim_{s\rightarrow 0}\text{exists}
”, we get
\begin{pmatrix}B&Bt^{-\lfloor 2\theta_{1}+2\theta_{2}\rfloor}\\
Bt^{-\lfloor-2\theta_{1}-2\theta_{2}\rfloor}&B\end{pmatrix}
. Notice for
{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\in(0,\frac{1}{2})^{2}
that when
0<\theta_{1}+\theta_{2}<\frac{1}{2}
, we get

\begin{pmatrix}B&B\\
tB&B\end{pmatrix}
, while when
\frac{1}{2}\leq\theta_{1}+\theta_{2}<1
, we get

\begin{pmatrix}B&t^{-1}B\\
t^{2}B&B\end{pmatrix}
.
In either case, for
{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\in(0,\frac{1}{2})^{2}
, we see that the operations of imposing regularity conditions and setting variables to be equal do not commute.

Example 3.2
.

This example shows that the multiplication law in higher-dimensional parahorics is more involved than in the case of discrete valuation rings.

Let
G:=\SL_{3}
,
\mathcal{O}:=\mathcal{O}_{2}
and
K:=K_{2}
. In this example we study
{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}:=(\frac{\omega^{\vee}_{\alpha_{1}}}{3},\frac{\omega^{\vee}_{\alpha_{2}}}{3})
. The co-weights are given by the points
(\frac{2}{3},\frac{-1}{3},\frac{-1}{3})
,
(\frac{1}{3},\frac{1}{3},\frac{-2}{3})\in\mathcal{A}_{T}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
;
i.e.

\omega^{\vee}_{\alpha_{1}}(t)=\diag(t^{\frac{2}{3}},t^{\frac{-1}{3}},t^{\frac{-1}{3}})
and
\omega^{\vee}_{\alpha_{2}}(t)=\diag(t^{\frac{1}{3}},t^{\frac{1}{3}},t^{\frac{-2}{3}})
. We have

(3.5)

{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}(s_{1},s_{2})=\diag\left(s_{1}^{\frac{2}{9}}s_{2}^{\frac{1}{9}},s_{1}^{\frac{-1}{9}}s_{2}^{\frac{1}{9}},s_{1}^{\frac{-1}{9}}s_{2}^{\frac{2}{9}}\right).

Conjugating the
3\times 3
matrix
\begin{pmatrix}E_{ij}(s_{1}z_{1},s_{2}z_{2})\end{pmatrix}
by
{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}(s_{1},s_{2})
, we get

(3.6)

\begin{pmatrix}E_{11}(s_{1}z_{1},s_{2}z_{2})&E_{12}(s_{1}z_{1},s_{2}z_{2})s_{1}^{\frac{1}{3}}&E_{13}(s_{1}z_{1},s_{2}z_{2})s_{1}^{\frac{1}{3}}s_{2}^{\frac{1}{3}}\\
E_{21}(s_{1}z_{1},s_{2}z_{2})s_{1}^{\frac{-1}{3}}&E_{22}(s_{1}z_{1},s_{2}z_{2})&E_{23}(s_{1}z_{1},s_{2}z_{2})s_{2}^{\frac{1}{3}}\\
E_{31}(s_{1}z_{1},s_{2}z_{2})s_{1}^{\frac{-1}{3}}s_{2}^{\frac{-1}{3}}&E_{32}(s_{1}z_{1},s_{2}z_{2})s_{2}^{\frac{-1}{3}}&E_{33}(s_{1}z_{1},s_{2}z_{2})\end{pmatrix}.

Imposing regularity conditions forces
E_{12}
,
E_{13}
and
E_{23}
to be regular and
E_{21}(z_{1},z_{2})
(resp.
E_{23}(z_{1},z_{2})
) to have a zero of order at least
1
with respect to
z_{1}
(resp.
z_{2}
) and
E_{31}(z_{1},z_{2})
to have a zero of order at least
1
with respect to both
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
. So we get

(3.7)

{\tt{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}F_{11}(z_{1},z_{2})&F_{12}(z_{1},z_{2})&F_{13}(z_{1},z_{2})\\
z_{1}F_{21}(z_{1},z_{2})&F_{22}(z_{1},z_{2})&F_{23}(z_{1},z_{2})\\
z_{1}z_{2}F_{31}(z_{1},z_{2})&z_{2}F_{32}(z_{1},z_{2})&F_{33}(z_{1},z_{2})\end{pmatrix}\;\Bigg|\;F_{ij}(z_{1},z_{2})\in\mathcal{O}_{2}\right\}.

We notice that

(3.8)

\displaystyle z_{1}z_{2}F_{31}=z_{1}z_{2}(g_{31}h_{11}+g_{32}h_{21}+g_{33}h_{31})\quad\text{but}

(3.9)

\displaystyle z_{1}F_{21}=z_{1}(g_{21}h_{11}+g_{22}h_{21}+g_{23}h_{31}z_{2}).

In other words, the multiplication law for the entry
(3,1)
is like that of
SL_{3}(\mathcal{O}_{2})
, but that of
(2,1)
has an extra
z_{2}
. Similarly, the law for the entries
(2,2)
and
(1,1)
are unlike the
1
-dimensional case.

3.1. Some remarks on specializations of
n
-parahoric groups and examples

The purpose of this subsection is to show that specialization of
n
-parahoric groups leads to a strictly larger scope than that of parahoric groups.

Example 3.3
.

This example shows the simplest case of relating higher-dimensional parahorics to lower-dimensional ones. We place ourselves in the context of the previous example. If we set
s_{2},z_{2}:=1
or
s_{1},z_{1}:=1
, we get the two standard maximal parabolics. If we set
s_{1},z_{1},s_{2},z_{2}:=t
, all variables as
t
, then conjugation is by
\diag(t^{\frac{1}{3}},1,t^{\frac{-1}{3}})
. This gives the standard Iwahori; see Section
2.6
.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
\theta\in\mathcal{A}_{T}
and let
m_{r}(\theta):=-\lfloor r(\theta)\rfloor
as in (
1.1
). Then the function
{\text{\linespread m}}_{\theta}\colon\tilde{\Phi}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
defined by
\text{\linespread m}_{\theta}(r):=m_{r}(\theta)
is concave.

Proof.

Let
r_{i}\in\tilde{\Phi}
be such that
\sumop\displaylimits_{i}r_{i}\in\tilde{\Phi}
as well. We need to check that

(3.10)

\text{\linespread m}_{\theta}\left({\sumop\displaylimits_{i}r_{i}}\right)=m_{\sumop\displaylimits_{i}r_{i}(\theta)}\leq\sumop\displaylimits_{i}m_{r_{i}}(\theta).

This follows by setting
a_{i}:=r_{i}(\theta)
and observing
\lfloor a_{1}+\cdots+a_{n}\rfloor\geq\lfloor a_{1}\rfloor+\lfloor a_{2}\rfloor+\cdots+\lfloor a_{n}\rfloor
.
∎

Definition 3.5
.

For an
n-concave
function
{\bf f}:=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n})
, by the specialization of
{\tt{P}}_{\bf f}\subset G(k(\!(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\!))
to the diagonal

(3.11)

{\tt{P}}_{\bf f}^{\diag}\subset G(k(\!(t)\!)),

we mean the
bounded subgroup
obtained by setting
z_{1}=z_{2}=\cdots=z_{n}:=t
.

In particular, we have the specializations
{\tt{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}^{\diag}
(of
{\tt{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}
) and
{\tt{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}^{\diag}
(of
{\tt{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
) associated to
\boldsymbol{\Omega}=({}_{1},\ldots,{}_{n})
and
{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}:=(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{n})
, respectively.

Since sums of concave functions are concave, in terms of the concave function
\Phi\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
given by
r\mapsto\sumop\displaylimits_{i}m_{r}(\theta_{i})
, we get

(3.12)

{\tt{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}^{\diag}=\left\langle T(k\llbracket t\rrbracket),U_{r}\left(t^{\sumop\displaylimits_{i}m_{r}(\theta_{i})}k\llbracket t\rrbracket\right),r\in\Phi\right\rangle.

We set

(3.13)

\mathfrak{P}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}^{\diag}:=\Lie\left({\tt{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}^{\diag}\right).

Remark 3.6
.

We now make some remarks comparing the
bounded subgroup

{\tt{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}^{\diag}
with the
parahoric subgroup

{\tt{P}}_{\sumop\displaylimits_{i}\theta_{i}}
, both subgroups of
G(K)
. Recall that
m_{r}(\theta)=-\lfloor r(\theta)\rfloor
. So let us compare
m_{r}(\sumop\displaylimits_{i}\theta_{i})
with
\sumop\displaylimits_{i}m_{r}(\theta_{i})
. For simplicity, we assume that all
\theta_{i}
lie in the dominant Weyl chamber so that for all positive roots
r
, we have
r(\theta_{i})>0
.

Consider the case when for all
r\in R^{+}
, we have
m_{r}(\sumop\displaylimits_{i}\theta_{i})=\sumop\displaylimits_{i}m_{r}(\theta_{i})
. This occurs for instance when all
\theta_{i}
lie in the alcove
\mathbf{a}_{0}
and their sum lies in
\mathbf{a}_{0}\setminus\text{far wall}
or when the
\theta_{i}
lie in the lattice defined by the
\theta_{\alpha_{i}}
; see (
2.1
). For alcove vertices
\theta_{\alpha_{i}}
, an example of such a
{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}=(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{\ell})
is given by assigning

(3.14)

\theta_{i}:=\frac{\theta_{\alpha_{i}}}{(\ell+1)}.

In this case, for any
r\in\Phi^{+}
, although
r(\theta_{i})>0
, we nonetheless have
m_{r}(\sumop\displaylimits_{i}\theta_{i})=0
and
m_{r}(\theta_{i})=0
and, further,
m_{-r}(\theta_{i})=1
and
m_{-r}(\sumop\displaylimits_{i}\theta_{i})=1
. Then the orders of poles that the group entries of
{\tt{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}^{\diag}
and
{\tt{P}}_{\sumop\displaylimits_{i}\theta_{i}}
can allow at
r
are the same. However, the order of zeros of elements of
{\tt{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}^{\diag}
at
-r
is the number
S_{r}
of distinct simple roots
\alpha\in S
occurring with strictly positive coefficient in the expression of
r
. Thus,

(3.15)

{\tt{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}^{\diag}=\left\langle T(k\llbracket t\rrbracket),U_{r}(R\llbracket t\rrbracket),U_{-r}\left(t^{S_{r}}k\llbracket t\rrbracket\right),r\in\Phi^{+}\right\rangle.

Further, the bounded subgroup
{\tt{P}}_{\sumop\displaylimits_{i}\theta_{i}}
is not a
G(K)
-conjugate of a subgroup of
{\tt{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}^{\diag}
in this case unless
|S|=1
.

Now consider the case wherein, for
r\in R^{+}
, we have
m_{r}(\sumop\displaylimits_{i}\theta_{i})<\sumop\displaylimits_{i}m_{r}(\theta_{i})
. This occurs for instance when
G=\SL_{2}
,
n=2
and
r(\theta_{1})=r(\theta_{2})=a_{1}=a_{2}=\frac{1}{2}
. In this case,
m_{r}(\theta_{i})=0
but
m_{r}(\sumop\displaylimits_{i}\theta_{i})=-1
. Then the orders of poles which the elements of
{\tt{P}}_{\sumop\displaylimits_{i}\theta_{i}}
can allow are strictly greater than those of the elements of
{\tt{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}^{\diag}
at
r
. On the other hand,
m_{-r}(\theta_{i})=1
and
m_{-r}(\sumop\displaylimits_{i}\theta_{i})=1
as well. So the order of zeros of
{\tt{P}}_{\sumop\displaylimits_{i}\theta_{i}}
at
-r
is at least
1
but that of
{\tt{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}^{\diag}
at
-r
is at least
2
. So reasoning on the orders of the poles and zeros, it follows that
{\tt{P}}_{\sumop\displaylimits_{i}\theta_{i}}
is not a
G(K)
-conjugate of a subgroup of
{\tt{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}^{\diag}
.

3.2. Constructing concave functions of
type III
from
type I

The purpose of this subsection is to illustrate the phenomenon
that one may begin with a group scheme datum of
type I
at the generic points of the divisors, but the
type
of the
BT
-group scheme at points of depth bigger than
1
could strictly be even of
type III
. The computations should be seen in the setting of Theorem
1.5
.

To illustrate the issue, we work with (
3.14
), which are the simplest non-trivial cases; these also highlight the role of the root system.
We make explicit constructions for
A_{2}
,
B_{2}
and
G_{2}
cases with
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
given by (
3.14
). The contrast between the
B_{2}
and
G_{2}
cases highlights the critical role that the root system of the group plays in the analysis.

Recall that for a simple root
\alpha
, we denote by
\theta_{\alpha}
, see (
2.1
), the vertices of the alcove.

Example 3.7

(
A_{2}
)
.

The set of positive roots is
\Phi^{+}=\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}\}
. We work with
\theta_{1}:=\theta_{\alpha_{1}}/3=\omega^{\vee}_{1}/3
and
\theta_{2}:=\theta_{\alpha_{2}}/3=\omega^{\vee}_{2}/3
. Recall that
m_{r}(\theta):=-\lfloor r(\theta)\rfloor
. We have

(3.16)

r(\theta_{1})=\begin{cases}1/3,0,1/3&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{+},\\
-1/3,0,-1/3&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{-},\end{cases}

(3.17)

m_{r}(\theta_{1})=\begin{cases}0,0,0&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{+},\\
1,0,1&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{-},\end{cases}

and similarly for
\theta_{2}
. We get

(3.18)

r(\theta_{1}+\theta_{2})=\begin{cases}1/3,1/3,2/3&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{+},\\
-1/3,-1/3,-2/3&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{-},\end{cases}

(3.19)

m_{r}(\theta_{1}+\theta_{2})=\begin{cases}0,0,0&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{+},\\
1,1,1&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{-}.\end{cases}

The function
\text{\linespread m}\colon r\mapsto\sumop\displaylimits m_{r}(\theta_{j})
is given by

(3.20)

\text{\linespread m}(r)=\begin{cases}0,0,0&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{+},\\
1,1,2&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{-};\end{cases}

it computes
{\tt{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}^{\diag}
.
Let
\Omega\subset\mathcal{A}_{T}
be the bounded subset
\Omega=\{b,b^{\prime}\}
, where
b=\theta_{1}+\theta_{2}
and
b^{\prime}=2\cdot\theta_{1}+2\cdot\theta_{2}
. Recall that
m_{r}(\Omega)=-\lfloor\Inf_{\theta\in\Omega}r(\theta)\rfloor
. For the subset
\Omega
, define the concave function
f(r):=m_{r}(\Omega)
; see (
1.3
). We have

(3.21)

r(b)=\begin{cases}1/3,1/3,2/3&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{+},\\
-1/3,-1/3,-2/3&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{-},\end{cases}

(3.22)

r(b^{\prime})=\begin{cases}2/3,2/3,4/3&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{+},\\
\penalty\ -2/3,-2/3,-4/3&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{-},\end{cases}

(3.23)

m_{r}(\Omega)=\begin{cases}0,0,0&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{+},\\
1,1,2&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{-}.\end{cases}

Hence,
\text{\linespread m}(r)=f(r)
for every
r\in\Phi
;
i.e.
it is of
type II
. In particular, the group scheme
\mathfrak G_{\text{\linespread m}}
associated to the concave function on
\spec(A)
is realized by a bounded subset of the affine apartment.

Before we discuss the next set of examples, we prove a lemma regarding the concave function
f
defined above. Observe that

(3.24)

\displaystyle f(r)=a\iff a-1<-\inf_{\theta\in\Omega}r(\theta)\leq a\iff 1-a>\inf_{\theta\in\Omega}r(\theta)\geq-a.

The last statement can be interpreted as saying that

(1)

for all
\theta\in\Omega
, we have
r(\theta)\geq-a
;

(2)

there exists a
\theta^{\prime}\in\Omega
such that
1-a>r(\theta^{\prime})
.

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
\Omega
be a non-empty bounded region in the affine apartment. Suppose that there is a root
r
such that
f(-r)=-f(r)
. Set
n:=f(r)
. Let
H_{r}(n)
be the hyperplane in the affine apartment defined by
H_{r}(n):=\{\theta\mid r(\theta)=-n\}
. Then,

(3.25)

\displaystyle\Omega\subset H_{r}(n).

Proof.

This is immediate from property (
1
) above.
∎

Example 3.9

(
B_{2}
, first example)
.

The set of positive roots is
\Phi^{+}=\{\beta,\alpha,\beta+\alpha,\beta+2\cdot\alpha\}
. We work with
\theta_{1}:=\theta_{\beta}/3=\omega^{\vee}_{1}/3
and
\theta_{2}:=\theta_{\alpha}/3=\omega^{\vee}_{2}/6
. We have

(3.26)

r(\theta_{1})=\begin{cases}1/3,0,1/3,1/3&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{+},\\
-1/3,0,-1/3,-1/3&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{-},\end{cases}

(3.27)

m_{r}(\theta_{1})=\begin{cases}0,0,0,0&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{+},\\
1,0,1,1&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{-},\end{cases}

(3.28)

r(\theta_{2})=\begin{cases}0,1/6,1/6,1/3&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{+},\\
\penalty\ 0,-1/6,-1/6,-1/3&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{-},\end{cases}

(3.29)

m_{r}(\theta_{2})=\begin{cases}0,0,0,0&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{+},\\
0,1,1,1&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{-}.\end{cases}

We get

(3.30)

r(\theta_{1}+\theta_{2})=\begin{cases}1/3,1/3,2/3,2/3&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{+},\\
-1/3,-1/3,-2/3,-2/3&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{-},\end{cases}

(3.31)

m_{r}(\theta_{1}+\theta_{2})=\begin{cases}0,0,0,0&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{+},\\
\penalty\ 1,1,1,1&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{-}.\end{cases}

The function
\text{\linespread m}\colon r\mapsto\sumop\displaylimits m_{r}(\theta_{j})
is given by

(3.32)

\text{\linespread m}(r)=\begin{cases}0,0,0,0&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{+},\\
1,1,2,2&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{-};\end{cases}

it computes
{\tt{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}^{\diag}
.
Let
\Omega\subset\mathcal{A}_{T}
be the bounded subset
\Omega=\{b,b^{\prime}\}
, where
b=\theta_{1}+\theta_{2}
and
b^{\prime}=(2-e)\cdot\theta_{1}+(2+e^{\prime})\cdot\theta_{2}
with
0<e,e^{\prime}<1
. Recall that
m_{r}(\Omega)=-\lfloor\text{Inf}_{\theta\in\Omega}r(\theta)\rfloor
. For the subset
\Omega
, define the concave function
f(r):=m_{r}(\Omega)
. We have

(3.33)

r(b)=\begin{cases}1/3,1/6,1/2,2/3&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{+},\\
\penalty\ -1/3,-1/3,-1/2,-2/3&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{-},\end{cases}

(3.34)

r(b^{\prime})=\begin{cases}2/3-e/3,1/3+e^{\prime}/6,1+(e^{\prime}-2e)/6,4/3+(e^{\prime}-e)/3&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{+},\\
-2/3+e/3,-1/3-e^{\prime}/6,-1-(2e-e^{\prime})/6,-4/3-(e-e^{\prime})/3&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{-},\end{cases}

(3.35)

m_{r}(\Omega)=\begin{cases}0,0,0,0&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{+},\\
1,1,2,2&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{-}.\end{cases}

Hence,
\text{\linespread m}(r)=f(r)
for every
r\in\Phi
;
i.e.
it is again of
type II
.

Example 3.10

(
B_{2}
, second example)
.

The second example here has special significance for Section
11
. This time we work with
\theta_{1}:=\theta_{\alpha}/2=\omega^{\vee}_{2}/4
and
\theta_{2}:=\theta_{\alpha}=\omega^{\vee}_{2}/2
. Then we get

(3.36)

m_{r}(\theta_{1})=\begin{cases}0,0,0,0&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{+},\\
0,1,1,1&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{-}\end{cases}

and

(3.37)

m_{r}(\theta_{2})=\begin{cases}0,0,0,-1&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{+},\\
0,1,1,1&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{-}.\end{cases}

The function
\text{\linespread m}\colon r\mapsto\sumop\displaylimits m_{r}(\theta_{j})
is given by

(3.38)

\text{\linespread m}(r)=\begin{cases}0,0,0,-1&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{+},\\
0,2,2,2&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{-}.\end{cases}

Claim
.

The concave function
m
in (
3.38
) is not of
type II
; i.e. there does not exist any bounded region
\Omega\subset\mathcal{A}
such that
\text{\linespread m}=f
.

If the claim is not true, then there is an
\Omega
such that
f(\beta)=-f(-\beta)=0
and hence
\Omega\subset H(0)
. Again, since
f(\alpha)=0,f(-\alpha)=2
, see (
3.38
), it follows that for all
\theta\in\Omega
, by (
1
) before Lemma
3.8
, we get the condition

(3.39)

\displaystyle 0\leq\alpha(\theta)\leq 2\quad\forall\theta\in\Omega.

Similarly,
f(\alpha+\beta)=0,f(-(\alpha+\beta))=2
, see (
3.38
), forces

(3.40)

\displaystyle 0\leq(\alpha+\beta)(\theta)\leq 2\quad\forall\theta\in\Omega,

and
f(2\cdot\alpha+\beta)=-1,f(-(2\cdot\alpha+\beta))=2
forces

(3.41)

\displaystyle(2\cdot\alpha+\beta)(\theta)\leq 2\quad\forall\theta\in\Omega.

On the other hand, since
f(-\alpha)=2
, property (
2
) says that there exists a
\theta^{\prime}\in\Omega
such that

(3.42)

\displaystyle 1<\alpha(\theta^{\prime}).

Notice that since
\beta(\theta^{\prime})=0
, the two inequalities (
3.41
) and (
3.42
) are not compatible.

Example 3.11

(
G_{2}
, first example)
.

The set of positive roots is
\Phi^{+}=\{\alpha,\beta,\alpha+\beta,2\cdot\alpha+\beta,3\cdot\alpha+\beta,3\cdot\alpha+2\cdot\beta\}
. We work with
\theta_{1}:=\theta_{\alpha}/3=\omega^{\vee}_{1}/9
and
\theta_{2}:=\theta_{\beta}/3=\omega^{\vee}_{2}/6
. We have

(3.43)

r(\theta_{1})=\begin{cases}1/9,0,1/9,2/9,1/3,1/3&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{+},\\
-1/9,0,-1/9,-2/9,-1/3,-1/3&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{-},\end{cases}

(3.44)

r(\theta_{2})=\begin{cases}0,1/6,1/6,1/6,1/6,1/3&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{+},\\
0,-1/6,-1/6,-1/6,-1/6,-1/3&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{-},\end{cases}

(3.45)

m_{r}(\theta_{1})=\begin{cases}0,0,0,0,0,0&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{+},\\
1,0,1,1,1,1&\quad{\text{i}f}r\in\Phi^{-}.\end{cases}

(3.46)

m_{r}(\theta_{2})=\begin{cases}0,0,0,0,0,0&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{+},\\
0,1,1,1,1,1&\quad{\text{i}f}r\in\Phi^{-},\end{cases}

We get

(3.47)

r(\theta_{1}+\theta_{2})=\begin{cases}1/9,1/6,5/18,7/18,1/2,2/3&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{+},\\
-1/9,-1/6,-5/18,-7/18,-1/2,-2/3&\quad{\text{i}f}r\in\Phi^{-},\end{cases}

(3.48)

m_{r}(\theta_{1}+\theta_{2})=\begin{cases}0,0,0,0,0,0&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{+},\\
1,1,1,1,1,1&\quad{\text{i}f}r\in\Phi^{-}.\end{cases}

The function
\text{\linespread m}\colon r\mapsto\sumop\displaylimits m_{r}(\theta_{j})
is given by

(3.49)

\text{\linespread m}(r)=\begin{cases}0,0,0,0,0,0&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{+},\\
1,1,2,2,2,2&\quad{\text{i}f}r\in\Phi^{-};\end{cases}

it computes
{\tt{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}^{\diag}
.

Claim
.

In contrast, the concave function
m
in (
3.49
) is not of
type II
; i.e. there does not exist any bounded region
\Omega\subset\mathcal{A}
such that
\text{\linespread m}=f
. This is explicit here, but see also
[
BT72
, Section 6.4.4]
.

To prove this claim, let us suppose to the contrary that there exists such a bounded subset
\Omega\subset\mathcal{A}_{T}
. Let
\theta\in\Omega
be given by

\theta:=\left(x\omega_{1}^{\vee},y\omega_{2}^{\vee}\right).

Then we have

(3.50)

r(\theta)=\begin{cases}x,y,x+y,2x+y,3x+y,3x+2y&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{+},\\
-x,-y,-x-y,-2x-y,-3x-y,-3x-2y&\quad{\text{i}f}r\in\Phi^{-}.\end{cases}

For
r\in\Phi^{+}
, we have
-\lfloor\inf_{\theta\in\Omega}(r,\theta)\rfloor=0
, so
\inf_{\theta\in\Omega}(r,\theta)\in[0,1)
. Thus for
r=\alpha
and
\beta
, for all points
\theta
in
\Omega
, we have

x,y\in[0,1).

Now let us assume that
\inf_{\theta\in\Omega}(r,\theta)
is realized at
(x_{1},y_{1})
for
r=-(\alpha+\beta)
and at
(x_{2},y_{2})
for
r=-(3\alpha+2\beta)
. In particular,
x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2}\in[0,1)
.

We have
-\lfloor-(x_{1}+y_{1})\rfloor=2
. So
-2\leq-x_{1}-y_{1}<-1
. Thus

(3.51)

\displaystyle 1<x_{1}+y_{1}\leq 2,

(3.52)

\displaystyle x_{1}>0.

By the condition for
-3\alpha-2\beta
, we have

-3x_{2}-2y_{2}\leq-3x_{1}-2y_{1}
. Thus

(3.53)

3x_{1}+2y_{1}\leq 3x_{2}+2y_{2}.

By
-\lfloor-3x_{2}-2y_{2}\rfloor=2
, we have
-1>-3x_{2}-2y_{2}\leq-2
. Thus

(3.54)

1<3x_{2}+2y_{2}\leq 2.

We get the following contradiction:

2\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{1}}}{{<}}2x_{1}+2y_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{2}}}{{<}}3x_{1}+2y_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{310}}}{{\leq}}3x_{2}+2y_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{4}}}{{\leq}}2.

This completes the proof of the claim.

Example 3.12

(
G_{2}
, second example)
.

The second example here has special significance for Section
11
. We now work with
\theta_{1}:=\theta_{\alpha}=\omega^{\vee}_{\alpha}/3
and
\theta_{2}:=2.\theta_{\alpha}=2.\omega^{\vee}_{\alpha}/3
. Then we get

(3.55)

m_{r}(\theta_{1})=\begin{cases}0,0,0,0,-1,-1&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{+},\\
1,0,1,1,1,1&\quad{\text{i}f}r\in\Phi^{-},\end{cases}

(3.56)

m_{r}(\theta_{2})=\begin{cases}0,0,0,-1,-2,-2&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{+},\\
1,0,1,2,2,2&\quad{\text{i}f}r\in\Phi^{-}.\end{cases}

The function
\text{\linespread m}\colon r\mapsto\sumop\displaylimits m_{r}(\theta_{j})
is given by

(3.57)

\text{\linespread m}(r)=\begin{cases}0,0,0,-1,-3,-3&\quad\text{if }r\in\Phi^{+},\\
2,0,2,3,3,3&\quad{\text{i}f}r\in\Phi^{-}.\end{cases}

Claim
.

The concave function
m
in (
3.57
) is not of
type II
.

If the claim is not true, then there is an
\Omega
such that
f(\beta)=-f(-\beta)=0
, and hence by Lemma
3.8
, we have
\Omega\subset H_{\beta}(0)
. Thus

(3.58)

\displaystyle\beta(\theta)=0,\quad\forall\theta\in\Omega.

Similarly,
f(3\alpha+2\beta)=3,f(-(3\alpha+2\beta))=-3
(
3.57
) forces

(3.59)

\displaystyle(3\alpha+2\beta)(\theta)=3\quad\forall\theta\in\Omega,

and (
3.58
) and (
3.59
) give us

(3.60)

\displaystyle\alpha(\theta)=1\quad\forall\theta\in\Omega.

Now since
f(-\alpha)=2
, by (
2
) before Lemma
3.8
, we know that there exists a
\theta^{\prime}\in\Omega
such that
\alpha(\theta^{\prime})>1
, which is incompatible with (
3.60
).

Example 3.13
.

(The case of a constant concave function). Let
a\geq 0
and
f(r)=a
for all
r\in\Phi
. In this case, when
G
is of type
A_{n}
,
B_{n}
or
C_{n}
, there exist regions
\Omega
such that
f=f
. For
G_{2}
when
a\geq 3
, there does not exist such a region.

3.3. Various cones of concave functions

We consider the question of how to construct general concave functions from points in the apartment. It turns out that this can always be done in the case
A_{n}
(see Proposition
3.21
and the remarks before it).
Let
\Phi
be an irreducible root system. Let

(3.61)

\Phi^{\Sigma}:=\left\{(\alpha,\beta)\in\Phi^{2}\mid\alpha+\beta\in\Phi\right\}.

For
(\alpha,\beta)\in\Phi^{\Sigma}
, we get functionals
\psi_{(\alpha,\beta)}
and
\psi_{\alpha}
defined on
\{f\colon\Phi\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by
f\mapsto f(\alpha)+f(\beta)-f(\alpha+\beta)
and
f\mapsto f(\alpha)+f(-\alpha)
, respectively.

Let
\mathcal{C}
denote the space of concave functions
\{f\colon\Phi\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\}
. It can be expressed as

(3.62)

\mathcal{C}=\cap\left\{\psi_{(\alpha,\beta)}\geq 0\right\}\cap\left\{\psi_{\alpha}\geq 0\right\}.

Being an intersection of half-spaces, it is a polyhedral cone, further since the intersection of the open half-spaces is non-empty, we see that
\mathcal{C}
is of dimension
|\Phi|
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
denote the cone of concave functions defined by bounded regions
\Omega\subset\mathcal{A}_{T}
.

Proposition 3.14
.

Let
\Phi
be an irreducible root system. The dimension of
\mathcal{C}
equals
|\Phi|
.

Proof.

Recall that a bounded subset
\Omega\subset\mathcal{A}_{T}
defines the concave function

(3.63)

f\colon\Phi\longrightarrow\mathbb{R},\quad r\longmapsto-\left\lfloor\inf_{\theta\in\Omega}r(\theta)\right\rfloor.

For
r\in\Phi
, let
H_{r}
be the hyperplane in
\mathcal{A}_{T}
where
r
vanishes. Consider the unit sphere
S
in
\mathcal{A}_{T}
. We fix an isomorphism of
T
with a product of
\mathbb{G}_{m}
. This gives a basis of
Y(T)
and
X(T)
. Now we may identify
\mathcal{A}_{T}
with its dual space so that for a root
r\in\Phi
and
\theta\in\mathcal{A}_{T}
, the evaluation
r(\theta)
equals the inner product of
\theta
with the image of
r
. Thus, after this identification, every root
r\in\Phi
defines a canonical unit vector normal to
H_{r}
meeting
S
in
p_{r}
. Since
\Phi
is irreducible,
H_{r}=H_{s}
implies
r={\pm}s
. Further,
-p_{r}=p_{-r}
.
So
r\mapsto p_{r}
defines a bijection. To prove the proposition, we now consider the bounded subset

(3.64)

\Omega:=\left\{p_{r}\mid r\in\Phi\right\}.

So
|\Omega|=|\Phi|
. For any
r\in\Phi
,
\inf_{\theta\in\Omega}r(\theta)
is attained at
p_{-r}
. We now let points vary in radial directions determined by
\{p_{r}\}
. In small open neighbourhoods of
p_{r}
, if
{}^{\prime}:=\{p_{r}^{\prime}\}
denotes a neighbouring set of points, we again see that

\inf_{\theta\in{}^{\prime}}r(\theta)
is attained at
p^{\prime}_{-r}
. This completes the proof.
∎

3.4. Recovering the bounded group
\boldsymbol{\tt{P}}
over a DVR in terms of
n
-parahoric groups

Let
\Omega\subset\mathcal{A}
be a bounded subset of the apartment. Without loss of generality, we may suppose that it is compact.

We make a small modification in the notation just for this lemma. Let
A_{1}=L[t]
be a polynomial ring over a field
L
that is not necessarily algebraically closed. Let
K_{1}=\Fract(A_{1})
. Similarly, let
A_{N}=L[z_{1},\ldots,z_{N}]
and
K_{N}=\Fract(A_{N})
.
Let

(3.65)

\displaystyle{\tt{P}}:=\left\langle T(L[t]),\,U_{r}\left(t^{m_{r}(\Omega)}L[t]\right),r\in\Phi\right\rangle

and for
\boldsymbol{\Theta}=(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{N})
,

(3.66)

\displaystyle{\tt{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}:=\left\langle T(A_{N}]),U_{r}\left(\prodop\displaylimits_{1\leq j\leq N}z_{j}^{-\lfloor(r,\theta_{j})\rfloor}A_{N}\right),\;r\in\Phi\right\rangle.

The next lemma shows the natural relationship between
n-bounded
groups associated to bounded subsets
\Omega
in the affine apartment and
N-parahoric
groups. We give it here for its general interest.

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
N=|\Phi|
and let the inclusion
G(K_{1})\subset G(K_{N})
be obtained by mapping the uniformizer
t
to
\prodop\displaylimits_{r\in\Phi}z_{r}
. Given a bounded domain
\Omega
, there exist an
N
-tuple
\boldsymbol{\Theta}=(\ldots,\theta_{r},\ldots)_{r\in\Phi}
of points such that taking intersection in
G(K_{N})
, we have the equality

(3.67)

{\tt{P}}=G(K_{1})\cap{\tt{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}.

Proof.

For every root
r\in\Phi
, we may choose a
\theta_{r}\in\Omega
such that we have

(3.68)

(r,\theta_{r})\leq(r,\theta)\quad\forall\theta\in\Omega.

Set
L[\Phi]:=L[z_{r},r\in\Phi]
and consider the
\tt N
-parahoric given by

\boldsymbol{\Theta}=(\ldots,\theta_{j},\ldots)_{j\in\Phi}.

Thus by definition, we have

(3.69)

{\tt{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}=\left\langle T(L[\Phi]),\,U_{r}\left(\prodop\displaylimits_{j\in\Phi}z_{j}^{-\lfloor(r,\theta_{j})\rfloor}L[\Phi]\right),r\in\Phi\right\rangle.

For
r\in\Phi
, consider the group
U_{r}\big(\prodop\displaylimits_{j\in\Phi}z_{j}^{-\lfloor(r,\theta_{j})\rfloor}L[\Phi]\big)
. By (
3.68
), we see that

(3.70)

(r,\theta_{r})\leq(r,\theta_{j})\quad\forall j\in\Phi.

Thus
-\lfloor(r,\theta_{r})\rfloor\geq-\lfloor(r,\theta_{j})\rfloor
for all
j\in\Phi
. In other words, we have

(3.71)

-\lfloor(r,\theta_{r})\rfloor=-\left\lfloor\inf_{\theta\in\Omega}(r,\theta)\right\rfloor=m_{r}(\Omega).

Now
K_{1}=L(t)
and
A_{1}=L[t]
, so letting
t=\prodop\displaylimits_{r\in\Phi}z_{r}
, for every root
r\in\Phi
, we get the desired order of zeros and poles. Further,
T(L[\Phi])\cap T(K_{1})=T(A_{1})
. Since the group law is inherited from
G(K_{1})
, the intersection is the parahoric subgroup
{\tt{P}}
.
∎

For
1\leq i\leq n
, let
f_{i}\colon\Phi\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be concave functions. Let
{\bf F}:=(\ldots,f_{i},\ldots)\colon\Phi\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
. In (
1.8
), we have defined the
n
-concave subgroup
{\tt{P}}_{\bf f}
of
G(K_{n})
in terms of generators.

Lemma 3.16
.

Let
G(K_{1})\subset G(K_{n})
by mapping the uniformizer
t
to
\prodop\displaylimits_{1\leq i\leq n}z_{i}
. Taking intersection in
G(K_{n})
, we have the equality

(3.72)

{\tt{P}_{sup_{i}f_{i}}}=G(K_{1})\cap{\tt{P}}_{\bf f},

Proof.

The proof is almost identical to that of Lemma
3.15
.
∎

Let
\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{ext}}
denote the cone defined by

(1)

taking sums of concave functions of the form
\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{m}f_{{}_{i}}
, where we let
m\geq 1
and the bounded regions
{}_{i}\subset\mathcal{A}_{T}
vary,

(2)

changing the uniformizers by the substitution
t=z^{n}
or
t^{n}=z
and

(3)

taking supremums.

We have the obvious inclusions

(3.73)

\mathcal{C}\subset\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{ext}}\subset\mathcal{C}.

Example 3.17
.

The first inclusion is strict for the concave function (
3.49
) by the claim in Example
3.10
.

For irreducible reduced root systems, the notions of quasi-concave and concave functions coincide. Let
f
be any concave function defined more generally on
\Phi
. Let
E
be the set of points in
\mathcal{A}_{T}
fixed by
G_{f}
. For a root
\alpha\in\Phi
, let

(3.74)

S(\alpha):=\left\{\left(\alpha_{i},\lambda_{i}\right)_{\{1\leq i\leq r\}}\in\cup_{r\geq 1}\left(\Phi\times\mathbb{Q}^{*}_{+}\right)^{r}\;\Big|\;\sumop\displaylimits_{1\leq i\leq r}\lambda_{i}\alpha_{i}=\alpha\right\}

denote all possible ways of writing
\alpha
in the rational positive cone of some subset of roots in
\Phi
. Let
f^{\prime}
be the function on
\Phi
defined by

(3.75)

f^{\prime}(\alpha):=\inf_{S(\alpha)}\;\left\lceil\sumop\displaylimits_{1\leq i\leq r}\lambda_{i}f(\alpha_{i})\right\rceil.

By
[
Cou11
, Proposition 3.3]
, we have
f^{\prime}=f_{E}
for some subset
E
of the apartment.

Example 3.18
.

For the case
G=\SL_{2}
, the three cones coincide because any concave function is of the form
f
(see Proposition
3.21
). Indeed, by
[
Cou11
, Proposition 3.3]
, for
G=\SL_{2}
, we have
S(\alpha)=\{(\alpha,1)\}
and
S(-\alpha)=\{(-\alpha,1)\}
. Thus for
G=\SL_{2}
, the subset
E
determines
f
in return.

Proposition 3.19
.

We now return to the case
G=G_{2}
. For the concave function
m
in (
3.49
),
\text{\linespread m}^{\prime}
equals
m
except at
-\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}
, where it is
1
. The region is the dilation of the alcove
\mathbf{a}_{0}
by a factor of
2
, i.e. enclosed by the origin,
2\theta_{\alpha_{1}}
and

2\theta_{\alpha_{2}}
.

Proof.

We give an indirect proof. Since
m
does not come from a region, we have the inequality
\text{\linespread m}^{\prime}<\text{\linespread m}
. Now we can check that the concave function coming from the dilation of
\mathbf{a}_{0}
by a factor of
2
is as stated above. Since there cannot be any other concave function between them, this must be
\text{\linespread m}^{\prime}
.
∎

Remark 3.20
.

It would be interesting to understand the inclusion
\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{ext}}\subset\mathcal{C}
better. Given bounded regions
\{{}_{1},\ldots,{}_{n}\}
, consider the concave function
f
taking
r
to

\sumop\displaylimits m_{r}({}_{i})
. It would be interesting to understand the region corresponding to
f^{\prime}
as a function of
\{{}_{i}\}
.

Recall from (
1.8
) that if
f\colon\Phi\to{\mathbb R}
is a concave function, then we can associate to it the bounded subgroup
{\tt{P}}_{f}:=\langle T(\mathcal{O}),U_{r}(z^{f(r)}\mathcal{O}),r\in\Phi\rangle
of
G(K)
. Note that
\mathfrak{G}_{f}(\mathcal{O})={\tt{P}}_{f}
.

Following
[
BT84a
, Section 4.5.2]
and
[
Cou11
, Section 2, p. 540]
) we say that a concave function
f
is
optimal
if for any concave function
f^{\prime}\colon\Phi\to{\mathbb R}
such that
f^{\prime}>f
, we have a strict inclusion
{\tt{P}}_{f^{\prime}}\subsetneq{\tt{P}}_{f}
. However, in the context of the present paper, it is easy to see that if
f
is a concave function, then so is
\lceil f\rceil
, which is in fact optimal. Moreover, the bounded groups
\mathfrak{G}_{f}(\mathcal{O})
coincides with
\mathfrak{G}_{\lceil f\rceil}(\mathcal{O})
. So we may as well work with optimal concave functions.

In
[
BT84b
, Remarques 3.9.3]
it is remarked that on root systems of type
A_{n}
, any optimal concave function is of the form
f
for an
enclosed
bounded subset
\Omega
of the apartment (see
[
BT72
, Section 2.4.6]
for the definition of “enclosed” subsets). For the sake of completeness, we give below a proof of this statement.

Proposition 3.21
.

Let
G
be of type
A_{n}
. Any optimal concave function
f
is of
type II
, i.e. of the form
f
for a enclosed bounded subset
\Omega
of the apartment. Thus we have
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}
; see Remark
3.20
