.

Proof.

For the convenience of the reader, we recast the notions of
[
Cou11
]
, which were developed for connected reductive groups over local fields, into the setting of this paper. Recall that the residue fields of our local rings are perfect (see Section
2.2
).
For a root
\alpha\in\Phi
, the
c(\alpha)
and
v(\alpha)
may be assumed to be
0
and
1
, respectively, and further an optimal concave function takes only integral values. By
[
Cou11
, Proposition 2.3]
, concavity is equivalent to pseudo-concavity since our group is split over
K
. A function is of the form
f
if and only if it is strongly concave according to
[
Cou11
, Proposition 3.3 ]
.

For a root
\alpha\in\Phi
, let
S^{\prime}(\alpha)\subset S(\alpha)
(see (
3.74
)) be the restricted set such that

•

the
\alpha_{i}
are linearly independent;

•

for every
i\neq j
, we have
(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j})\geq 0
; we can assume that
\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{j}
is not a root;

•

for every
i
,
\lambda_{i}<1
.

In
[
Cou11
, Lemma 5.1]
F. Courtès gives an equivalent reformulation of strong concavity in terms of a technical numerical inequality which should be checked for each element in
S^{\prime}(\alpha)
, where
\alpha
varies in
\Phi
. We claim that when
G=A_{n}
, this numerical condition is vacuously true because
S^{\prime}(\alpha)=\emptyset
. We see this as follows.

Let
\alpha=\epsilon_{l}-\epsilon_{m}
be a root of
A_{n}
. Since
\alpha=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{r}\lambda_{i}\alpha_{i}=\epsilon_{l}-\epsilon_{m}
and
0<\lambda_{i}<1
, we see that
r\geq 2
and there exist at least two indices
1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}\leq r
such that
\alpha_{i_{1}}=\epsilon_{l}-\epsilon_{j_{1}}
and
\alpha_{i_{2}}=\epsilon_{l}-\epsilon_{j_{2}}
. We suppose firstly that
j_{1}
is different from
m
. In this case, there must exist another index
1\leq i_{3}\leq r
such that
\alpha_{i_{3}}=\epsilon_{j_{1}}-\epsilon_{j_{2}}
. But then either
\alpha_{i_{1}}+\alpha_{i_{3}}=\epsilon_{l}-\epsilon_{j_{2}}
is a root of
A_{n}
, or
\alpha_{i_{1}}=-\alpha_{i_{3}}
. Both these possibilities violate the conditions for
\{(\lambda_{i_{1}},\alpha_{i_{1}}),(\lambda_{i_{2}},\alpha_{i_{2}})\}
to be a subset of an element in
S^{\prime}(\alpha)
. Now suppose that
j_{1}
equals
m
. Then
\alpha_{i_{1}}
equals
\alpha
. So we have
\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{r}\lambda_{i}\alpha_{i}-\lambda_{i_{1}}\alpha_{i_{1}}=(1-\lambda_{i_{1}})\alpha=(1-\lambda_{i_{1}})(\epsilon_{l}-\epsilon_{m})\neq 0
.
Repeating the above argument, we find that
\alpha_{i_{2}}
equals
\alpha
as well. But then
\alpha_{i_{1}}
and
\alpha_{i_{2}}
are the same. This violates the conditions for
\{(\lambda_{i_{1}},\alpha_{i_{1}}),(\lambda_{i_{2}},\alpha_{i_{2}})\}
to be a subset of an element in
S^{\prime}(\alpha)
.
∎

Remark 3.22
.

Let
f\colon\tilde{\Phi}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be a concave function. We have
f(r)=f(0+r)\leq f(0)+f(r)
. Thus,
0\leq f(0)
. Let us fix a value
f(0)
of
0\in\tilde{\Phi}
. For each simple root
\alpha\in S
, by the condition
f(0)=f(\alpha+-\alpha)\leq f(\alpha)+f(-\alpha)
, we have

(f(\alpha),f(-\alpha))\in\left\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\mid x+y\geq f(0)\right\}.

On the other hand, the condition
f(r_{1})=f(r_{1}+r_{2}-r_{2})\leq f(r_{1}+r_{2})+f(-r_{2})
implies

f(r_{1})-f(r_{2})\leq f(r_{1}+r_{2}).\leq f(r_{1})+f(r_{2}).

Thus, for an integral concave function
f
, we have infinitely many choices for values on
S^{\pm}\cup\{0\}
but only finitely many choices for non-simple roots.

Part 2.  Schematization

4. The
n
-parahoric Lie algebra bundle
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}
on an affine space

The aim of this section is to present an
ab initio
construction of the
n-parahoric
Lie algebra bundle on the affine
n
-space together with a full description of the fibres at points of higher depth. This played the initially important role for us to guess the definition of the
n
-parahoric group. The reader who is interested only in the group scheme constructions may skip this section.

For
1\leq i\leq n
, let
H_{i}\subset{\bf A}:=\mathbb{A}^{n}_{k}
denote the coordinate hyperplanes.
Let
{\bf A_{0}}\subset{\bf A}
be the open subscheme which is the complement of the union of hyperplanes. Let
\boldsymbol{\Theta}:=(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{n})\in\mathcal{A}^{n}
be a point of the
n
-apartment satisfying the conditions of Section
2.2
.
The aim of this section is to construct an
n
-parahoric Lie algebra
bundle
\mathcal{R}
on
{\bf A}:=\mathbb{A}^{n}_{k}
such that its restrictions to the generic points of the hyperplane
H_{i}
are the parahoric Lie algebra bundles associated to the weight
\theta_{i}
and to describe it on the whole of
{\bf A}
.

4.1. Elementary remarks on reflexive sheaves

We begin with three elementary lemmas which should be well known.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
E
be a locally free sheaf on an irreducible smooth scheme
X
. Let
\xi\in X
be the generic point, and let
W\subset E_{\xi}
be an
\mathcal{O}_{\xi}
-submodule. Then there exists a unique coherent subsheaf
F\subset E
such that
F_{\xi}=W
and
Q:=\Coker(F\hookrightarrow E)
is torsion-free. Moreover,
F
is a reflexive sheaf.

Proof.

Define a sheaf
\tilde{F}
by the following condition: its sections on an affine open
U\subset X
are given by
\tilde{F}(U):=E(U)\cap W
. Then it is easily seen that
\tilde{F}
defines a coherent subsheaf
F\subset E
and that it is the maximal coherent subsheaf of
E
whose fibre over
\xi
is
W
. To check that
Q
is torsion-free, let
T
be the torsion submodule of
Q
. Let
K:=\Ker(E\to Q/T)
. Then since
\xi\notin\Supp(T)
,
K_{\xi}=W
, and hence, by the maximality of
F
, we have
K=F
. Since
E
is locally free, we have
F^{\vee\vee}/F\hookrightarrow E^{\vee\vee}/F=E/F
. But since
F^{\vee\vee}/F
is only torsion and
E/F=Q
is torsion-free, it follows that
F
is automatically a reflexive sheaf.∎

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
X
be as above and
i\colon U\hookrightarrow X
an open subset such that
X\setminus U
has codimension at least
2
in
X
. Let
F_{U}
be a reflexive sheaf on
U
. Then
i_{*}(F_{U})
is a reflexive sheaf on
X
which extends
F_{U}
.

Proof.

By
[
EGAI
, Corollaire 9.4.8]
, there exists a coherent
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module
F_{1}
such that
F_{1}|_{U}\simeq F_{U}
. Set
F:=F_{1}^{**}
to be the double dual. Then
F
is reflexive, and also since
F_{U}
is reflexive,
F|_{U}\simeq F_{U}
. Hence, we have
i_{*}(F_{U})=F
; see
[
Har80
, Proposition 1.6]
. ∎

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
X
be integral and factorial. Then any reflexive sheaf of rank
1
is locally free.

Proof.

(
cf.

[
Har80
, Proposition 1.9]
) Let
\mathcal{F}
be a reflexive sheaf of rank
1
. Then since
X
is normal, there is an
open

U\subset X
such that
\codim_{X}(U)\geq 2
and
\mathcal{F}_{U}
is locally free. Since
X
is locally factorial, we have
\Pic(X)\to\text{Pic}(U)
is bijective. Hence, there is a locally free sheaf
\mathcal{L}
of rank
1
on
X
such that
\mathcal{L}_{U}=\mathcal{F}_{U}
. It is clear that
\mathcal{L}\simeq\mathcal{F}
on
X
. ∎

4.2. The construction of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}

Theorem 4.4
.

Associated to
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
, there exists a canonical Lie algebra bundle
\mathcal{R}
on
{\bf A}
which extends the trivial bundle with fibre
\mathfrak g
on
{\bf A_{0}}\subset{\bf A}
; furthermore, in a formal neighbourhood
V:=\spec(\mathcal{O}_{n})
of the origin in the affine
n
-space
{\bf A}
, we have the identification of functors from the category of
k
-algebras to
k
-Lie algebras

(4.1)

L_{n}^{+}(\mathcal{R}\mid_{V{}})=L_{n}^{+}(\mathfrak{P}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}).

Proof.

Let
b_{i\alpha}\in\mathbb{Q}
be defined by the relations

(4.2)

\theta_{i}:=\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha\in S}b_{i\alpha}\omega_{\alpha}^{\vee}\quad\text{for }i\leq n.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
db_{i\alpha}\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
and
\alpha\in S
.

Consider an inclusion of
k
-algebras
B_{0}\subset B
, where

(4.3)

B:=k\left[y_{i}^{\pm 1}\right]_{1\leq i\leq n},\quad B_{0}:=k\left[x_{i}^{\pm 1}\right]_{1\leq i\leq n}

and
\{y_{i}^{d}=x_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
. Let
{\bf Y^{0}}:=\spec(B)
and
{\bf A_{0}}=\spec(B_{0})
, and let
p\colon{\bf Y^{0}}\to{\bf A_{0}}
be the natural morphism. We define the “roots” map

(4.4)

\displaystyle\mathfrak r\colon{\bf Y^{0}}\longrightarrow{\bf A_{0}}\quad\text{as}

(4.5)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak r}^{\#}\left(\left(x_{i}\right)\right):=\left(y_{i}\right).

Note that as a map between tori,
\mathfrak r
is an isomorphism. Let
T_{\mathrm{ad}}=\spec(k[z_{\alpha}^{\pm 1}])
. We define the action map

(4.6)

\displaystyle\mathfrak a\colon{\bf Y^{0}}\longrightarrow T_{\mathrm{ad}}\quad\text{as}

(4.7)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak a}^{\#}\left(\left(z_{\alpha}\right)\right):=\left(\prodop\displaylimits_{1\leq i\leq n}y_{i}^{db_{i\alpha}}\right).

We consider the map

(4.8)

\displaystyle\Ad\circ\mathfrak a\colon{\bf Y^{0}}\times\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow{\bf Y^{0}}\times\mathfrak{g}

(4.9)

\displaystyle({\bf t},x)\longmapsto\left({\bf t},\Ad\big(\mathfrak a({\bf t})\big)(x)\right).

We define the embedding of modules

(4.10)

j\colon\mathfrak{g}(B_{0})\xhookrightarrow{\makebox[10.0pt]{}}\mathfrak{g}(B)\xrightarrow{\Gamma(\Ad\circ\mathfrak a)}\mathfrak{g}(B),

where the second map is the one induced by (
4.8
) on sections.
The
T
-weight space decomposition of
\mathfrak{g}
induces a
T
-weight space decomposition on
\mathfrak{g}(B_{0})
by viewing the latter as sections of the trivial bundle on
\bf A_{0}
with fibres
\mathfrak{g}
.

Let
B^{+}=k[y_{i}]_{1\leq i\leq n}
and
B_{0}^{+}:=k[x_{i}]_{1\leq i\leq n}
. Taking intersection as Lie submodules of
\mathfrak{g}(B)
, we define the following
B_{0}^{+}
-submodule of
\mathfrak{g}(B_{0})
:

(4.11)

\mathcal{R}\left(B_{0}^{+}\right):=j^{-1}\left(j\left(\mathfrak{g}(B_{0})\right)\cap\mathfrak{g}\left(B^{+}\right)\right).

In words, it is the set of elements in
\mathfrak{g}(B_{0})
which lie in
\mathfrak{g}(B^{+})
under
j
.
Taking
E
as the bundle defined by the free
B_{0}^{+}
-module
\mathfrak{g}(B^{+})
and the submodule
W:=j(\mathfrak{g}(B_{0}))
in Lemma
4.1
, we observe that
\mathcal{R}
is a reflexive sheaf on the affine embedding
{\bf A_{0}}\hookrightarrow{\bf A}:=\spec(B_{0}^{+})(\simeq{\mathbb A}^{n})
. Further, this is a sheaf of Lie algebras with its Lie bracket induced from
\mathfrak{g}(B)
.

We now check that
\mathcal{R}
is locally free on
{\bf A}
. Observe that the intersection (
4.11
) also respects
T_{\mathrm{ad}}
-weight space decomposition on the sections. More precisely, for any root
r\in\Phi
, since by definition we have
\mathcal{R}(B_{0}^{+})_{r}=j^{-1}\big(j(\mathfrak{g}_{r}(B_{0}))\cap\mathfrak{g}_{r}(B^{+})\big)
, we get the following equalities:

(4.12)

j^{-1}\left(j\left(\mathfrak{g}\left(B_{0}\right)\right)\cap\mathfrak{g}_{r}(B^{+})\right)=j^{-1}\left(j\left(\mathfrak{g}_{r}\left(B_{0}\right)\right)\cap\mathfrak{g}(B^{+})\right)=\mathcal{R}\left(B_{0}^{+}\right)_{r}.

Since
\mathcal{R}
is reflexive, there is an open subset
U\subset{\bf A}
with complement of codimension at least
2
such that the restriction
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}:=\mathcal{R}|_{U}
is locally free.
Clearly,
U
contains
{\bf A_{0}}
and the generic points
\zeta_{\alpha}
of the divisors
H_{\alpha}
. This gives a decomposition on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
obtained by restriction from
\mathcal{R}
. The locally free sheaf
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
is a direct sum of the trivial bundle
\Lie(T_{\mathrm{ad}})\times U
(coming from the
0
-weight space) and the invertible sheaves coming from the root decomposition. Now since invertible sheaves extend as invertible sheaves across codimension at least
2
, see Lemma
4.3
, the reflexivity of
\mathcal{R}
implies that this direct sum decomposition of
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
extends to
{\bf A}
. Hence
\mathcal{R}
is locally free.

We introduce some convenient notation to check the main assertion of the theorem.
Recall that
\lambda_{i}\colon\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow{\bf A_{0}}
is the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
axis of
{\bf A_{0}}
. For example,
\lambda_{i}
may be expressed in
n
coordinates as follows:

(4.13)

\lambda_{i}({\tt x})=(1,\ldots,{\tt x},\ldots 1)

with
{\tt x}
at the coordinate corresponding to
1\leq i\leq n
. The map
\mathfrak r\colon{\bf Y^{0}}\to{\bf A_{0}}
is abstractly an isomorphism of tori, and we can therefore consider the
1
-PS
\boldsymbol{\lambda_{i}}\colon\mathbb G_{m}\to{\bf Y^{0}}
defined by
\boldsymbol{\lambda_{i}}:=\mathfrak r^{-1}\circ\lambda_{i}
. We observe the following:

(4.14)

\displaystyle p\circ\boldsymbol{\lambda_{i}}=\lambda_{i},

(4.15)

\displaystyle\mathfrak a\circ(\boldsymbol{\lambda_{1}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\lambda_{n}})=(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{n})=\boldsymbol{\Theta}.

Recall that
V=\spec({\mathcal{O}}_{n})
denotes a formal neighbourhood of the origin. Let us check the isomorphism
L_{n}^{+}({\mathcal{R}}|_{V})\simeq L_{n}^{+}(\mathfrak P_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}})
by first evaluating at
k
-valued points. Since
d\cdot{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
is an
n
-tuple of
1
-PSs of
{\bf A_{0}}
, we see that

\boldsymbol{\Theta}(\ldots,{\tt x}_{i},\ldots)=\boldsymbol{\Theta}(\ldots,{\tt y}^{d}_{i},\ldots)=(d\boldsymbol{\Theta})(\ldots,{\tt y}_{i},\ldots)
has become integral in
\{{\tt y}_{i}\}
.

By (
4.11
), a section
s
of
\mathcal{R}
over
V
is first of all given by a local section
\text{\linespread s}_{K}
over the generic point
\spec(K_{n})
of
\spec(\mathcal{O}_{n})
. More precisely, this is firstly an element in
j(\mathfrak{g}(K))
. Hence this element may be written as

\text{\linespread s}_{K}=\Ad\left(\mathfrak{a}\prodop\displaylimits_{1\leq i\leq n}\boldsymbol{\lambda_{i}}({\tt x}_{i})\right)(x_{K})=\Ad\left(\prodop\displaylimits_{1\leq i\leq n}\theta_{i}({\tt x}_{i})\right)(x_{K}).

Therefore, the membership of
s
in
\mathfrak{g}(B^{+})
, see (
4.11
), gets interpreted as follows:

(4.16)

\Ad\left(\prodop\displaylimits_{1\leq i\leq n}\theta_{i}({\tt x}_{i})\right)(x_{K})\in L\mathfrak{g}(B^{+}).

But this is exactly the Lie algebra version of the conditions “
\lim_{s\rightarrow 0}\text{exists}
” for
\eta=(1,\theta)
in the observation (
2.9
) and Section
2.5
, for each individual
{\tt x}_{i}
.
In our situation we have assumed
R=k
, and so by (
4.15
), the above is equivalent to the membership

(4.17)

\text{\linespread s}\in\mathfrak{P}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}.

This proves the assertion for
k
-valued points.
The above proof goes through for all
k
-algebras because the underlying module structure is already defined on
k
, and so is Lie bracket. This completes the proof of the theorem.
∎

Corollary 4.5
.

Let the notation be as in Theorem
4.4
. Then
\lambda:=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\leq n}\lambda_{i}
is a
1
-PS of
{\bf A_{0}}
defining the diagonal curve
D
whose limit point is the origin. Let
\mathcal{D}
be a formal neighbourhood of the origin in
D
.
We get the identification
(
see (
3.13
) for the notation
)

(4.18)

L^{+}\left(\mathcal{R}\mid_{\mathcal{D}}\right)=L^{+}\left(\mathfrak{P}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}^{\diag}\right)

of the specialized objects.

Let
\lambda=\sumop\displaylimits k_{i}\lambda_{i}
, where
k_{i}\geq 0
but not all are zero. Let
\mathcal{D}
be the formal neighbourhood of the origin associated to the curve defined by
\lambda
. Let
f\colon\Phi\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be the concave function defined by the assignment
r\mapsto\sumop\displaylimits k_{i}m_{r}(\theta_{i})
. Then for any
k
-algebra
R
, we have

(4.19)

L^{+}(\mathcal{R}\mid_{\mathcal{D}})(R)=\left\langle\mathfrak{t}(R\llbracket t\rrbracket),\mathfrak{u}_{r}\left(t^{f(r)}R\llbracket t\rrbracket\right),r\in\Phi\right\rangle.

Proof.

Note that restriction to
\mathcal{D}
followed by taking global sections of
\mathcal{R}
sets all variables
z_{i}
to
t
in the sections of
\mathcal{R}
on
U
. Now the first assertion follows by the definition (
3.13
). The restriction of
\mathcal{R}
to
\mathcal{D}
in the second case followed by taking global sections corresponds to setting
z_{i}:=t^{k_{i}}
in the sections of
\mathcal{R}
on
U
.
∎

Remark 4.6
.

In the setting of Theorem
4.4
, we may view
p\colon\spec(B^{+})\to\spec(B_{0}^{+})
as a ramified covering space of affine toric varieties induced by an inclusion of lattices. The Galois group for this covering is the dual of the quotient of lattices. The computation in Theorem
4.4
can be seen as a higher-dimensional analogue of
[
BS15
]
.

5. Schematization of
n
-parahoric groups

In this section and the next couple of sections, we will freely use the theorems from
[
SGA3
]
, the paper of Bruhat–Tits
[
BT84a
]
and M. Raynaud’s book
[
Ray70
]
. We begin by recalling a few essential facts about Weil restrictions of scalars and related things.

5.1. Weil restrictions and Lie algebras

Let
X
be an arbitrary
k
-scheme.
For an affine (or possibly ind-affine) group scheme
\mathcal{H}\rightarrow X
, we denote by
\Lie(\mathcal{H})
the sheaf of Lie algebras on
X
whose sections on
U\rightarrow X
are given by

(5.1)

\Lie(\mathcal{H})(U)=\ker(\mathcal{H}(U\times k[\epsilon])\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}(U)).

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
p\colon\tilde{X}\rightarrow X
be a finite flat map of Noetherian schemes. Let
\Res_{\tilde{X}/X}
denote the “Weil restriction of scalars” functor. Let
\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\tilde{X}
be an affine group scheme. Then we have a natural isomorphism

(5.2)

\Lie\left(\Res_{\tilde{X}/X}\mathcal{H}\right)\simeq\Res_{\tilde{X}/X}\Lie(\mathcal{H}).

When
p
is also Galois with Galois group
\Gamma
, under tameness assumptions
(
see Section
1.1

)
, we have a natural isomorphism

(5.3)

\Lie\left((\Res_{\tilde{X}/X}\mathcal{H})^{\Gamma}\right)\simeq\left(\Res_{\tilde{X}/X}\Lie(\mathcal{H})\right)^{\Gamma}.

Proof.

See
[
CGP15
, Section A.7, p. 533]
and
[
Edi92
, Proposition 3.1]
, respectively.
∎

Notation 5.2
.

Following
[
BLR90
]
, we will denote the Weil restriction of scalars as
p_{*}
. The functor which composes with taking group invariants will be denoted by
p^{\Gamma}_{*}
. This is often called the “invariant direct image” functor following
[
BS15
]
. The conclusion of Lemma
5.1
can be expressed as

(5.4)

\Lie\left(p^{\Gamma}_{*}(\mathcal{H})\right)\simeq p^{\Gamma}_{*}(\Lie(\mathcal{H})).

5.2. Some constructions from SGA

We recall briefly some facts about vector group schemes from
[
SGA3
]
. For a coherent
S
-module
\mathcal{F}
, we recall, see
[
SGA3
, Exposé I, Section 4.6.3 and Proposition 4.6.5]

(see also
[
BT84a
, Section 1.4.1]
), the functor from the category of
S
-schemes to Abelian groups given by

(5.5)

\displaystyle W\colon S^{\prime}\longmapsto\Gamma(S^{\prime},\mathcal{F}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{S}}\mathcal{O}_{S^{\prime}}).

When
\mathcal{F}
is locally free, this is represented by a smooth group scheme
W(\mathcal{F})
defined by the affine scheme
\Sym(\mathcal{F}^{\vee})
. Its Lie algebra is canonically isomorphic to
\mathcal{F}
. In fact, there is a canonical isomorphism

(5.6)

\displaystyle W(\mathcal{F})\simeq W(\Lie(W(\mathcal{F}))).

Following
[
SGA3
]
, we will call
W(\mathcal{F})
a
vector group scheme
, noting that

(5.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\longmapsto W(\mathcal{F})

gives a functor from the category of locally free
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-modules of finite rank to the category of vector
S
-groups. Observe that over a field, a vector group is simply
\mathbb G_{a}^{n}
for some
n
. We now state a few important properties satisfied by the functor
W
:

(a)

If
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
is a locally free
S
-submodule of
\mathcal{F}
, then
W(\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
is a closed subgroup scheme of
W(\mathcal{F})
if and only if
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
is a direct summand of
\mathcal{F}
(see
[
DG70
, Section 2.1, p. 148]
and
[
BT84a
, Section 1.4.1]
).

(b)

In particular, if
S=\spec(\Lambda)
with fractional field
\tt F
, a projective
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-module
\mathcal{L}
of rank
1
corresponds to an
S
-group scheme
W(\mathcal{L})
whose generic fibre is isomorphic to the additive group
\mathbb G_{a,\tt F}
.

(c)

It is easy to check that Weil restriction and taking invariants commute with the functor
W
.

5.3. Restriction of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}
to formal neighbourhoods of generic points of coordinate hyperplanes
\boldsymbol{H_{i}}

Let
C_{i}
be the coordinate curve defined by the vanishing of
\lambda_{j}
(see Section
4.2
) for
j\neq i
. Let
U_{i}
be the formal neighbourhood of the origin in
C_{i}
. For
1\leq i\leq n
, let
\theta_{i}
be the point in the apartment corresponding to the hyperplane
H_{i}
.

For each
\theta_{i}
, let
d_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
d\theta_{i}\in Y(T)
. We first suppose that
\theta_{i}
lies in the fundamental domain of
Y(T)
in
\mathcal{A}_{T}
. Recall (see Section
A.2
) that by
[
BS15
, Proposition 5.1.2]
, there exists a ramified cover
q\colon U^{\prime}_{i}\to U_{i}
of ramification index
d_{i}
and Galois group
\Gamma
, together with a
\Gamma
-equivariant
G
-torsor
E_{i}
such that the adjoint group scheme
\mathcal{H}_{i}=E_{i}(G)
has simply connected fibres isomorphic to
G
and we have the
canonical
identification of
U
-group schemes

(5.8)

\displaystyle q_{*}^{\Gamma}(\mathcal{H}_{i})\simeq\mathfrak{G}_{\theta_{i}}.

For the general case, move the points
\theta_{{\tt i}}
into the fundamental domain for the action of
Y(T)
on
\mathcal{A}_{T}
by elements
h_{i}\in Y(T)
. These
h_{i}\in Y(T)
are uniquely determined. By an abuse of notation, we may view them as
h_{i}\in T(K)
as well. We now consider the modified embedding of
G
in the generic fibre of
\mathfrak{G}_{h_{i}.\theta_{i}}
given by conjugation by
h_{i}
. Then the
schematic closure
via this embedding in
\mathfrak{G}_{h_{i}.\theta_{i}}
gives the group scheme
\mathfrak{G}_{\theta_{i}}
. Now (
5.8
) generalises without any restriction on
\theta_{i}
. Henceforth in this paper, we fix the identification of
U
-group schemes thus obtained:

(5.9)

\displaystyle\phi(h_{i})\colon q_{*}^{\Gamma}(\mathcal{H}_{i})\simeq\mathfrak{G}_{\theta_{i}}.

We therefore get the following useful corollary to Theorem
4.4
.

Corollary 5.3
.

As sheaves of Lie algebras, we have an isomorphism

(5.10)

Lie\big(\phi(h_{i})\big)\colon q^{\Gamma}_{*}(E_{i}(\mathfrak g))\simeq\mathcal{R}|_{U_{i}}.

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of Theorem
4.4
and Lemma
5.1
. ∎

5.4. Group schemes on smooth varieties with normal crossing divisors

We now prove a result on constructing group schemes on varieties with normal crossing divisors under some general hypotheses. This plays a role in this paper as a useful substitute for the Artin–Weil theorem on the existence of group scheme structures stemming from birational group laws. Let
\boldsymbol{\Theta}:=(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{n})
be an
n
-tuple of rational points in the affine apartment
\mathcal{A}_{T}
satisfying the conditions of Section
2.2
. Let
k
be a
perfect field
with characteristic assumptions as in Section
2.2
.

Theorem 5.4
.

Let
X
be a smooth quasi-projective scheme over
k
, and let
D=\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}D_{i}

be a reduced normal crossing divisor. Let
\{\zeta_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
be the set of generic points of the components
\{D_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
, and let
A_{i}=\mathcal{O}_{X,\zeta_{i}}
,
X_{i}:=\spec(\hat{A}_{i})
and
X_{o}:=X-D
. Let
\mathfrak{G}_{\theta_{i}}
be the
parahoric
group scheme on
X_{i}
for each
i
associated to the point
\theta_{i}
of the affine apartment. Let
\mathcal{R}
denote a Lie algebra bundle on
X
which is such that
\mathcal{R}|_{X_{o}}\simeq\mathfrak{g}\times X_{o}
and
\mathcal{R}|_{X_{i}}\simeq\Lie(\mathfrak{G}_{\theta_{i}})
for each
i
. Then there is a smooth, affine group scheme
\mathfrak{G}
on
X
with connected fibres, unique up to isomorphism, such that

(1)

\mathfrak{G}|_{X_{o}}\simeq G\times X_{o}
,

(2)

\mathfrak{G}|_{X_{i}}\simeq\mathfrak{G}_{\theta_{i}}
for each
i
,

(3)

\Lie(\mathfrak{G})\simeq\mathcal{R}
.

Proof.

Let
L_{i}:=\Fract(A_{i})
(resp.
\hat{L}_{i}:=\Fract(\hat{A}_{i})
) for each
i
. By (
5.9
), for each
i
, there exists a
\Gamma_{i}
-equivariant
G
-torsor
E_{i}
on a ramified cover
q_{i}\colon\tilde{X}_{i}\to X_{i}
such that the adjoint group scheme
\mathcal{H}_{i}=E_{i}(G)
has simply connected fibres isomorphic to
G
and we have the identification of
A_{i}
-group schemes

(5.11)

\displaystyle\phi(h_{i})\colon q_{i,*}^{\Gamma}(\mathcal{H}_{i})\simeq\mathfrak{G}_{\theta_{i}}.

Furthermore, by Corollary
5.3
, we also get the identifications

(5.12)

\displaystyle\Lie\big(\phi(h_{i})\big)\colon q_{i,*}^{\Gamma}(E_{i}(\mathfrak g))\simeq\Lie(\mathfrak{G}_{\theta_{i}}).

Let
\mathfrak{G}_{o}:=G\times X_{o}
.
Let
X^{\prime}\subset X
be an open subset containing
X_{o}
whose complement in
X
has codimension at least
2
. We restrict the Lie algebra bundle
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}_{X}
further to
X^{\prime}
. By assumption, over the open subset
{X_{o}}\subset X^{\prime}
, we have
\mathcal{R}\simeq{X_{o}}\times\mathfrak{g}
. Observe that
\Aut(\mathfrak{g})=\Aut(G)
since
G
is simply connected. Further, since the local parahoric group schemes
\mathfrak{G}_{\theta_{i}}
are assumed to be
generically split
, it follows that for each
i
, the transition functions of
\mathcal{R}
give an isomorphism

\tau_{i}\colon\mathfrak{G}_{o}\times_{X_{o}}\hat{L}_{i}\simeq\mathfrak{G}_{\theta_{i}}\times_{X_{i}}\hat{L}_{i}.

We can now apply
[
BLR90
, Section 6.2, Proposition D.4(b)]
to each
\tau_{i}\colon\mathfrak{G}_{o}\times_{X_{o}}L_{i}\times_{L_{i}}\hat{L}_{i}\simeq\mathfrak{G}_{\theta_{i}}\times_{X_{i}}\hat{L}_{i}
to obtain by “descent” an affine, finite-type group scheme
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}_{\theta_{i}}
over
\spec(A_{i})
for each i, such that
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}_{\theta_{i}}\times_{A_{i}}\hat{A}_{i}\simeq\mathfrak{G}_{\theta_{i}}
and
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}_{\theta_{i}}\times_{A_{i}}L_{i}\simeq\mathfrak{G}_{o}\times_{X_{o}}L_{i}
.
By an abuse of notation, we will denote the group scheme
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}_{\theta_{i}}
simply by
\mathfrak{G}_{\theta_{i}}
.

Since the group schemes
\mathfrak{G}_{\theta_{i}}
on
\spec(A_{i})
are of finite type, they can be extended (by clearing denominators) to open subschemes
X_{f_{i}}
of
X
. By a further shrinking of this neighbourhood
X_{f_{i}}
of
\zeta_{i}
, one can glue it to
\mathfrak{G}_{o}
along the intersection. Since the group schemes are smooth and affine,
we obtain the connected, smooth, affine group scheme

(5.13)

\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}\longrightarrow X^{\prime}.

Transporting structures by Corollary
5.3
(see also the appendix), the Lie algebra bundle
\mathcal{R}
gets canonical parabolic structures at the generic points
\zeta_{i}
of the components
\{D_{i}\}_{i}
.
By Definition
A.1
, for a (generic) parabolic vector bundle with prescribed rational weights such as
\mathcal{R}
, we firstly get a global Kawamata cover (see Section
A.1
)
p\colon Z\rightarrow{X}
ramified over
D
with ramification prescribed by the weights
\{d_{i}\}
, see (
2.2
), with Galois group
\Gamma
which “realizes the local ramified covers
q_{i}
at the points
\zeta_{i}
”;
i.e.
the isotropy subgroup of
\Gamma
at
\zeta_{i}
is
\Gamma_{i}
.

We
claim
that we in fact get an
\Gamma
-equivariant vector bundle
V
on
Z
such that

(5.14)

p_{*}^{\Gamma}(V)\simeq\mathcal{R}.

Let
Z^{\prime}:=p^{-1}(X^{\prime})
. As the first step, following the reasoning in the appendix, it follows by the dimension
1
strategy that there exists a
\Gamma
-equivariant vector bundle
V^{\prime}
on
Z^{\prime}
such that

p_{*}^{\Gamma}(V^{\prime})\simeq\mathcal{R}\mid_{X^{\prime}}
.

Furthermore, in formal neighbourhoods over the generic points
\zeta_{i}
of the components
D_{i}
, we may identify
V^{\prime}
with
E_{i}(\mathfrak g)
and
\Gamma
with
\Gamma_{i}
. Also, over
Z_{o}:=p^{-1}(X_{o})
we can identify
V^{\prime}
with
\mathfrak g\times Z_{o}
. The transition functions of
\mathcal{R}|_{X^{\prime}}
can be lifted to give transition functions for
V^{\prime}
, and we get a canonical
\Gamma
-equivariant Lie algebra bundle structure on
V^{\prime}
over
Z^{\prime}
(with fibre type
\mathfrak g
) such that

p_{*}^{\Gamma}(V^{\prime})\simeq\mathcal{R}\mid_{X^{\prime}}

as Lie algebra bundles.

Since the weights
\theta_{i}
are all chosen to lie in the same affine apartment
\mathcal{A}_{T}
, the local representations for the stabilizers
\Gamma_{i}\subset\Gamma
in
G
take values in the maximal torus
T
. This determines a uniform Cartan decomposition on the fibres of the Lie algebra bundle
V^{\prime}
on
Z^{\prime}
(compare with the proof of Theorem
4.4
, where one has similar arguments to check that
\mathcal{R}
is locally free on
{\bf A}
). This gives a decomposition of the underlying vector bundle
V^{\prime}
as a direct sum of line bundles.

Now since invertible sheaves extend as invertible sheaves across codimension at least
2
, see Lemma
4.3
, and since
\codim(Z\setminus Z^{\prime})\geq 2
, the reflexivity of the double dual
V
of
V^{\prime}
implies that this direct sum decomposition of
V^{\prime}
extends term by term to
Z
. Hence the double dual
V
is locally free. The equivariant structure on
V^{\prime}
canonically gives an equivariant structure on
V
, and it is easy to check that (
5.14
) holds. This completes the proof of the claim.

The structure of Lie algebra bundle on
V^{\prime}
is such that the Killing form is non-degenerate everywhere on
Z^{\prime}
by virtue of our assumptions; see Section
2.2
(
cf.

[
Jan98
, Section 6.4, p. 199]
). By a Hartogs-type argument (see for example
[
EGAIV
, Theorem 5.10.5]
or
[
Har67
, Theorem 3.8]
), the Lie bracket
[,]
on
V^{\prime}
extends to a Lie bracket on the locally free sheaf
V
with the Killing form being non-degenerate on the whole of
Z
.
In other words,
V
is now a locally free sheaf of Lie algebras on the whole of
Z
with semisimple fibres; these fibres are isomorphic to the Lie algebra
\mathfrak g
by the rigidity of semisimple Lie algebras
. We denote this bundle of Lie algebras by
V_{\mathfrak g}
.

By construction,
V^{\prime}
has the structure of an equivariant Lie algebra bundle. The
[,]
on
V_{\mathfrak g}
is a section of
V_{\mathfrak g}\otimes V^{*}_{\mathfrak g}\otimes V^{*}_{\mathfrak g}
. For each
g\in\Gamma
, the sections
g\cdot[,]
and
[,]
agree on
Z^{\prime}
. Hence they agree everywhere on
Z
;
i.e.
the underlying equivariant structure on
V_{\mathfrak g}
gives
V_{\mathfrak g}
the structure of an
equivariant Lie algebra bundle
. The transition functions of this equivariant Lie algebra bundle lie in
\Aut(\mathfrak g)=\Aut(G)
. Hence we obtain an equivariant
\Aut(G)
-torsor
\mathcal{E}
on
Z
. Note that
\mathcal{E}
is only étale locally trivial. Also observe that the associated Lie algebra bundle
\mathcal{E}(\mathfrak g):=\mathcal{E}\times^{\Aut(G)}\mathfrak g
is isomorphic to
V_{\mathfrak g}
as a Lie algebra bundle.

Let
\mathcal{H}:=\mathcal{E}\times^{\Aut(G)}G
denote the associated fibre space for the canonical action of
\Aut(G)
on
G
. Since the group
\Aut(G)
acts on
G
as group automorphisms, it preserves the group structure, and hence
\mathcal{H}
is a group scheme. Further, since
\mathcal{E}
is étale locally trivial, by étale descent, it follows that
\mathcal{H}
is an
affine
smooth group scheme on
Z
. Since
\mathcal{E}
is an equivariant
\Aut(G)
-torsor, the group scheme
\mathcal{H}
is furthermore an equivariant group scheme on
Z
for the action of the Galois group
\Gamma
, and so we define

(5.15)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak G}_{X}:=p_{*}^{\Gamma}(\mathcal{H}).

By the properties of Weil restriction of scalars and taking invariants, it follows that
{\mathfrak G}_{X}
is a
smooth, affine group scheme
.
Also,
{\mathfrak G}_{X}|_{X^{\prime}}=\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
.

Note that we have the identification
\Lie(\mathcal{H})\simeq V_{\mathfrak g}
as Lie algebra bundles on
Z
. Since by Lemma
5.1
, the “invariant direct image” functor commutes with taking Lie algebras, we moreover get isomorphisms of locally free sheaves of Lie algebras

(5.16)

\Lie({\mathfrak G}_{X})\simeq\Lie\left(p_{*}^{\Gamma}(\mathcal{H})\right)\simeq p_{*}^{\Gamma}\left(\Lie(\mathcal{H})\right)\simeq p_{*}^{\Gamma}\left(V_{\mathfrak g}\right)\simeq\mathcal{R}.

This proves the third claim in the theorem.
∎

5.5. The “big cell” structure on
\mathfrak{G}

Recall that the choice of the set of positive roots
\Phi^{+}
determines a
big cell
of
G
over
k
.

By (
5.13
), the group scheme
\mathfrak{G}_{X\textasciiacute}
is obtained by gluing the parahoric group schemes
\mathfrak{G}_{\theta_{i}}
with
G\times X_{o}
using the identity map
\id_{G}
. Therefore, the parahoric group schemes
\mathfrak{G}_{\theta_{i}}
come with a canonical
big cell
which glues with the standard big cell of the split group scheme
G\times X_{o}
. This gives a big cell
\mathfrak B_{X\textasciiacute}=\mathfrak U_{X\textasciiacute}^{+}\times\mathcal{T}_{X\textasciiacute}\times\mathfrak U_{X\textasciiacute}^{-}\subset\mathfrak{G}_{X\textasciiacute}
. This also gives the decomposition

(5.17)

\displaystyle\Lie\left(\mathfrak{G}_{X\textasciiacute}\right)=\Lie\left(\mathfrak U_{X\textasciiacute}^{+}\right)\oplus\Lie\left(\mathcal{T}_{X\textasciiacute}\right)\oplus\Lie\left(\mathfrak U_{X\textasciiacute}^{-}\right).

In the next proposition, we show the existence of an open subscheme
\mathfrak B_{{\Phi}}
of
\mathfrak{G}
obtained in Theorem
5.4
. This open subscheme is analogous to the
big cell in a split reductive group scheme
; see
[
SGA3
, Exposé XXII, Section 4.1]
. Following
[
BT84a
]
, we will call this open subscheme a
“big cell”
of
\mathfrak{G}
. The
big cell
in particular provides a neighbourhood of the identity section of the group scheme
\mathfrak{G}
.

Proposition 5.5
.

We work in the setting of Theorem
5.4
and choose the transition function for the gluing to be
\id_{G}\in\Aut(G)
. Let
\Phi^{+}
be a system of positive roots in
\Phi
. Let
\mathfrak B_{\mathcal{H}}
denote the big cell of
\mathcal{H}
. Let

\mathfrak B_{\Phi}:=p_{*}^{\Gamma}\left(\mathfrak B_{\mathcal{H}}\right)

denote the invariant direct image of
\mathfrak B_{\mathcal{H}}
.

Then
\mathfrak B_{\Phi}
restricts to the standard
big cell
of
G
over the open subset
X_{o}
. In fact, there exist unipotent subgroup schemes
\mathfrak U^{\pm}
and a toral subgroup scheme
\mathcal{T}
of
\mathfrak{G}
such that the morphism

(5.18)

\mathfrak U^{-}\times\mathcal{T}\times\mathfrak U^{+}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{G}

to
\mathfrak{G}

induced by multiplication
is an open immersion with image
\mathfrak B_{\Phi}
.

Proof.

The notation is as in the proof of Theorem
5.4
. Since the Galois action of
\Gamma
is via the fixed maximal torus
T
, we observe that the Cartan decomposition of the Lie algebra bundle is preserved under the functor that takes
\Gamma
-invariants. Notice that the functor
W
of Section
5.2
commutes with Weil restriction and taking invariants. Hence the root group schemes and the toral subgroup scheme of the reductive group scheme
\mathcal{H}
can be pushed down by
p_{*}^{\Gamma}
to give the corresponding root group schemes and toral group scheme below. The group structure on
\mathcal{H}
induces one on the product of the root groups for the positive and negative roots (with a fixed and prescribed order) to give closed unipotent subgroup schemes on
\mathcal{H}
. These again carry the action of the Galois group
\Gamma
.

Hence these unipotent subgroup schemes get pushed down by
p_{*}^{\Gamma}
to give closed subgroup schemes
\mathfrak U^{\pm}
of
\mathfrak{G}
. Further, Weil restriction and taking invariants preserve the product structure, and this shows that the
big cell
structure on the reductive group scheme
\mathcal{H}
goes down by Weil restriction and invariants to give the required
big cell
structure on
\mathfrak{G}
. ∎

5.6. Bruhat–Tits group scheme associated to concave functions

Let
\tilde{\Phi}:=\Phi\cup\{0\}
.
Recall that a function
f\colon\tilde{\Phi}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a concave function such that
f(0)=0
; see (
1.5
).
We recall a few salient features of the main results from
[
BT84a
, Section 4.6.2]
and
[
Yu15
, Theorem 0.1]
in our special setting, where
G_{K}
is a generically split, simple, simply connected group over
K
,
i.e.

G_{K}\simeq G\times\spec(K)
.

Bruhat–Tits
[
BT84a
, Section 4.6.2]
: Let
\mathcal{P}_{f}
be the subgroup of
G(K)
generated by
U_{r,f(r)}
and the subgroup
T(A)
.

There exists a canonical affine smooth group scheme
\mathfrak G_{f}
on
\spec({\mathcal{O}})
with generic fibre
G_{K}
such that

(1)

\mathfrak G_{f}(A)=\mathcal{P}_{f}
,

(2)

the multiplication morphism

(5.19)

\left(\prodop\displaylimits_{r\in\Phi^{-}}{\mathfrak U}_{r,f(r)}\right)\times\mathcal{T}_{f,\mathcal{O}}\times\left(\prodop\displaylimits_{r\in\Phi^{+}}{\mathfrak U}_{r,f(r)}\right)\longrightarrow\mathfrak G_{f}

is an open immersion. The group scheme
\mathcal{T}_{f,\mathcal{O}}
is the schematic closure of
T_{K}
in
\mathfrak G_{f}
, and since
T_{K}
is a split torus,
\mathcal{T}_{f,\mathcal{O}}
is a connected multiplicative group scheme; see
[
BLR90
, Section 10.1, Example 5]
. By computing the tangent space at the identity section which lies in the image of the immersion,
i.e.
, in the
big cell

\mathfrak B_{f}
of
\mathfrak G_{f}
, we see that

(5.20)

\Lie\left(\mathfrak G_{f}\right)=\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{r\in\Phi}\Lie\left({\mathfrak U}_{r,f(r)}\right)\oplus\Lie\left(\mathcal{T}_{f,\mathcal{O}}\right).

5.7. On the Lie algebra bundle
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}
in Theorem
5.4

We now prove a small but significant variant of Theorem
5.4
. In the hypothesis in Theorem
5.4
, we have assumed the existence of an
n-parahoric
Lie algebra bundle
\mathcal{R}
on
X
. We will give sufficient general conditions for the existence of a Lie algebra bundle alone.

Proposition 5.6
.

Let the notation be as in Theorem
5.4
. Let
{\bf f}=\{f_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
be a collection of
n
-concave functions. Let
\hat{A}_{i}
be the completion of
A_{i}
at the maximal ideal. Suppose that we are given an assignment of
BT
-group schemes
\mathfrak{G}_{f_{j}}
on
X_{j}:=\spec(\hat{A}_{j})
for each
j
. Then there exists a Lie algebra bundle
\mathcal{R}
on
(X,D)
such that
\mathcal{R}|_{X_{o}}\simeq\mathfrak{g}\times X_{o}
, the Cartan decomposition on
\mathfrak{g}
extends to
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{R}|_{X_{j}}\simeq\Lie(\mathfrak{G}_{f_{j}})
for each
j
.

Proof.

The notation is as in Theorem
5.4
and its proof. But unlike in Theorem
5.4
, we do not begin with a Lie algebra bundle. So we are free to choose the initial gluing data according to our requirements. Since the
BT
-group schemes for us are always connected, smooth and generically split, we can choose (as in Proposition
5.5
)
\id_{G}
as the map for gluing the product group scheme
\mathfrak{G}_{o}=G\times X_{o}
with the
\mathfrak{G}_{f_{i}}
.

Exactly as in the proof of Theorem
5.4
, by a descent argument, we get smooth, affine group schemes
\mathfrak{G}_{f_{i}}
, now on
\spec({A}_{i})
for each
i
, and hence we get a smooth, affine group scheme
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
on
X^{\prime}
.
We now define

(5.21)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{\prime}:=\Lie\left(\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}\right),

which is a Lie algebra bundle on
X^{\prime}\subset X
.

Since the gluing functions are
\id_{G}
, exactly as in the proof of Proposition
5.5
, we also have an extended
big cell
structure on the group scheme
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
over the whole of
X^{\prime}
. As in (
5.17
), we see that the Cartan decomposition extends to give one for
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
. In particular, this gives a direct sum decomposition of
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
in terms of line bundles.

Now define
\mathcal{R}
to be the reflexive closure of
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
; see Lemma
4.2
. Since we are on an irreducible smooth scheme
X
, by Lemma
4.3
, reflexive closures of invertible sheaves remain invertible sheaves. Thus
\mathcal{R}
is also a locally free sheaf on
X
. The Lie bracket on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
now extends by a Hartogs-type argument, and we get a Lie algebra bundle structure on
\mathcal{R}
with the desired properties.
∎

5.8. Construction of the
n
-parahoric group scheme

The notation in this section is as in the previous one; namely,
\bf A\simeq\mathbb A^{n}
and
{\bf A_{0}}\subset{\bf A}
is the complement of the coordinate hyperplanes. Recall that
\mathcal{O}_{n}=k\llbracket z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}\rrbracket
and
K_{n}=\Fract(\mathcal{O}_{n})
.

Let
\{H_{i}\}
be the hyperplanes of
{\bf A}
. For each
i
, let
\zeta_{i}
denote the generic point of the divisor
H_{i}
. Let

(5.22)

A_{i}=\mathcal{O}_{{\bf A},\zeta_{i}}

be the DVR obtained by localizing at the height
1
primes given by
\zeta_{i}
, and let
X_{i}:=\spec(A_{i})
. Base changing by the local morphism
X_{i}\to\bf A
, we have a Lie algebra bundle
\mathcal{R}|_{X_{i}}
for each
i
. Moreover, the Lie algebra bundle
\mathcal{R}
on
{\bf A}
, see Theorem
4.4
, gives canonical gluing data to glue
\mathcal{R}|_{X_{i}}
with the trivial bundle
\mathfrak g\times\bf A_{0}
.

The following first case is the key to proving the main result, Theorem
1.3
.

Theorem 5.7
.

The main theorem, Theorem
1.3
, holds when the
n
-tuple of concave functions is of
type I

(
see Definition
1.2

)
.

Proof.

We begin by making constructions on
{\bf A}
. The existence of the Lie algebra bundle
\mathcal{R}
on
{\bf A}
follows by applying Proposition
5.6
to the
n-concave
function of
type I
defined by the point
\boldsymbol{\Theta}=(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{n})
. Since we are in the
type I
case, we could have obtained the Lie algebra bundle directly from Theorem
4.4
as well. Again since we are in
type I
, the existence of the smooth
affine
group scheme
\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
with connected fibres is a consequence of Theorem
5.4
; its cell structure and properties (
4
) and (
5
) of Theorem
1.3
follow from Proposition
5.5
.

We now restrict the group scheme
\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
to
\spec(\mathcal{O}_{n})\subset{\bf A}
and continue to denote the restricted group scheme by
\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
. Since the base is smooth and the group scheme is also smooth, by a theorem of Weil, see
[
BLR90
, Section 4.4, Theorem 1]
, it follows that the global sections are determined on any open subset
U
whose complement is of codimension at least
2
. Let us call such an open subset
a big open subset
. Sections over
U
are obtained by intersecting the sections of the parahoric groups defined at the height
1
primes in
G(K_{n})
. It follows immediately that the sections over the big open subset
U
coincide with the
n
-parahoric group
\tt{P}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
, proving assertion (
2
) of Theorem
1.3
.

We now discuss
the uniqueness of the group scheme
; we note that although we do this here in the setting of type I, the arguments are identical and will imply the same for the most general type III case, once the construction along with properties (
4
) and (
5
) of Theorem
1.3
is carried out in Section
7
.

If
\mathfrak H
is a connected, affine smooth group scheme over
\mathcal{O}_{n}
which is generically split and such that it has closed toral and root subgroup schemes as in (
4
) and a
big cell
as in (
5
) of Theorem
1.3
, then by
[
BT84a
, Sections 1.2.13 and 1.2.14]
, we get the desired isomorphism of
\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
and
\mathfrak H
, showing the uniqueness.

A subtler feature regarding the uniqueness can be extracted from these arguments. Once the smooth connected group scheme
\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
is constructed abstractly, the big cell structure over the big open subset
U
(which contains all points of height
1
) can be obtained at each of the height
1
primes as in
[
BT84a
]
. So the cell structure coincides with the
big cell
of the
parahoric
group schemes
\mathfrak{G}_{\theta_{j}}
for each
j
, and hence by the uniqueness of the group schemes over discrete valuation rings, see
[
BT84a
, Section 3.8.3]
, we get the identification of the group schemes
\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
and
\mathfrak H
over
U
. Now, we can appeal to the deeper results in
[
Ray70
, Corollaire IX.1.5]
to extend the isomorphism of group schemes from
U
to
\spec(\mathcal{O}_{n})
.
∎

Remark 5.8
.

In continuation of Proposition
5.6
, the description of the diagonal restriction of
\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
can be obtained directly from the facts that it is affine by Theorem
5.7
and the group
\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}(\mathcal{O}_{n})
of
\mathcal{O}_{n}
-valued points is the
n-parahoric
group
{\tt{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
. Let the notation be as in Theorem
1.5
. Let
\Delta:=\mathbb A_{\tt I}
for
I=\{1,\ldots,n\}
be the diagonal curve in the affine space and
\mathcal{O}
be the ring of functions on
\Delta
at the origin. Hence the restriction
\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\mid_{\Delta}
is such that the space of its sections
\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\mid_{\Delta}(\mathcal{O})
is identified with
\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}(\mathcal{O}_{n})
, in which we set all variables as equal. Thus we get the subgroup
{\tt{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}^{\diag}\subset G(k(\!(t)\!))
; see (
3.11
). By the étoffé property of
BT
-group schemes, this forces that
\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\mid_{\Delta}
is the
BT
-group scheme given by the concave function
\text{\linespread m}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
, where
\text{\linespread m}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}(r):=\sumop\displaylimits_{i}m_{r}(\theta_{i})
. We remark that the above discussion still holds if we work with subdiagonal curves
\mathbb A_{\tt I}
as Theorem
1.5
(
b
), which are defined by any subcollection of the hyperplanes indexed by a non-empty
{\tt I}\subset\{1,\ldots,n\}
.

Corollary 5.9
.

Consider an
n
-tuple
{\bf f}
of concave functions, each of which is a sum of such functions of
type I
. Then the group scheme
\mathfrak{G}_{\bf f}
is
affine
.

Proof.

Let
{\bf f}=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n})
, where
f_{i}=\sumop\displaylimits_{j}f_{\theta_{ij}}
. Set
\boldsymbol{\Theta}=(\theta_{ij})
. Then
\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
is affine by Theorem
5.7
. Note that this group scheme constructed out of
N
-parahoric group schemes is over an affine space of dimension
N
, for
N\gg 0
, and obtained from the data of the
\{\theta_{ij}\}
.

Observe that the group scheme
\mathfrak{G}_{\bf f}
can be seen as a restriction to a subdiagonal in the larger affine space of
\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
. Now the assertion of affineness follows by Remark
5.8
.
∎

5.9. Revisiting two «contre-exemples » in Bruhat–Tits
[
BT84a
]

In this subsection we take a closer look at a couple of counter-examples in Bruhat–Tits
[
BT84a
, Section 3.2.15]
. The examples highlight how the procedure developed in Section 3 of
[
BT84a
]
of taking schematic closures in certain linear groups could give schemes which are neither
flat
nor
group schemes
. This phenomenon happens when the base scheme is
\mathbb A^{2}
. By discarding certain bad components in the fibre over
o\in\mathbb A^{2}
, they extract a
2BT
-group scheme whose
affineness
is left in doubt.

The base ring is
\mathcal{O}=k[\xi,\eta]
, the group is
G=\SL_{2}
, the concave function
f
is defined by
f(a)=\xi\cdot\mathcal{O}
and
f(-a)=\eta\cdot\mathcal{O}
, and the representation is
\rho=\id\times\Ad
on the module
K^{2}\times\mathfrak g
. The
\mathcal{O}
-module
M
is a free module generated by the basis of
K^{2}
and the elements
\eta\cdot e_{-a},h,\xi\cdot e_{a}
. We view them as
\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
-\eta&0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}1/2&0\\
0&-1/2\end{pmatrix}
and
\begin{pmatrix}0&\xi\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}
. Thus we have

(5.23)

\rho\begin{pmatrix}x&y\\
z&t\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}x&y\\
z&t\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}t^{2}&-\eta\cdot zt&\xi\eta^{-1}\cdot z^{2}\\
-2\eta\cdot yt&xt+yz&-2\xi\cdot xz\\
\eta\cdot\xi^{-1}y^{2}&-\xi\cdot xy&x^{2}\end{pmatrix}\end{pmatrix}

The schematic closure of
\rho(G)
in
\mathfrak{GL}(M)
is
\mathfrak G
. The fibre over
o\in\mathbb A^{2}
is

(5.24)

\displaystyle\mathfrak G_{o}=\mathfrak G^{1}_{o}\cup\mathfrak G^{2}_{o},

where
\mathfrak G^{1}_{o}
has dimension
4
and
\mathfrak G^{2}_{o}
dimension
3
. Hence
\mathfrak G
is not flat over
\mathcal{O}
, while
\mathfrak H:=\mathfrak G\setminus\mathfrak G^{1}_{o}
is a
flat group scheme
, albeit in the words of
[
BT84a
, Section 3.2.15, p. 60]
«
mais probablement pas affine
»,
i.e.

but probably not affine
.

The fibre of
\mathfrak H
at
o
is obtained from
[
BT84a
, Section 3.2.14, Equation (2)]
, which is obtained by a diagonal restriction.

We now reinterpret this example as the simplest case of Theorem
5.7
, for
G=\SL_{2}
. The surprising picture which emerges from our approach is that we even get the
affineness
of their group scheme
\mathfrak H
as a consequence.

In our setting of Section
3.2
, we have
\Phi^{+}=\{a\}
, and in terms of the alcove vertices (
2.1
), we have
\theta_{1}:=\theta_{a}/2=\omega^{\vee}/2
and
\theta_{2}:=-\theta_{a}/2=-\omega^{\vee}/2
. With
m_{r}(\theta):=-\lfloor r(\theta)\rfloor
, we get

(5.25)

a(\theta_{1})=\begin{cases}1/2&\quad\text{if }a\in\Phi^{+},\\
-1/2&\quad{\text{i}f}a\in\Phi^{-},\end{cases}

(5.26)

m_{a}(\theta_{1})=\begin{cases}0&\quad\text{if }a\in\Phi^{+},\\
1&\quad{\text{i}f}a\in\Phi^{-},\end{cases}

and similarly for
\theta_{2}
. We get

(5.27)

a(\theta_{2})=\begin{cases}-1/2&\quad\text{if }a\in\Phi^{+},\\
1/2&\quad{\text{i}f}a\in\Phi^{-},\end{cases}

(5.28)

m_{a}(\theta_{2})=\begin{cases}1&\quad\text{if }a\in\Phi^{+},\\
0&\quad{\text{i}f}a\in\Phi^{-}.\end{cases}

By Theorem
5.7
, if
\boldsymbol{\theta}=(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})
, the group scheme
\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
is precisely
\mathfrak H
and also a
smooth, affine
group scheme over
\mathcal{O}
. Furthermore, the diagonal restriction is the affine group scheme
\mathfrak G_{\text{\linespread m}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}
given by the concave function

\text{\linespread m}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\colon r\mapsto\sumop\displaylimits m_{r}(\theta_{j})
with

(5.29)

\text{\linespread m}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}(a)=1\quad\forall a\in\Phi,

which computes
{\tt{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\diag}
and recovers the concave function considered in
[
BT84a
, Section 3.2.14]
.

6. Schematization of
n
-bounded groups
{\tt{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}

The notation in this section is as in the previous one; namely,
\bf A\simeq\mathbb A^{n}
and
{\bf A_{0}}\subset{\bf A}
is the complement of the coordinate hyperplanes.
In this entire section, we work with
G
being one of
A_{n}
,
G_{2}
,
F_{4}
or
E_{6}
.

We begin by remarking that in the foundational paper
[
BT72
]
, Bruhat and Tits study bounded groups associated to concave functions on the
\Phi\cup\{0\}
. In the sequel
[
BT84a
]
, the main result is to prove that these groups are
schematic
.

A class of concave functions, which play a basic role even in the general study, are the ones which come associated to bounded subsets
\Omega
of the affine apartment. In this section, we extend the above theory of
n
-parahoric groups (with their schematic properties) to the bounded groups associated to products of bounded regions
\boldsymbol{\Omega}:=({}_{1},\ldots,{}_{n})
in the apartment.

In the following
key
remark, we recall a few basic facts from
[
BT84b
]
.

Remark 6.1
.

Let
G=\SL(n)
for some
n
. Let
\Omega
be a non-empty
enclosed
bounded subset of the apartment
\mathcal{A}
. It is the convex hull of a finite subset
\{\theta_{\tt 1},\ldots,\theta_{\tt s}\}
of points in
\mathcal{A}
.
In terms of the points
\theta_{j}
, the
BT
-group scheme
\mathfrak{G}
on
\spec(\mathcal{O})
is precisely the
schematic closure
of the image of
G
in
\prodop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{\tt s}\mathfrak{G}_{\theta_{j}}
under the diagonal map
. This is a consequence of the discussion in
[
BT84b
, Remarques 3.9]
, together with
[
BT84b
, Section 5.3]
, where it is shown that the group scheme
\mathfrak{G}
over
\spec(\mathcal{O})
can be realized as the
closed image
in
\prodop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{\tt s}\mathfrak{G}_{\theta_{j}}
of the diagonal morphism of
G
in the generic fibre
G^{\tt s}
of
\prodop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{\tt s}\mathfrak{G}_{\theta_{j}}
.

We further remark that the stated result for
\SL(n)
also works by the results
[
GY03
, Theorem 10.1]
for
G_{2}
and
[
GY05
, Theorem 9.1]
for
F_{4}
and
E_{6}
.

Remark 6.2
.

An important aspect of the remark made above which will be used in what follows is to view the group scheme
\prodop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{\tt s}\mathfrak{G}_{\theta_{j}}
as an
invariant direct image
from a suitable ramified cover (see Section
A.2
). Now the group scheme
\mathfrak{G}_{\theta_{j}}
arises from an invariant direct image if
\theta_{j}
belongs to the alcove
\mathbf{a}_{0}
(see Section
2.1
) by
[
BS15
]
. This generalizes to the case when
\theta_{j}
belongs to the fundamental domain of
Y(T)
by Remark
8.1
and Section
9.1
. For the general case, we may have to move the points
\{\theta_{\tt 1},\ldots,\theta_{\tt s}\}
in apartment
\mathcal{A}
into the fundamental domain for the action of
Y(T)
. Let
h_{j}\in T(K)
be elements such that the point
\{h_{1}\cdot\theta_{\tt 1},\ldots,h_{\tt s}\cdot\theta_{\tt s}\}
now lies in the
\tt s
-fold product of the fundamental domain of
Y(T)
. We now consider the modified diagonal embedding of
G
in the generic fibre of
\prodop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{\tt s}\mathfrak{G}_{h_{j}\cdot\theta_{j}}
which is given by a simultaneous conjugation by the
h_{j}
,
i.e.

g\mapsto(h_{1}\cdot g\cdot h_{1}^{-1},\ldots,h_{\tt s}\cdot g\cdot h_{\tt s}^{-1})
. Then the
schematic closure
via this embedding in
\prodop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{\tt s}\mathfrak{G}_{h_{j}\cdot\theta_{j}}
gives the group scheme
\mathfrak{G}
up to an isomorphism. Moreover, the new ambient group scheme
\prodop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{\tt s}\mathfrak{G}_{h_{j}\cdot\theta_{j}}
comes as an invariant direct image from a single cover.

6.1. The schematic hull
\mathbb S_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}
of an
n
-bounded set
\boldsymbol{\Omega}
and its big cell structure

For simplicity of notation, let
\Lambda
stand for the
polynomial ring

k[z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}]
and
{\tt F}
stand for
\Fract(\Lambda)
throughout this proof
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\Omega}=({}_{1},\ldots,{}_{n})
, where we may assume that the
i
are
enclosed
bounded subsets of the affine apartment
\mathcal{A}
. Let
i
be the convex hull of points
\{\theta_{\tt i1},\ldots,\theta_{\tt is}\}
of
\mathcal{A}_{T}
. Note that
a priori
the number
s
of points also varies with
i
.
By Remarks
6.1
and
6.2
, we may suppose

(1)

that these points lie in a single fundamental domain of
Y(T)
in
\mathcal{A}_{T}
and

(2)

that over a DVR
\spec(\mathcal{O})
, we realize each
\mathfrak{G}_{{}_{i}}
as the schematic closure of
G
in
\prodop\displaylimits_{j=1}^{\tt s}\mathfrak{G}_{\theta_{\tt ij}}
.

By choosing
\tt s
to be the maximal number of terms for varying
i=1,\ldots,n
, and by repeating the
\theta_{\tt ij}
when the number is less than
\tt s
, we may assume that all the group schemes
\mathfrak{G}_{{}_{i}}
are schematic closures of products of
\tt s
parahoric group schemes on
\spec(\mathcal{O})
.

Fix a
j\in\{\tt 1,\ldots,\tt s\}
. For each hyperplane
H_{i}
(
i=1,\ldots,n
), we attach the point
\theta_{\tt ij}
. For this configuration of points in the apartment, we have by Theorem
5.7
an
n-parahoric
group scheme
\mathfrak{G}_{\theta_{\tt 1j},\ldots,\theta_{\tt nj}}
on
\bf A
. We set

(6.1)

\displaystyle\mathbb S_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}:=\prodop\displaylimits_{1\leq j\leq s}\mathfrak{G}_{\theta_{\tt 1j},\ldots,\theta_{\tt nj}}

to be the
s
-fold product of
n-parahoric
group schemes on
\bf A
. Thus
\mathbb S_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}
is a
smooth, affine group scheme
on
\spec(\Lambda)=\bf A
with generic fibre being the split group scheme
G^{\tt s}\times\spec({\tt F})
. Since each
n-parahoric
group scheme
\mathfrak{G}_{\theta_{\tt 1j},\ldots,\theta_{\tt nj}}
is realized as a Weil restriction of scalars from a finite flat cover of
\bf A
(see Theorem
5.7
), so is the group scheme
\mathbb S_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}
.

Definition 6.3
.

We call the product group scheme
\mathbb S_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}
, see (
6.1
), the
schematic hull
of the
n-bounded
set
\boldsymbol{\Omega}
and will denote it simply as
\mathbb S_{\Lambda}
.

This group scheme
\mathbb S_{\Lambda}
will play a central role in all that follows.

Recall that each of the
n-parahoric
group schemes
\mathfrak{G}_{\theta_{\tt 1j},\ldots,\theta_{\tt nj}}
occurring in (
6.1
) is equipped with a
big cell
by Theorem
5.7
(see Section
5.5
). Taking their product, we define the
big cell
of the group scheme
\mathbb S_{\Lambda}
. Let us write it as the image of the product morphism

(6.2)

\displaystyle\mathfrak B^{-}\times\mathcal{T}\times\mathfrak B^{+}\longrightarrow\mathbb S_{\Lambda}.

Let
\mathfrak{U}_{r,j}
denote the root group of
\mathfrak{G}_{\theta_{\tt 1j},\ldots,\theta_{\tt nj}}
corresponding to
r\in\Phi
. For
r\in\Phi
, let us set

(6.3)

\mathfrak{B}_{r}:=\prodop\displaylimits_{1\leq j\leq s}\mathfrak{U}_{r,j}.

Then under the induced group law from
\mathbb S_{\Lambda}
, each
\mathfrak{B}_{r}
is Abelian.

6.2. The diagonal embedding

Let
U
be an open subset of
\spec(\Lambda)
which contains all primes of height at most
1
. At the completion of the local rings at the height
1
prime ideal associated to the generic point of each
H_{i}
indexed by
i=1,\ldots,n
, we assign the Bruhat–Tits group scheme
\mathfrak{G}_{{}_{i}}
. By definition, these group schemes are also generically split.

We let
\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\tt F}}
denote the
image of the diagonal embedding
of
G\times\spec({\tt F})
in
G^{\tt s}\times\spec({\tt F})
. As in Proposition
5.6
and in the proof of Theorem
5.4
, by gluing (using the map
\id_{G}
) with the
\mathfrak{G}_{{}_{j}}
, the group scheme
\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\tt F}}
extends to each of the generic points of the coordinate hyperplanes. Let
\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},U}
denote this smooth, affine group scheme on
U
. We begin with the closed embedding
\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\tt F}}\subset\mathbb S_{\tt F}
, where
\mathbb S_{\tt F}=\mathbb S_{\Lambda}|_{\spec(\tt F)}
. Let

(6.4)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak Y}_{\Lambda}:=\overline{\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\tt F}}}

denote the
schematic closure
of
\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\tt F}}
in
\mathbb S_{\Lambda}
.

Lemma 6.4
.

The group scheme
\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},U}
is in fact the schematic closure
\mathfrak Y_{U}
of
\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\tt F}}
in
{\mathbb S}_{U}
.

Proof.

Since
U
contains all primes of height at most
1
, we see that the schematic closure
\mathfrak Y_{U}
of
\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\tt F}}
in
{\mathbb S}_{U}
is in fact a
flat
group scheme on
U
, and since
\mathbb S_{U}
is affine, it is in fact
affine
as well.

Since
\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\tt F}}
is smooth and hence reduced,
{\mathfrak Y}_{\Lambda}
is in fact the closure of
\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\tt F}}
in
\mathbb S_{\Lambda}
with its reduced scheme structure. Since the base is of dimension at least
2
, this schematic closure, however, need not be a group scheme (see
[
BT84a
, Section 3.2.15]
).

To prove the contention of the lemma, we need to verify that the restriction of
\mathfrak Y_{U}
to the completion of the local rings of each height
1
prime associated to
H_{i}
is in fact isomorphic to the
BT
-group scheme
\mathfrak{G}_{{}_{i}}
. This follows from Remarks
6.1
and
6.2
.
∎

6.3.
n
-bounded-groups and schematization

The schematization of the
n
-bounded analogue is now carried out in two steps. As in the construction of the
n
-parahoric, we construct the Lie algebra bundle
\mathcal{R}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}
first (see Theorem
4.4
). This will be eventually turn out to be
\Lie(\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega}})
.

Proposition 6.5
.

Let the notation be as in Theorem
4.4
and Proposition
5.6
. The trivial bundle with fibre
\mathfrak g
on
{\bf A_{0}}
extends to
{\bf A}
, preserving the Cartan decomposition as a canonical Lie algebra bundle
\mathcal{R}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}
. Further, for each
\zeta_{i}
, the restriction
\mathcal{R}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}|_{X_{i}}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{R}_{{}_{i}}
.

Proof.

This is special case of Proposition
5.6
. ∎

We will denote the Cartan decomposition of
\mathcal{R}
as follows:

(6.5)

\mathcal{R}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}=\Lie(T)\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\bf A}\oplus_{r\in\Phi}\mathcal{R}_{r}.

The group scheme construction on the higher-dimensional base has two obstructions. Firstly, schematic closures need not be flat when the base is not a Dedekind domain (see Section
5.9
), and secondly, there is no immediate analogue of Theorem
5.4
. This is because the bounded subsets
i
need not be singletons, and so the local BT-group schemes at the height
1
primes do not necessarily come from ramified covers.

Towards this end, we begin with a useful lemma.

Lemma 6.6

(
cf
.
[
BT84a
, Section 1.2, pp. 17–18]
)
.

Let
K
be an infinite field and
A\subset K
be a subring such that
K
is the quotient field of
A
. Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a morphism of schemes over
A
, and let
i\colon Z\hookrightarrow Y
be a closed subscheme. Suppose that
X
is
A
-flat and that
f_{K}\colon X_{K}\to Y_{K}
factorises through
Z_{K}
. Then
f
factorises through
Z
.

Proof.

By going to an affine cover of
Y
, we may assume that
X
and
Y
are
A
-affine with coordinate rings
\mathcal{O}[X]
and
\mathcal{O}[Y]
. Since
X
is
A
-flat, the map
j_{X}\colon\mathcal{O}[X]\to K\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}\mathcal{O}[X]
is
injective
. Let
a\in I(Z)
be an element in the ideal defining
Z
in
Y
. Then
j_{X}\circ f^{*}(a)=f_{K}^{*}\circ j_{Y}(a)=0
since
f_{K}
has a factorization. By the injectivity of
j_{X}
, we get
f^{*}(a)=0
. ∎

6.4. Continuation of proof of Theorem
1.3

Theorem 6.7
.

The main theorem, Theorem
1.3
, holds when the
n
-tuple of concave functions is of
type II

(
see Definition
1.2

)
and when
G
is of type
A_{n}
,
G_{2}
,
F_{4}
or
E_{6}

(
see Remark
6.1

)
.

Proof.

We will break up the rather long and technical proof into two parts.

Part (I)

constructs the candidate for
big cell
in the type II case.

Part (II)

then constructs the group scheme by using this cell structure.

In this sense, the key issue is the extension of the
schematic root datum
.

Part (
I
) of the proof
. We begin with the closed subgroup scheme
\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\tt F}}
of
\mathbb S_{\tt F}
(obtained by the diagonal embedding of
G
in
G^{s}
).

The closed subgroup scheme
\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\tt F}}\hookrightarrow\mathbb S_{\tt F}
over
\spec({\tt F})
is such that each factor of the product
\mathfrak U_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\tt F}}^{-}\times T_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\tt F}}\times\mathfrak U_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\tt F}}^{+}
embeds factor by factor in the corresponding term in the cell of
\mathbb S_{{\tt F}}
. The full cell
\mathfrak C_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\tt F}}:=\operatorname{Im}\big(\mathfrak U_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\tt F}}^{-}\times T_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\tt F}}\times\mathfrak U_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\tt F}}^{+}\to\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\tt F}}\big)
therefore sits as a flat closed subscheme of the open subscheme

(6.6)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Im}\left(\mathfrak B^{-}_{\tt F}\times\mathcal{T}_{\tt F}\times\mathfrak B^{+}_{\tt F}\longrightarrow\mathbb S_{\tt F}\right).

As before, let
U
be an open subset of
\spec(\Lambda)
which contains all primes of height at most
1
. By Proposition
6.5
, we have a locally free Lie algebra bundle
\mathcal{R}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}
on
\spec(\Lambda)={\bf A}
. This gives an extension of
\Lie(\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\tt F}})
as a Lie subalgebra bundle
\mathcal{R}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}
of
\Lie(\mathbb S_{\Lambda})
. Under the Cartan decomposition, we get the following refinement of subbundles:

(6.7)

\mathcal{R}_{r,\boldsymbol{\Omega}}\xhookrightarrow{\makebox[10.0pt]{}}\Lie\left(\mathfrak B_{r}\right).

In the proof, as a preliminary step we will construct a
big cell

\mathfrak C_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}
as a subscheme of the group scheme
\mathbb S_{\Lambda}
. This is achieved in (
6.14
) below. This scheme
\mathfrak C_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}
will be such that when restricted to the open subset
U
of
\spec(\Lambda)
, we have an open immersion

(6.8)

\displaystyle\mathfrak C_{U}\xhookrightarrow{\makebox[10.0pt]{}}\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},U}.

We recall that by (
6.4
) and the proof of Lemma
6.4
, the scheme
{\mathfrak Y}_{\Lambda}

is in fact the
schematic closure
of
\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},U}
in
\mathbb S_{\Lambda}
.
The main aim of this first part of the proof is not only to show the existence of a
big cell

\mathfrak C_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}
in (
6.14
), but to show in fact that Proposition
6.9
may be applied to it because it satisfies the following key property.

Claim 6.8
.

There is an open immersion from the scheme
\mathfrak C_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}
into
{\mathfrak Y}_{\Lambda}
.

We begin with the
big cell

\mathfrak C_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\tt F}}:=\operatorname{Im}\big(\mathfrak U_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\tt F}}^{+}\times T_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\tt F}}\times\mathfrak U_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\tt F}}^{-}\to\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\tt F}}\big)
. This gives the decomposition

\Lie\left(\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\tt F}}\right)=\Lie\left(\mathfrak U_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\tt F}}^{+}\right)\oplus\Lie\left(T_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\tt F}}\right)\oplus\Lie\left(\mathfrak U_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\tt F}}^{-}\right).

The goal now is to extend
\mathfrak C_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\tt F}}
to a
big cell

\mathfrak C_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}
, as an open subscheme of
{\mathfrak Y}_{\Lambda}
. We prove this in several steps.

Step
1:

Toral extension to
\Lambda
and closed embedding in
\mathfrak Y_{\Lambda}
.
Since we work with the
generically split
case, the maximal torus
T_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\tt F}}\subset\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\tt F}}
is
split
. We now work with a
U
which contains all primes of height at most
1
. The torus
T_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\tt F}}
canonically extends to a smooth closed multiplicative subgroup scheme
\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},U}
of
\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},U}
over
U
. This can be explicitly constructed as in
[
BT84a
, Section 4.4.18 and 4.6.2]
(or by using the identity component of the Néron-lft model from
[
BLR90
, Section 10.1, Proposition 6]
, as in
[
Lan96
, Proposition 3.2]
).

By
[
Ray70
, Proposition IX.2.4]
, it follows that the inclusion
\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},U}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},U}\hookrightarrow\mathbb S_{U}
extends uniquely to a smooth group scheme
\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}
together with a closed immersion
\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}\hookrightarrow\mathbb S_{\Lambda}
as a subtorus of the group scheme
\mathbb S_{\Lambda}
. Since
\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}
is
flat and hence torsion-free
, it follows that
\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}
is the schematic closure of
\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},U}
in
\mathbb S
. Again, since
\mathfrak Y_{\Lambda}
is the schematic closure of
\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},U}
in
\mathbb S
, we conclude that the embedding
\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}\hookrightarrow\mathbb S_{\Lambda}
factors via a closed embedding
\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak Y_{\Lambda}
.

Step
2:

Unipotent and big cell extension to
U
.
Next, we need to extend the unipotent group schemes
\mathfrak U_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\tt F}}^{\pm}
, firstly as closed subschemes of
\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},U}
. This is easy since these are precisely the schematic closures of the root groups
\mathfrak U_{r,{\tt F}}^{\pm}
, for
r\in\Phi
, and hence of
\mathfrak U_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\tt F}}^{\pm}
in
\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},U}
; we obviously rely on Bruhat–Tits theory over discrete valuation rings. Call these extended group schemes
\mathfrak U_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},U}^{\pm}
and observe that since we work over height
1
primes, these are
flat
over
U
.

Thus we have the
big cell

(6.9)

\displaystyle\mathfrak C_{U}:=\operatorname{Im}\left(\mathfrak U_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},U}^{-}\times\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},U}\times\mathfrak U_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\tt U}}^{+}\xhookrightarrow{\makebox[10.0pt]{}}\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},U}\right)

in
\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},U}
which has the property that at each of the height
1
primes, it restricts to the big cell in the usual Bruhat–Tits group schemes
\mathfrak{G}_{{}_{j}}
.

Step
3:

Unipotent extension to
\spec(\Lambda)
as closed subschemes of
\mathfrak Y_{\Lambda}
.
We next ensure that the group schemes
\mathfrak U_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},U}^{\pm}
extend as subschemes of the schematic closure
{\mathfrak Y}_{\Lambda}
to ensure that a
big cell

\mathfrak C_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}
can be openly embedded in
{\mathfrak Y}_{\Lambda}
.

We do this in three substeps.

Step
3.1.

We first extend the unipotent group schemes abstractly from
U
to the whole of
\spec(\Lambda)
. For each
r\in\Phi
, consider the
fractional invertible ideal
of
\Lambda
,
i.e.
a finitely generated projective sub
\Lambda
-module of
\tt F
given by

(6.10)

f(r):=z_{1}^{m_{r}({}_{1})}\cdots z_{n}^{m_{r}({}_{n})}\Lambda\subset\tt F.

By
[
BT84a
, Section 3.2.6]
, this ideal defines smooth
\Lambda
-group schemes
\mathfrak U_{r,\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}
extending
the root groups
U_{r,{\tt F}}
as well as the root groups
\mathfrak U_{r,\boldsymbol{\Omega},U}
over
U
.

Step
3.2.

We now prove that the group schemes
\mathfrak U_{r,\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}
are closed subgroup schemes of the Abelian subgroup schemes
\mathfrak{B}_{r}
(see (
6.3
)) of
\mathbb S_{\Lambda}
and therefore closed subschemes of
{\mathfrak Y}_{\Lambda}
. We do so by showing in fact that they are
schematic closures
of the root groups
\mathfrak U_{r,\boldsymbol{\Omega},U}
in
\mathfrak{B}_{r}
. Our method is to reduce verifications by Section
5.2
to an inclusion of Lie algebra bundles on
\Lambda
. This check further reduces to
U
.

Step
3.2.1.

By Section
5.2
, the following group schemes arise from the
W
-construction of their Lie algebra bundles:

(6.11)

\mathfrak U_{r,\boldsymbol{\Omega},U}\simeq W\left(\Lie\left(\mathfrak U_{r,\boldsymbol{\Omega},U}\right)\right),\quad\quad\mathfrak B_{r}\simeq W\left(\Lie\left(\mathfrak B_{r}\right)\right).

Note that the root groups
\mathfrak U_{r,\boldsymbol{\Omega},U}
are closed subgroup schemes (over
U
) of the restrictions
\mathfrak B_{r,U}
to
U
. Moreover, the
\mathfrak B_{r}
are themselves closed Abelian subgroup schemes of
\mathbb S_{\Lambda}
(see (
6.3
)). Thus, by
[
SGA3
, Exposé I, Proposition 4.6.6]
(recalled in Section
5.2
), we conclude that the corresponding rank
1
Lie algebra bundle
\Lie(\mathfrak U_{r,\boldsymbol{\Omega},U})
is a direct summand of the Lie algebra bundle
\Lie(\mathfrak B_{r}|_{U})
.

Step
3.2.2.

We claim that
\Lie(\mathfrak U_{r,\boldsymbol{\Omega},U})
extends to
\spec(\Lambda)
as the line bundle
\mathcal{R}_{r}
of (
6.5
). Indeed, since each
\mathcal{R}_{r}
restricts to
\Lie(\mathfrak U_{r,\boldsymbol{\Omega},U})
on
U
, it follows that by taking their reflexive closures inside
\mathcal{R}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}
(see Proposition
6.5
), each
\Lie(\mathfrak U_{r,\boldsymbol{\Omega},U})
extends to
\spec(\Lambda)
as the line bundle
\mathcal{R}_{r}
. This extension
\mathcal{R}_{r}
is a summand of
\Lie(\mathfrak{B}_{r})
because its restriction to
U
has this property.

Moreover, these are extensions as Lie algebra subbundles of
\mathcal{R}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}
because by the Hartogs lemma, the Lie bracket extends across codimension bigger than
2
.

Step
3.2.3.

By Step
33.1
, the root group schemes
\mathfrak U_{r,\boldsymbol{\Omega},U}
already have the extensions as unipotent group schemes given by
\mathfrak U_{r,\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}
. Again, since the Lie algebra bundles
\mathcal{R}_{r}
and
\Lie(\mathfrak U_{r,\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda})

agree on
U
, we have an isomorphism of Lie algebra bundles

(6.12)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{r}\simeq\Lie\left(\mathfrak U_{r,\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}\right).

This therefore identifies
\Lie(\mathfrak U_{r,\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda})
as a Lie subalgebra bundle of
\mathcal{R}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}
as well as a
direct summand
of
\Lie(\mathfrak{B}_{r})
.

Step
3.2.4.

We observe that
\mathfrak U_{r,\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}
arises from the functor
W
construction of Section
5.2
. More precisely, we have scheme-theoretic isomorphisms of vector group schemes

(6.13)

\displaystyle\mathfrak U_{r,\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}=W\left(\Lie\left(\mathfrak U_{r,\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}\right)\right).

Since the
\Lie(\mathfrak U_{r,\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda})
are direct summands of
\Lie(\mathfrak{B}_{r})
, by Section
5.2
it follows that the
\mathfrak U_{r,\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}
are closed subgroup schemes of
\mathfrak{B}_{r}
and therefore of
\mathbb{S}_{\Lambda}
. It follows in particular that the inclusions
\mathfrak U_{r,\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}\subset{\mathfrak Y}_{\Lambda}
are
closed embeddings
, as claimed.

Step
3.3.

We have arrived at the setting of
[
BT84a
, Section 2.2.2]
. For
r\in\Phi^{\pm}
, we denote the scheme-theoretic product of group schemes
\mathfrak U_{r,\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}
as the
\Lambda
-scheme
\mathfrak U_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\Lambda}}^{\pm}
. Since each
\mathfrak U_{r,\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}
is the schematic closure of
\mathfrak U_{r,\boldsymbol{\Omega},U}
in
\mathbb{S}_{\Lambda}
, it follows that the
\mathfrak U_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\Lambda}}^{\pm}
are the
schematic closures
of
\mathfrak U_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},U}^{\pm}
in
\mathbb{S}_{\Lambda}
. The
\mathfrak U_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},U}^{\pm}
are smooth unipotent subgroup schemes of
\mathbb S_{\Lambda}
over
U
, and since the
\mathfrak U_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\Lambda}}^{\pm}
are flat, by
[
BT84a
, Section 1.2.7]
, it follows that the
\mathfrak U_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\Lambda}}^{\pm}
are closed subgroup schemes of
\mathbb S_{\Lambda}
. Hence we get the smooth unipotent group scheme structure on the
\mathfrak U_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\Lambda}}^{\pm}
as well as realize them as closed subschemes of
{\mathfrak Y}_{\Lambda}
.

This completes the proofs of Steps
33.3
,
33.2
and
33.1
and, as a consequence, of Step
3
.

Step
4:

Closed embedding of the big cell in
\mathfrak{Y}_{\Lambda}
.
We finally need to ensure that the open immersion
j_{U}\colon\mathfrak U_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},U}^{-}\times\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},U}\times\mathfrak U_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},U}^{+}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},U}
extends as an open immersion
j_{\Lambda}\colon\mathfrak U_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}^{+}\times\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}\times\mathfrak U_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}^{-}\hookrightarrow{\mathfrak Y}_{\Lambda}
.

More precisely, we show that the schematic closure of the image of the multiplication map of
\mathfrak U_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}^{-}\times\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}\times\mathfrak U_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}^{+}
in
\mathbb S_{\Lambda}
is actually
\mathfrak Y_{\Lambda}
and then derive the open immersion property as a consequence.

By using the discussion preceding (
6.6
) and taking closures term by term, we see that
\mathfrak U_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}^{-}\times\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}\times\mathfrak U_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}^{+}
is a flat closed subscheme of
\mathfrak B^{-}\times\mathcal{T}\times\mathfrak B^{+}
, which is itself an open subscheme of
\mathbb S_{\Lambda}
. In other words, the product defines an isomorphism of schemes of
\mathfrak U_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}^{-}\times\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}\times\mathfrak U_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}^{+}
onto a flat closed subscheme

(6.14)

\displaystyle\mathfrak C_{{\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}}\subset\operatorname{Im}\left(\mathfrak B^{-}\times\mathcal{T}\times\mathfrak B^{+}\right)

and, moreover,
\operatorname{Im}\big(\mathfrak B^{-}\times\mathcal{T}\times\mathfrak B^{+}\big)
is an open subscheme of
\mathbb S_{\Lambda}
.

Let
\bar{\mathfrak C}
be the schematic closure of
\mathfrak C_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}
in
\mathbb S_{\Lambda}
. We
claim
that
\bar{\mathfrak C}={\mathfrak Y}_{\Lambda}
. By what we have checked above, it is clear that
\bar{\mathfrak C}
is contained in
{\mathfrak Y}_{\Lambda}
.

Conversely, the big cell
\mathfrak C_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\tt F}}
is an open dense subscheme of
\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\tt F}}
, hence
\bar{\mathfrak C}\supset\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},{\tt F}}
, implying that
\bar{\mathfrak C}
contains
{\mathfrak Y}_{\Lambda}
. Hence, by applying
[
BT84a
, Section 1.2.6]
, we conclude that
\mathfrak C_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}
is an
open dense subscheme
of its schematic closure which has been identified with
{\mathfrak Y}_{\Lambda}
.

This completes the proof of Claim
6.8
and hence of Part (
I
) of the argument.

Part (
II
) of the proof
.
We now state and give the proof of Bruhat and Tits
[
BT84a
, Proposition 2.2.10]
, minimally tweaked and tailored for our purposes. The result is referred to in a couple of places in their work (see
[
BT84a
, Section 3.9.4]
), where they talk of the possibility of an extension of their theory to
2BT
-group schemes; but for us it is indispensable. In
[
BT84a
, Proposition 2.2.10]
, the role of the group scheme
\mathfrak{GL}(M)
is played by the group scheme
\mathbb S_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}
(see Definition
6.3
) in our situation. Hence we almost “reproduce” their rather long and technical proof in its entirety, with suitable changes.

Proposition 6.9

(
cf.

[
BT84a
, Proposition 2.2.10]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak H
be the image of
\mathfrak C_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}\times\mathfrak C_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}
in
\mathbb S_{\Lambda}
by the product morphism
\pi
of
\mathbb S_{\Lambda}
. Then
\mathfrak H
is open in

{\mathfrak Y}_{\Lambda}
and is a subgroup scheme of
\mathbb S_{\Lambda}
, with fibre
\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},U}
over
U
, flat, of finite type, containing
\mathfrak U_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}^{-}
,
\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}
and
\mathfrak U_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},U}^{+}
as closed subgroup schemes and containing
\mathfrak C_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}
as an open subscheme
(
a big cell
)
. Furthermore, the group scheme
\mathfrak H
is a quasi-affine smooth group scheme over
\Lambda
.

Proof.

For simplicity of notation, we will do away with all
multiple Greek letters
in subscripts.

As a preliminary step so as to follow the rest of the argument in
[
BT84a
]
, we need to check that
\mathfrak U^{-}_{\Lambda}\times\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}\times\mathfrak U^{+}_{\Lambda}
are group schemes.

For this we consider the morphism

(6.15)

\displaystyle\mathfrak U^{-}_{\Lambda}\times\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}\longrightarrow\mathbb S_{\Lambda}

given by
(u,t)\mapsto t\cdot u\cdot t^{-1}
. By
[
BT84a
, Section 4.4.19]
, this gives an action of
T_{U}
on
\mathfrak U^{-}_{U}
, and hence over
U
, the image lands in
\mathfrak U^{-}_{U}
. Since
\mathfrak U^{-}_{\Lambda}
is a closed subscheme of
\mathbb S_{\Lambda}
and since
U
contains all points of height at most
1
, it follows that the morphism given by conjugation has its image in
\mathfrak U^{-}_{\Lambda}
. In other words,
\mathfrak U^{-}_{\Lambda}\times\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}=\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}\times\mathfrak U^{-}_{\Lambda}
in
\mathbb S_{\Lambda}
;
i.e.
it is a subgroup scheme; see
[
DG70
, Section 3.10, p. 166]
.
Similarly,
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}\times\mathfrak U^{+}_{\Lambda}
is also a group scheme.

As
\mathfrak C\times\mathfrak C
is flat and
\pi(\mathfrak C_{U}\times\mathfrak C_{U})\subset\mathfrak{G}_{U}
, by Lemma
6.6
(where
\mathfrak Y
plays the role of
Z
), we have the inclusion
\mathfrak H\subset\mathfrak Y
.

By what we have seen in part (
I
) above,
\mathfrak C
is an open subset of
\mathfrak Y
. So the inverse image
\Gamma
of
\mathfrak C
by
\pi\colon\mathfrak C\times\mathfrak C\to\mathfrak Y
is therefore an open subset of
\mathfrak C\times\mathfrak C
. Since
\mathfrak U^{-}_{\Lambda}\times\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}\times\mathfrak U^{+}_{\Lambda}
are groups, we get the inclusion

(6.16)
