\displaystyle\Gamma\supset\left(\left(\mathfrak U^{-}_{\Lambda}\times\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}\times\{1\}\right)\times\left(\mathfrak U^{-}_{\Lambda}\times\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}\times\{1\}\right)\right)\cup\left(\left(\{1\}\times\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}\times\mathfrak U^{+}_{\Lambda}\right)\times\left(\{1\}\times\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}\times\mathfrak U^{+}_{\Lambda}\right)\right).

Let
p\in\spec(\Lambda)
be a closed point, and let
k
be the residue field of the local ring
\Lambda_{p}
. The fibre
\Gamma_{p}
of
\Gamma
is an open subset of
\mathfrak C_{p}\times\mathfrak C_{p}
, dense in
\mathfrak C_{p}\times\mathfrak C_{p}
: in fact, if
u,u^{\prime}\in\mathfrak U^{-}_{p}
and
z,z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{p}
, the set
V
of
(v,v^{\prime})\in\mathfrak U^{+}_{p}\times\mathfrak U^{+}_{p}
such that
\big((u,z,v),(u^{\prime},z^{\prime},v^{\prime})\big)\in\Gamma_{p}
is an open subset of
\mathfrak U^{+}_{p}\times\mathfrak U^{+}_{p}
and is non-empty since
(1,1)\in V
by (
6.16
), therefore dense in
\mathfrak U^{+}_{p}\times\mathfrak U^{+}_{p}
since
\mathfrak U^{+}_{p}
is connected.

Let
H_{p}
be the closure of
\mathfrak C_{p}
in
\mathbb S_{p}
. As
\mathfrak C_{p}
is open in
\mathfrak Y_{p}
,
a fortiori
,
\mathfrak C_{p}
is open in
H_{p}
. Moreover,
\Gamma_{p}
is dense in
H_{p}\times H_{p}
, and
\pi(H_{p}\times H_{p})\subset H_{p}
since by definition
\pi(\Gamma_{p})\subset\mathfrak Y_{p}
. In other words,
H_{p}
is a subgroup scheme of
\mathbb S_{p}
. As
\mathfrak C_{p}
is open dense in
H_{p}
, we have
H_{p}=\mathfrak C_{p}\times\mathfrak C_{p}
, hence

(6.17)

\displaystyle H_{p}=\mathfrak H\cap\mathbb S_{p}.

We also have
H_{p}=\mathfrak C_{p}^{-1}\times\mathfrak C_{p}=\mathfrak U^{+}_{p}\mathcal{T}_{p}\mathfrak U^{-}_{p}\times\mathfrak U^{-}_{p}\mathcal{T}_{p}\mathfrak U^{+}_{p}=\mathfrak U^{+}_{p}\mathfrak C_{p}
,
and hence

(6.18)

\displaystyle H_{p}=\bigcupop\displaylimits_{a\in\mathfrak U^{+}_{p}(k)}a\times\mathfrak C_{p}.

We now show that
\mathfrak H

is open in
\mathfrak Y
and is flat.
Firstly,
\mathfrak H
is a
constructible
subset of
\mathfrak Y
. To show that it is open, (since
\Lambda
is Noetherian), it suffices to show that if
x\in\mathfrak H
and
y\in\mathfrak Y
are such that
x\in\overline{\{y\}}
, then
y\in\mathfrak H
(see
[
DG70
, Corollaire 3.4, p. 76]
).

Let
p
be the projection of
x
in
\spec(\Lambda)
. Since the smooth group scheme
\mathbb S_{\Lambda}
is obtained by a Weil restriction of scalars (see the paragraph just before Definition
6.3
), it behaves well under base change. Let
j\colon\spec(\Lambda_{p})\to\spec(\Lambda)
be the induced morphism. As the local ring
\Lambda_{p}
is flat over
\Lambda
, the schematic closures of
\mathfrak{G}_{U}
and
\mathfrak C\times_{\Lambda}\Lambda_{p}
in
j^{*}(\mathbb S_{\Lambda})
coincide with
j^{*}(\mathfrak Y)
(see
[
BLR90
, Section 2.5, Proposition 2]
,
[
DG70
, Section 4.14, p. 56]
). Hence we may reduce to the case when
\Lambda
is a local ring with maximal ideal
p
.

Let
B_{x}
be the local ring at
x
in
\mathbb S_{\Lambda}
, and let
\tilde{B}
be the strict henselization of
B_{x}
. Let
x^{\prime}\in\spec(\tilde{B})
be the closed point, and let
\alpha\colon\spec(\tilde{B})\to\spec(B_{x})
be the canonical morphism: we have
\alpha(x^{\prime})=x
. For us the residue field at
x
is algebraically closed and hence by (
6.18
), there exists an
a\in\mathfrak U^{+}_{p}(k)
such that
x\in a\cdot\mathfrak C_{p}
. Since
\mathfrak U^{+}_{\Lambda}
is smooth, by Hensel’s lemma, there exists a section
\hat{a}\in\mathfrak U^{+}_{\Lambda}(\tilde{B})
of
\mathfrak U^{+}_{\Lambda}
above
\spec(\tilde{B})
such that
\hat{a}(x^{\prime})=a
.

We now do a base change by
\Lambda\to\tilde{B}
. Let
\mathfrak X
(resp.
X
) be a
\Lambda
-scheme (resp. a
U
-scheme, where
U
has been base changed to the local ring at
p
), set
\tilde{\mathfrak X}:=\mathfrak X\times_{\spec(\Lambda)}\spec(\tilde{B})
(resp.
\tilde{X}:=X\times_{U}\tilde{U}
, with
\tilde{U}=U\times_{\spec(\Lambda)}\spec(\tilde{B})
). If
f\colon\mathfrak X\to\mathfrak Z
is a morphism of
\Lambda
-schemes, set
\tilde{f}=f\times\id\colon\tilde{\mathfrak X}\to\tilde{\mathfrak Z}
. Finally, let
\beta\colon\tilde{\mathbb S}\to\mathbb S_{\Lambda}
be the first projection. As
\tilde{B}
is flat over
B_{x}
and
B_{x}
is flat over
\Lambda
(as
\mathbb S_{\Lambda}
is flat),
\tilde{B}
is flat over
\Lambda
, and we see as above that
\tilde{\mathfrak Y}
is the schematic closure of both
\tilde{\mathfrak C}
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{G}}_{U}
in
\tilde{\mathbb S}
. On the other hand, the left-translation by the section
\hat{a}
is an automorphism of the scheme
\tilde{\mathbb S}
, and the restriction
\hat{a}_{U}
of
\hat{a}
to
\tilde{U}
belongs to
\mathfrak U^{+}_{p}(\tilde{U})\subset\tilde{\mathfrak{G}}_{U}(\tilde{U})
; thus we have
\hat{a}\cdot\tilde{\mathfrak{G}}_{U}=\hat{a}_{U}\cdot\tilde{\mathfrak{G}}_{U}=\tilde{\mathfrak{G}}_{U}
and
\hat{a}\cdot\tilde{\mathfrak Y}=\tilde{\mathfrak Y}
. As
\tilde{\mathfrak C}
is an open in
\tilde{\mathfrak Y}
, it follows that
\hat{a}\cdot\tilde{\mathfrak C}
is
open in

\tilde{\mathfrak Y}
.

As
\tilde{B}
is faithfully flat over
B_{x}
, the morphism
\alpha
is surjective. We have
x\in\overline{\{y\}}
, thus
y\in\spec(B_{x})
and there exists a
y^{\prime}\in\spec(\tilde{B})
such that
\alpha(y^{\prime})=y
. Moreover,
x^{\prime}\in\overline{\{y^{\prime}\}}
since the closed point of
\spec(\tilde{B})
belongs to the closure of any other point of
\spec(\Lambda)
. Set
x^{\prime\prime}:=(\alpha\times\id)(x^{\prime})=(x,x^{\prime})
and
y^{\prime\prime}:=(\alpha\times\id)(y^{\prime})=(y,y^{\prime})
; then
x^{\prime\prime}\in\hat{a}\cdot\tilde{\mathfrak C}
,
y^{\prime\prime}\in\tilde{\mathfrak Y}
and
x^{\prime\prime}\in\overline{\{y^{\prime\prime}\}}
. As
\hat{a}\cdot\tilde{\mathfrak C}
is open in
\tilde{\mathfrak Y}
, it follows that

(6.19)

\displaystyle y^{\prime\prime}\in\hat{a}\cdot\tilde{\mathfrak C}=\tilde{\pi}\circ\left(\hat{a}\times\id\right)\left(\spec\left(\tilde{B}\times_{\tilde{B}}\tilde{\mathfrak C}\right)\right)\subset\tilde{\pi}\left(\tilde{\mathfrak C}\times_{\tilde{B}}\tilde{\mathfrak C}\right).

We then deduce that

(6.20)

\displaystyle y=\beta\left(y^{\prime\prime}\right)\in\beta\circ\tilde{\pi}\left(\tilde{\mathfrak C}\times_{\tilde{B}}\tilde{\mathfrak C}\right)=\pi\circ\left(\beta\times\beta\right)\left(\tilde{\mathfrak C}\times_{\tilde{B}}\tilde{\mathfrak C}\right)\subset\pi\left(\mathfrak C\times\mathfrak C\right)=\mathfrak H

and hence that
\mathfrak H
is
open in

\mathfrak Y
.

Moreover,
\hat{a}\cdot\tilde{\mathfrak C}
is flat over
\spec(\tilde{B})
. As it is a neighbourhood of
x
in
\mathfrak H_{\tilde{B}}
, it follows that the local ring at
x
in
\mathfrak H_{\tilde{B}}
is flat over
\spec(\tilde{B})
, and by faithfully flat descent, the local ring of
x
in
\mathfrak H
is flat over
\Lambda
. Hence
\mathfrak H

is flat
.

It follows that
\mathfrak H
, being an open subscheme of
\mathfrak Y
, is a subscheme of
\mathbb S_{\Lambda}
(which is no reason for it to be affine!). It is a subgroup scheme of
\mathbb S_{\Lambda}
. In fact, the restriction to
\mathfrak H
of the product
\pi
of
\mathbb S_{\Lambda}
sends
\mathfrak H_{U}\times\mathfrak H_{U}
into
\mathfrak{G}_{U}
, and hence, since
\mathfrak H
is flat, it factorises by a morphism
\mathfrak H\times\mathfrak H\to\mathfrak Y
; see Lemma
6.6
. But set-theoretically, we have
\pi(\mathfrak H\times\mathfrak H)\subset\mathfrak H
since the fibres of
\mathfrak H
are subgroups; see (
6.18
). Since
\mathfrak H
is an open in
\mathfrak Y
, it implies that the morphism
\pi\colon\mathfrak H\times\mathfrak H\to\mathfrak Y
factorises through
\mathfrak H
. A similar argument for the
inverse
morphism shows that
\mathfrak H
is a subgroup scheme.

Now
\mathfrak H
is an open subscheme of
\mathfrak Y
which is closed in the
schematic hull

\mathbb S_{\Lambda}
. Further, by (
6.1
),
\mathbb S_{\Lambda}
is affine and hence
\mathfrak H
is
quasi-affine
. That it has the remaining stated properties is easy to check. ∎

Remark 6.10
.

The quasi-affineness can also be deduced as follows. Since the fibre of
\mathfrak H
over
U
is
\mathfrak{G}_{U}
, which is affine, and since
\mathfrak H
, being the image of
\mathfrak C\times\mathfrak C
, has connected fibres, by
[
Ray70
, Corollary VII.2.2]
, we conclude that
\mathfrak H
is
quasi-affine
.

Remark 6.11
.

When the characteristic of the residue field is zero, the group scheme
\mathfrak H
is
flat
and hence smooth. Over residue fields of positive characteristics, the smoothness of
\mathfrak H
is ensured by the smoothness of the torus
\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}
since the unipotent groups
\mathfrak U_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}^{\pm}
are smooth. By the argument in the paragraph following (
5.17
), the torus on the big open subset extends as a torus
\mathcal{T}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},\Lambda}
and is therefore smooth even in the general case.

To complete the proof of Theorem
6.7
, we now set

(6.21)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}:={\mathfrak H}.

This group scheme clearly satisfies the properties in the statement of Theorem
1.3
. Indeed, in general it is quasi-affine, its sections can be computed by restriction to
U
, (
4
) and (
5
) follow from part (
I
) of the proof, and the proof of the characterization and uniqueness of the group scheme follows exactly as in Theorem
5.7
.
∎

6.5. Proof of Theorem
1.5

Proof.

Since
\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}
is quasi-affine by Theorem
6.7
, as in Remark
5.8
we can identify the restriction of
\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}
to a subdiagonal (see Theorem
1.5
(
b
)) with the group scheme
\mathfrak{G}_{f_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{m})}}
, where
f_{i_{\ell}}:=f_{{}_{i_{\ell}}}
. The description of the closed fibre follows from
[
BT84a
, Proposition 4.6.5 and Corollaire 4.6.12]
applied to the concave function
f_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{m})}=\sumop\displaylimits_{\ell=1}^{m}f_{i_{\ell}}
.∎

7. Schematization of
n
-bounded groups
{\tt P}_{\bf f}

The notation in this section is as in the previous one; namely,
\bf A\simeq\mathbb A^{n}
and
{\bf A_{0}}\subset{\bf A}
is the complement of the coordinate hyperplanes. In this section we return to the general case of an almost simple group
G
. We complete the picture by proving Theorem
1.3
for
n
-concave functions of
type III
. More precisely, we construct the group scheme over
\bf A
, given the data of
n
-concave functions
{\bf f}=\{f_{j}\}
on the root system
\Phi
of
G
, along with an assignment of the
BT
-group schemes
\{\mathfrak{G}_{f_{j}}\}_{j=1}^{n}
at the generic points of each of the coordinate hyperplanes
H_{j}\subset\bf A
.

Although the strategy of the proof is broadly a reduction to the proof in the
\SL(n)_{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}
case, in the present case of
(G,{\bf f})
, there are two crucial differences. First, unlike in the case of the concave function
f
, we do not have any way of assigning points in the affine apartment (see Example
3.11
for an example) to get a product
n
-parahoric group scheme
{\mathbb S}_{\Lambda}
(see Section
6.1
) for
G
which gave us an ambient group scheme to carry out the strategy. And second, as it will become clearer by the discussion below, the condition on the characteristic of the residue field needs to be weakened to
p>{\tt h}_{G}
.

As a first step, we recall some of the main results of
[
BT84a
]
. These will play the key role in this section. In the setting of
[
BT84a
, Section 3]
,
K
is an infinite field containing a subring
A
having
K
as its field of fractions. The aim of that section (see
[
BT84a
, Section 3.1.3]
) is to show that if
G
has a
donnée radicielle schématique

\mathcal{D}=(\mathcal{Z},(\mathcal{U}_{a})_{a\in\Phi})
(see
[
BT84a
, Définition 3.1.1]
) or a
schematic root datum
, then there exists an
A
-group scheme
\mathfrak{G}
extending
G\times\spec(K)
with big cell given by
\mathcal{D}
. This is done by showing that for any faithful representation
G\hookrightarrow\GL(V)
, there is an
A
-lattice
M\subset V_{K}
such that the
BT
-group scheme
\mathfrak{G}
is the schematic closure of
G_{K}
in
\mathfrak{GL}(M)
. In
[
BT84a
, Section 4.5.4]
, when
K
is equipped with a valuation, it is shown that given any concave function
f
on
\Phi
, one can construct a
schematic root datum
for
G
. We apply these results to our context as follows. Since
G
is almost simple, we may assume that we have a factorization
G\hookrightarrow\SL(V)
. Hence,
\mathfrak{G}_{f}
is the schematic closure of
G_{K}
in the schematic closure of
\SL(V)_{K}
in
\mathfrak{GL}(M)
. By choosing an
\mathcal{O}
-basis for
M
, it is easy to see that we get a concave function
h
on the root system
\Phi_{V}
of
\SL(V)
such that the schematic closure of
\SL(V)_{K}
in
\mathfrak{GL}(M)
is precisely the
BT
-group scheme
\SL(V)_{h}
.

By Proposition
3.21
and the remarks preceding it, we may assume that
h
is an
optimal concave function
on
\Phi_{V}
, and hence it is of the form
h
for a bounded subset
\Omega
in the affine apartment of
\SL(V)
.

Thus, we are reduced to the following situation: the
BT
-group scheme
\mathfrak{G}_{f}
is the schematic closure of
G_{K}
in
\SL(V)
. This can be carried out for each of the
\{f_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
, and we get bounded subsets
\boldsymbol{\Omega}=({}_{1},\ldots,{}_{n})
in the affine apartment of
\SL(V)
.

By Theorem
6.7
, taking
\boldsymbol{\Omega}=({}_{1},\ldots,{}_{n})
and the group
\SL(V)
, we obtain an
nBT
-group scheme
\mathfrak{SL}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}
on
\bf A
. Although only quasi-affine, this group scheme will play the role of the schematic hull
\mathbb{S}_{\Lambda}
(see (
6.1
)) of Section
6
. Further, on
U
whose complement in
\bf A
has codimension at least
2
, we have an inclusion of group schemes

(7.1)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{G}_{\bf f,U}\subset\mathfrak{SL}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega},U}.

The proof strategy for proving Theorem
6.7
now almost applies. Let us explain the difference. In that proof, it was shown in Step
33.2
that the root groups
\mathfrak U_{r,{\bf\Omega},\Lambda}
are the schematic closures of
\mathfrak U_{r,{\bf\Omega},U}
in
\mathbb S_{\Lambda}
by the intermediary of
vector subgroup

\mathfrak B_{r}
of
\mathfrak S_{\Lambda}
. This vector group exists because of the specific
diagonal embedding
into the ambient group scheme
\mathbb S_{\Lambda}
. In the present setting, however, our chosen representation, namely
G\subset\SL(V)
, is arbitrary and cannot be expected to have the desirable property that for every root
r\in\Phi
, we have a vector subgroup scheme of
\SL(n)_{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}
whose restriction to
U
contains
\mathfrak U_{r,{\bf f},U}
. Consequently, to replace the arguments of Step
33.2
, we recover the group law of unipotent group schemes by the Baker–Campbell–Hausdorff formula. We do this as follows.
If
\{c_{\alpha}\}
denote the coefficients of the higher root, recall that the Coxeter number
{\tt h}_{G}
of
G
is defined as
1+\sumop\displaylimits c_{\alpha}
.
Let
\mathfrak{N}
be a nilpotent Lie algebra bundle on
\mathbf{A}
.
By
[
Ser96
, Section 2.1]
(see also
[
BDP17
, Section 2.2]
and other references there),
when the characteristic satisfies
p>{\tt h}_{G}
, the Baker–Campbell–Hausdorff group law equips the scheme
W(\mathfrak{N})
with the structure of a
smooth, affine, unipotent group scheme with connected fibres
together with an isomorphism
\operatorname{exp}\colon{\mathfrak N}\to\Lie(W(\mathfrak{N}))
of nilpotent Lie algebra bundles. Conversely, if
\mathfrak U
is an affine, smooth, unipotent group scheme with connected fibres, we have a unique isomorphism of unipotent group schemes
\operatorname{exp}\colon W(\Lie(\mathfrak U))\to\mathfrak U
. Note that the Baker–Campbell–Hausdorff formula realizes the possible non-Abelian unipotent group scheme structures on
\mathfrak U
. In the absence of vector group schemes
\mathfrak B_{r}
, this is the key substitute. Let us now go over arguments that replace Step
33.2
.

As in Step
33.2
, we need to prove that the extended root group schemes
\mathfrak U_{r,\bf f,\bf A}
are closed subgroup schemes of
\mathfrak{SL}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}
on
{\bf A}
. We begin by noting that over the open subset
U\subset{\bf A}
, the root group schemes
\mathfrak U_{r,\bf f,U}
are closed subgroup schemes of the restriction to
U
of one of the big cells, say
\mathfrak B^{+}
of
\mathfrak{SL}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}
. By Theorem
6.7
,
\mathfrak B^{+}
of
\mathfrak{SL}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}
is affine. We set
\mathfrak N^{\pm}:=\Lie(\mathfrak B^{\pm})
. This is a nilpotent Lie algebra bundle on
\bf A
. When
p>{\tt h}_{G}
, we have the natural isomorphism

(7.2)

\displaystyle\operatorname{exp}\colon W\left(\mathfrak N^{\pm}\right)\simeq\mathfrak B^{\pm}

of affine, smooth, connected unipotent group schemes. Since
\mathfrak U_{r,\bf f,U}\subset\mathfrak B^{+}_{U}
is a closed subgroup scheme, we may view this inclusion in terms of the “W”-functor as follows:

(7.3)

\displaystyle W\left(\Lie\left(\mathfrak U_{r,\bf f,U}\right)\right)\subset W\left(\Lie\left(\mathfrak B^{+}_{U}\right)\right).

By this closed embedding of affine
U
-schemes, we get the Lie algebra subbundle
\Lie(\mathfrak U_{r,\bf f,U})\subset\Lie(\mathfrak B^{+}_{U})
structure. This is in fact a
direct summand
of locally free sheaves on
U
. Now taking the
reflexive closure

\overline{\Lie(\mathfrak U_{r,\bf f,U})}
of the rank
1
Lie algebra bundle
\Lie(\mathfrak U_{r,\bf f,U})
, we see that
\overline{\Lie(\mathfrak U_{r,\bf f,U})}
is a locally free
direct summand
of
\mathfrak N^{+}
over
\bf A
.

As in Step
33.2
, the reflexive closure
\overline{\Lie(\mathfrak U_{r,\bf f,U})}
gets identified with the Lie algebra line bundle
\mathcal{R}_{r}
. It is therefore a
direct summand
of
\mathfrak N^{+}
over
{\bf A}
. This implies that as affine schemes, we have a closed embedding

(7.4)

\displaystyle W\left(\mathcal{R}_{r}\right)\subset W\left(\mathfrak N^{+}\right)\simeq\mathfrak B^{+}.

Setting
\mathfrak U_{r,\bf f,\bf A}:=W(\mathcal{R}_{r})
, it follows that the scheme underlying
\mathfrak U_{r,\bf f,\bf A}
is a closed subscheme of
\mathfrak B^{+}
. By the Baker–Campbell–Hausdorff formula, this inclusion is in fact one of closed subgroup schemes over
\bf A
and hence of
\mathfrak{SL}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}
on
\bf A
. This completes the proof of the key difference we mentioned above. In particular, at this stage the
big cell

\mathfrak B_{\Phi}
can also easily be seen to exist with the standard properties exactly as in the proof of Theorem
6.7
.

With these changes in place, the second part of the proof of Theorem
6.7
may be applied. We get the required
nBT
-group scheme
\mathfrak{G}_{\bf f,\bf A}
associated to
{\bf f}
on
{\bf A}
. It is open inside a closed subscheme, say
\mathcal{Z}
, of
\mathfrak{SL}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}
. Recall that
\mathfrak{SL}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}
is open inside a closed subscheme, say
C
, of
\mathbb{S}_{\Lambda}
. Let
\overline{\mathcal{Z}}
denote the closure of
\mathcal{Z}
in
C
. The intersection of
\overline{\mathcal{Z}}
with
\mathfrak{SL}_{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}
in
C
is
\mathcal{Z}
. It follows that
\mathcal{Z}
is open in
\overline{\mathcal{Z}}
. Therefore,
\mathfrak{G}_{\bf f,\bf A}
is open inside
\overline{\mathcal{Z}}
. Now
\overline{\mathcal{Z}}
is affine because
\mathbb{S}_{\Lambda}
is affine. We conclude that
\mathfrak{G}_{\bf f,\bf A}
is quasi-affine (this can also be checked using
[
Ray70
, Corollary VII.2.2]
). In conclusion, when
\charr(k)
is at least
h_{G}
and satisfies the hypothesis of Section
2.2
, we have constructed
\mathfrak{G}_{\bf f,\bf A}
as a
smooth, quasi-affine
group scheme with all the properties stated in Theorem
1.3
for
n
-concave functions of
type III
.

8. Schematization of
n
-Moy–Prasad groups

In this section we discuss Moy–Prasad groups over complete discrete valuation rings in the equal-characteristic case. The aim is to recover these anew in the spirit of
[
BS15
]
. This then gives the generalization to the higher-dimensional bases.

8.1. Tameness Assumptions

In this section the given data
(\boldsymbol{\Theta},{\bf e}^{\prime}):=\big((\theta_{1},e^{\prime}_{1}),\ldots,(\theta_{n},e^{\prime}_{n})\big)
will consist of an
n
-tuple of rational one-parameter subgroups
\theta_{i}
and positive rational numbers
e_{i}^{\prime}
. We suppose that there exists a positive number
d
coprime to the characteristic of the residue field such that
d\theta_{i}
is a one-parameter subgroups and the
de_{i}^{\prime}
are positive numbers.

8.2. The unit group

Let
\mathcal{O}:=k\llbracket z\rrbracket
and
K
be its quotient field. Let
B:=k\llbracket\omega\rrbracket
and
L
be its quotient field, where
\omega^{d}=z
. We view
N:=\spec(B)
as a Galois cover over
D:=\spec(\mathcal{O})
with
\Gal(N/D)={\sf\mu_{d}}
. Let
p\colon N\to D
be the quotient morphism. Let
\rho\colon{\sf\mu_{d}}\rightarrow G
be a representation. Consider the twisted
{\sf\mu_{d}}
-action on
N\times G
defined by

(8.1)

\gamma(u,g)=(\gamma u,\rho(\gamma)g)\quad\text{for }u\in N,\gamma\in{\sf\mu_{d}}.

This defines a
({\sf\mu_{d}},G)
-bundle on
N
.
We will denote it by
E
.

Define the
unit group
of
E
as an automorphisms of the
({\sf\mu_{d}},G)
-torsor
E
:

(8.2)

{\tt U}_{E}:=\Aut_{({\sf\mu_{d}},G)}(E).

Let
e\geq 0
be a non-negative integer. Consider the thickened fibre
j_{e}\colon E|_{\Spec(B/{\omega}^{e}B)}\hookrightarrow E
. Define
{\tt U}^{(e)}_{E}
to be

(8.3)

{\tt U}^{(e)}_{E}:=\ker\left(\Aut_{({\sf\mu_{d}},G)}(E)\longrightarrow\Aut_{({\sf\mu_{d}},G)}\left(j_{e}^{*}(E)\right)\right),

i.e.
the subgroup of
({\sf\mu_{d}},G)
-automorphisms of
E
over
N

inducing identity on the restriction of
E
to
\Spec(B/{\omega}^{e}B)
. For
\underline{e=0}
, this is just the unit group
{\tt U}_{E}
of
E
.

Consider the adjoint group scheme of
E

(8.4)

\mathfrak{G}_{E}\simeq G\times N,

which is the constant group scheme together with a
\Gamma
-action.

It is well known (
cf.

[
Yu15
, Section 2.4, p. 234]
)
that for each
e\geq 0
, by a
dilatation
of
\mathfrak{G}_{E}
, there exists a smooth group scheme
\mathfrak{G}_{E}^{(e)}
such that

(8.5)

\mathfrak{G}_{E}^{(e)}(B)=\Gamma\left(\omega^{e},\mathfrak{G}_{E}\right),

where
\Gamma(\omega^{e},\mathfrak{G}_{E})
is the
e^{\mathrm{th}}
congruence subgroup
defined by

\Gamma(\omega^{e},\mathfrak{G}_{E})=\ker\left(\mathfrak{G}_{E}(B)\longrightarrow\mathfrak{G}_{E}\left(B/{\omega}^{e}B\right)\right).

8.3. The representation
\boldsymbol{\rho\colon\Gamma\rightarrow T}
and rational one-parameter subgroups
\boldsymbol{\theta}
of
\boldsymbol{T}

Following
[
BS15
, Lemma 2.2.8]
, make the identification

(8.6)

\Hom({\sf\mu_{d}},T)=\frac{Y(T)}{dY(T)}\simeq\frac{1}{d}Y(T)\mod(Y(T)).

We will interpret this identification explicitly to suit our specific needs. Given a one-parameter subgroup

(8.7)

\Delta\colon\mathbb{G}_{m}\longrightarrow T,

by projecting it onto
\frac{Y(T)}{dY(T)}
and using (
8.6
), we obtain from this
1
-PS
\Delta

a rational one-parameter subgroup
\theta_{\Delta}\in\frac{1}{d}Y(T)\mod(Y(T))
. By (
8.6
) again, this gives a homomorphism

(8.8)

\rho_{\Delta}\colon{\sf\mu_{d}}\longrightarrow T.

In the reverse construction, given a homomorphism
\rho\in\Hom({\sf\mu_{d}},T)
, we choose a rational one-parameter subgroup
\theta_{\rho}
determined by the class of
\rho
(we will need to make a choice here). Then
\theta_{\rho}
defines a one-parameter subgroup

(8.9)

\Delta=d\theta_{\rho}\colon\mathbb{G}_{m}\longrightarrow T.

Remark 8.1
.

The point
\theta_{\rho}
determined above by the choice of
\Delta
firstly gives a point of the Weyl alcove
\mathbf{a}_{0}
(see Section
2.1
). This point gives a precise
weight
as obtained by the process of invariant push-forward in
[
BS15
]
. More precisely, the point to be noted is that this construction
\rho\mapsto\theta_{\rho}
gives a point of the
fundamental domain
for the action of
Y(T)
and not just the affine Weyl group. Further, the proofs in Section
8.5
and that of Theorem
9.1
show that
[
BS15
, Theorem 2.3.1 and Proposition 5.1.2]
hold more generally for
\theta_{\rho}
in the fundamental domain of
Y(T)
and not just the affine Weyl group.

8.4. Brief background on Moy–Prasad groups

Moy–Prasad groups have been defined in
[
MP94
, Section 2.6]
.
Using a Chevalley basis of
G
, below we give an equivalent reformulation which is suitable for our setup.
Let
\mathcal{A}=\Hom(\mathbb{G}_{m},T)\otimes\mathbb{R}
denote the apartment corresponding to
T
. For a point
\theta\in\mathcal{A}
, recall that the parahoric group
\tt{P}_{\theta}\subset G(K)
is defined as

(8.10)

{\tt{P}}_{\theta}=\left\langle T(\mathcal{O}),\,U_{r}\left(z^{-\lfloor r(\theta)\rfloor}\mathcal{O}\right),r\in\Phi\right\rangle,

and for a non-negative integer
e^{\prime}\geq 0
, the Moy–Prasad group is defined as

(8.11)

G_{\theta,e^{\prime}}:=\left\langle T\left(1+z^{\lceil e^{\prime}\rceil}\mathcal{O}\right),\,U_{r}(z^{-\lfloor r(\theta)-e^{\prime}\rfloor}\mathcal{O}),r\in\Phi\right\rangle.

While considering parahoric or Moy–Prasad groups, it suffices to assume that
\theta\in\mathcal{A}_{T}
is a rational point and
e^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
. This follows because for any
(r,n)\in\Phi\times\mathbb{Z}
, the functional
\mathcal{A}_{T}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
defined by
(x,e^{\prime})\mapsto r(x)+n-e^{\prime}
is defined over rationals.

8.5. Invariant direct image of dilatations of reductive group schemes

The proof of the following theorem follows the proofs of
[
BS15
, Theorem 2.3.1 and Proposition 5.1.2]
closely.

Theorem 8.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{G}_{\theta,e^{\prime}}
be the Bruhat–Tits group scheme satisfying
\mathfrak{G}_{\theta,e^{\prime}}(\mathcal{O})=G_{\theta,e^{\prime}}

(
see (
8.11
)
)
. Then
(
see (
8.5
)
)
we have a natural isomorphism of group schemes

(8.12)

p_{*}^{\sf\mu_{d}}\left(\mathfrak{G}_{E}^{(e)}\right)\simeq\mathfrak{G}_{\theta_{\rho},e^{\prime}}.

Proof.

Note that the
\mathcal{O}
-valued points are enough to determine the group scheme since
\mathfrak{G}_{\theta,e^{\prime}}
is étoffé in the sense of
[
BT84a
, Définition 1.7.1]
.

We begin with a few observations.
Now an element
\phi_{0}\in{\tt U}_{E}
corresponds to a
{\sf\mu_{d}}
-equivariant automorphism of the
({\sf\mu_{d}},G)
-bundle,
i.e.

\phi_{0}\colon N\times G\rightarrow N\times G
together with the equivariance. In other words,
\phi_{0}\in{\tt U}^{(e)}_{E}
corresponds to a regular morphism
\phi\colon N\rightarrow G
(
i.e.

\phi\in G(B)
), given by

(8.13)

\phi_{0}(u,g)=(u,\phi(u)g),

which satisfies the following conditions:

(1)

{\sf\mu_{d}}
-equivariance,
i.e.

(8.14)

\phi(\gamma u)=\rho(\gamma)\phi(u)\rho(\gamma)^{-1},\quad u\in N,\gamma\in{\sf\mu_{d}};

(2)

when
\underline{e\geq 1}
, the map
\phi
restricted to
\Spec(B/{\omega}^{e}B)
is the identity,
i.e.

(8.15)

\phi\in\ker\left(G(B)\longrightarrow G(B/{\omega}^{e}B)\right).

Thus, as in
[
BS15
, Equation (2.3.1.5)]
, we can identify
{\tt U}_{E}
with
\phi
satisfying (
8.14
); let
{\tt U}^{(e)}_{E}\subset{\tt U}_{E}
be the subgroup of
{\tt U}_{E}
given by the above two conditions.

From here onward, we follow closely the proof of
[
BS15
, Theorem 2.3.1]
.

For each
\phi\in{\tt U}_{E}
, define

(8.16)

\psi(\omega)=\Delta^{-1}\cdot\phi\cdot\Delta.

Then
\psi(\gamma\cdot u)=\psi(u)
for all
\gamma\in{\sf\mu_{d}},u\in N
. It therefore descends to a rational function
\tilde{\psi}\colon\spec(K)\to G
such that

(8.17)

\tilde{\psi}(z)=\psi(\omega).

Now for
\underline{e\geq 1}
, by (
8.15
), we have
\phi(o)=1
, which lies in
G^{\circ}
, where
G^{\circ}\subset G
is the big cell in
G
. Since
N
is a formal neighbourhood of the origin
o
, it follows that
\phi(N)\subset G^{\circ}
.

For
\underline{e=0}
, in the following we will assume that we work with a
\phi
such that
\phi(N)\subset G^{\circ}
. The set of such
\phi
may be called the big cell of
{\tt U}_{E}
; we will denote it by the symbol
\mathfrak U
.

The condition
\phi(N)\subset G^{\circ}
allows us to describe
\phi\colon N\rightarrow G^{\circ}
uniquely as a tuple of the following morphisms:

\left\{\{\phi_{r}\colon N\longrightarrow U_{r}\}_{r\in\Phi},\;\phi_{t}\colon N\longrightarrow T\right\},

where
U_{r}\subset G
is the root subgroup corresponding to
r\in\Phi
. Let us view
\phi_{r}
and
\phi_{t}
as elements of
G(B)\subset G(L)
.

We may write

(8.18)

\phi_{r}(\omega)=\psi_{r}(\omega)\omega^{r(\Delta)}=\tilde{\psi}_{r}(z)\omega^{r(\Delta)}.

Since
\phi_{r}(\omega)
has a zero of order at least
e
in
\omega
,

\frac{\phi_{r}(\omega)}{\omega^{e}}=\tilde{\psi}_{r}(z)\omega^{r(\Delta)-e},

and the right side defines a regular function in
\omega
;
i.e.
it extends to a regular function on
N
. So
\tilde{\psi_{r}}(z)
can have a pole in
\omega
of order at most
r(\Delta)-e
. Thus in
z
it can have a pole of order at most

\left[\frac{r(\Delta)-e}{d}\right];

i.e.

[r(\frac{\Delta}{d})-\frac{e}{d}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{deltaandtheta}}}{{=}}[r(\theta_{\rho})-e^{\prime}]
, where
e^{\prime}=\frac{e}{d}
. This forces the containment

(8.19)

\tilde{\psi_{r}}(z)\in U_{r}\left(z^{-\lfloor r(\theta_{\rho})-e^{\prime}\rfloor}\mathcal{O}\right),

and hence
\tilde{\psi_{r}}
belongs to
G_{\theta_{\rho},e^{\prime}}
.
On the other hand, since
\phi_{t}(u)\in T
for
u\in N
, it follows that
\phi_{t}(\gamma.u)=\rho(\gamma)\phi_{t}(u)\rho(\gamma)^{-1}=\phi_{t}(u)
. Hence
\phi_{t}\colon N\rightarrow T
is
{\sf\mu_{d}}
-invariant. Also, by (
8.16
), it follows that
\phi_{t}=\psi_{t}
. So it descends to a regular function on
D
, and this function is
\tilde{\psi}_{t}\colon D\rightarrow T
. Now
\phi_{t}(u)-1
has a zero of order at least
e
in
\omega
. So by the definition of
e^{\prime}
, we see that
\tilde{\psi_{t}}-1
has a zero in
z
of order at least

\left\lceil\frac{e}{d}\right\rceil=\left\lceil e^{\prime}\right\rceil.

Hence
\tilde{\psi}_{t}
also belongs to
G_{\theta_{\rho},e^{\prime}}
.

Since
\psi
and
\phi
are related by a conjugation in
G(L)
by
\Delta(\omega)
,
\psi
like
\phi
can also be described by
\psi_{r}
and
\psi_{t}
uniquely. So the same holds for
\tilde{\psi}
. Hence since
\tilde{\psi}_{r}
and
\tilde{\psi}_{t}
belong to
G_{\theta_{\rho},e^{\prime}}
, therefore so does
\tilde{\psi}\in G_{\theta_{\rho},e^{\prime}}
.

For
\underline{e\geq 1}
,
(1)
(1)

(1)

For
\underline{e=0}
, we get an isomorphism

(8.20)

j\colon\mathfrak U\longrightarrow\mathfrak B

of the big cell
\mathfrak U
of
{\tt U}_{E}
with that of
G_{\theta_{\rho}}
, which we will denote by
\mathfrak B
.
we get the following isomorphism of groups in the spirit of
[
BS15
, Theorem 2.3.1]
:

(8.21)

{\tt U}^{(e)}_{E}\simeq G_{\theta_{\rho},e^{\prime}}.

Combining these with (
8.14
) and (
8.15
), we deduce that for
\underline{e\geq 1}
,

p_{*}^{\sf\mu_{d}}\left(\mathfrak{G}_{E}^{(e)}\right)(A)=\mathfrak{G}_{E}^{(e)}(B)^{\sf\mu_{d}}={\tt U}^{(e)}_{E}\simeq G_{\theta_{\rho},e^{\prime}}\simeq\mathfrak{G}_{\theta_{\rho},e^{\prime}}(A).\qed

Remark 8.3
.

Note that the existence and defining properties for
BT
-group schemes which realize Moy–Prasad groups were constructed in
[
Yu15
]
. They arise by enlarging the definition of concave functions and constructing
BT
group schemes associated to them. We observe that the above realization of these group schemes as
invariant direct images
can be interpreted as giving a different construction of these group schemes, namely as invariant direct images of congruence subgroup schemes from ramified covers. We have done this in the simple case field of characteristic zero, and this can be extended to “good” characteristics for the group
G
.

8.6. The
n
-Moy–Prasad-group scheme

The proof of Theorem
5.7
does not quite apply as it is. Unlike the scenario of Theorem
5.7
, on the Kawamata cover we no longer have a constant group scheme with fibre
G
but have
congruence subgroup schemes
.

Theorem 8.4
.

We have an
n-Moy--Prasad
-group scheme
\mathfrak{G}_{(\boldsymbol{\Theta},\bf e^{\prime})}
which is smooth and affine on
\spec(\mathcal{O})
which is given by the data of
(\boldsymbol{\Theta},{\bf e}^{\prime}):=\big((\theta_{1},e^{\prime}_{1}),\ldots,(\theta_{n},e^{\prime}_{n})\big)
. It satisfies the properties in Theorem
1.3
.

Proof.

For the given data
(\boldsymbol{\Theta},{\bf e}^{\prime}):=\big((\theta_{1},e^{\prime}_{1}),\ldots,(\theta_{n},e^{\prime}_{n})\big)
, under the assumptions of Section
8.1
, setting
\boldsymbol{\Theta}=(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{n})
, we have a Kawamata cover
q\colon Z\to{\bf A}
which recovers the
n
-parahoric group scheme
\mathfrak{G}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
on
{\bf A}
as the invariant push-forward
q^{\Gamma}_{*}(\mathcal{H})
, where
\mathcal{H}
is a group scheme on
Z
with fibres
G
. Recall that
q
is ramified with varying ramification indices
d_{i}
. In fact, by our tameness hypothesis, the ramification indices
d_{i}
are such that
e_{i}:=d_{i}e_{i}^{\prime}
are positive integers which are coprime to the residue characteristic. Without loss of generality, we may suppose that the
e_{i}
are
increasing with
i
.

Set
e_{0}=0
.
Now for
j
going from
1
to
n
, we use the main result in
[
MRR20
]
to construct an iterated dilatation of
\mathcal{H}
along each component
q^{*}(\cup_{i\geq j}D_{i})_{\mathrm{red}}
of the reduced divisor in
Z
thickened by
e_{j}-e_{j-1}
. We get a new smooth, affine group scheme
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}
on
Z
. By the universal property of dilatation, it follows that
q^{\Gamma}_{*}(\tilde{\mathcal{H}})
is the required
n-Moy--Prasad
-group scheme on
{\bf A}
. It is smooth and affine since
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}
is so and because the invariant direct image functor preserves these properties.
∎

9. Schematization in the mixed-characteristic case

Let
(\mathcal{O},\mathfrak m)
be a complete discrete valuation ring with residue field
k
of characteristic
p
and uniformizer
z
. We want to extend all of the results of the previous sections to the mixed-characteristics case. This will be possible under the assumptions of Section
2.2
.

9.1. Extension of
[
BS15
, Theorem 2.3.1 and Proposition 5.1.2]

Let
\theta\in\mathcal{A}_{T}
be a rational point satisfying the assumptions of Section
2.2
. As in Section
2.2
, we assume that
d
is coprime to
\charr(k)
such that
d\theta\in Y(T)
.
Consider the Eisenstein polynomial
f(x)=x^{d}-z\in A[x]
.
Let
B:=\mathcal{O}[x]/f(x)
. Thus
z
is totally ramified in
B
, and the residue field
k_{B}
has
d
distinct roots of unity. Let
\varpi
be a uniformizer in
B
such that
\varpi^{d}=z
. Let
\zeta
be a primitive
d^{\mathrm{th}}
root of unity in
B
.

Let
K
be its quotient field of
\mathcal{O}
and
L
the quotient field of
B
. We view
N:=\spec(B)
as a Galois cover over
D:=\spec(\mathcal{O})
with
\Gal(N/D)={\sf\mu_{d}}
. Let
p\colon N\to D
be the quotient morphism. Let
E
be the trivial principal
G
-bundle
N\times G
together with the twisted
\mu_{d}
-action as in (
8.1
). This makes
E
into a
(\mu_{d},G)
-bundle on
N
. Let
\mathfrak{G}_{E}
be its adjoint group scheme (see (
8.4
)). Let
\mathfrak{G}_{\theta_{\rho}}
be the Bruhat–Tits group scheme over
\mathcal{O}
associated to it as in Theorem
8.2
. Analogously to (
1.2
), it will be convenient to write

(9.1)

{\tt{P}}_{\theta}=\left\langle T(\mathcal{O}),\,U_{r}\left(z^{m_{r}(\theta)}\mathcal{O}\right),r\in\Phi\right\rangle

in both the equal- and mixed-characteristic cases. This abuse of notation in the mixed-characteristic case should not cause any confusion because of the structure theorem for complete discrete valuation rings.

Following the discussion in Section
8
under the tameness assumptions, let us prove the required generalization of
[
BS15
, Theorem 2.3.1]
, which is Theorem
8.2
for
e^{\prime}=0
.

Theorem 9.1
.

We have an isomorphism of group schemes

(9.2)

p_{*}^{\sf\mu_{d}}\left(\mathfrak{G}_{E}\right)\simeq\mathfrak{G}_{\theta_{\rho}}.

Proof.

Let us state at the outset that by
[
BT84a
, Section 1, p. 13]
, the references we will cite are valid for an infinite field
K
containing a subring
A
such that
K
is its quotient field.

The
\mathcal{O}
-group scheme
\mathfrak{G}_{\theta_{\rho}}
is connected and smooth because it is a parahoric group scheme. Weil restriction of scalars followed by taking invariants preserves connectedness. Further, Weil restrictions of smooth group schemes are smooth. Moreover, under the tameness assumptions, it is easily checked that the fixed-point subscheme is also smooth. Thus, the
\mathcal{O}
-group scheme
p_{*}^{\sf\mu_{d}}(\mathfrak{G}_{E})
is connected and smooth. As in (
8.14
), the restriction of
p_{*}^{\sf\mu_{d}}(\mathfrak{G}_{E})
to
\spec(K)
consists of
\phi\colon\spec(L)\rightarrow G
satisfying the

{\sf\mu_{d}}
-equivariance;
i.e.

(9.3)

\phi(\gamma u)=\rho(\gamma)\phi(u)\rho(\gamma)^{-1},\quad u\in\spec(L),\gamma\in{\sf\mu_{d}}.

Conjugate
\phi
by
\Delta
(see (
8.9
)), as is also done in (
8.16
); the conjugate
\Delta^{-1}\phi\Delta
descends to a rational function
\tilde{\psi}\colon\spec(K)\rightarrow G
as in (
8.17
). This defines on
\spec(K)
a morphism

(9.4)

j_{K}\colon p_{*}^{\sf\mu_{d}}\left(\mathfrak{G}_{E}\right)|_{K}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{G}_{\theta_{\rho}}|_{K}.

By evaluating on sections, it may be verified that
j_{K}
is an isomorphism.

Recall that in the proof of Theorem
8.2
, we defined the big cell
\mathfrak U
of the unit group
{\tt U}_{E}
(see (
8.2
)) to be those
\phi\colon\spec(B)\rightarrow G
whose image lies in the big cell
G^{\circ}
of
G
. This defines the big cell of
p_{*}^{\sf\mu_{d}}(\mathfrak{G}_{E})
. We will denote it as
\mathfrak U
as well. Taking
\mathfrak U
to be the open neighbourhood of the identity of
p_{*}^{\sf\mu_{d}}(\mathfrak{G}_{E})
, we exhibited in (
8.20
) the isomorphism
j\colon\mathfrak U\rightarrow\mathfrak B
onto the big cell
\mathfrak B
of
\mathfrak{G}_{\theta_{\rho}}
.

Now by
[
BT84a
, Section 1.2.13]
,
j_{K}
extends uniquely to an
\mathcal{O}
-morphism

j\colon p_{*}^{\sf\mu_{d}}\left(\mathfrak{G}_{E}\right)\longrightarrow\mathfrak{G}_{\theta_{\rho}}

of group schemes. Since
j
restrict to an isomorphism from
\mathfrak U
to
\mathfrak B
between big cells, by
[
BT84a
, Section 1.2.14]
, it follows that
j
is an isomorphism of
p_{*}^{\sf\mu_{d}}(\mathfrak{G}_{E})
onto an open subgroup scheme of
\mathfrak{G}_{\theta_{\rho}}
. Thus
j
is an isomorphism because
\mathfrak{G}_{\theta_{\rho}}
is connected.
∎

Theorem
9.1
has two consequences. Firstly,
[
BS15
, Proposition 5.1.2]
, recalled in (
5.9
), generalizes following
[
BS15
, Sections 4 and 5]
.
Secondly, Theorem
8.2
extends without any extra conditions imposed by
e^{\prime}
. This is so because in the proof of Theorem
8.2
, we only need to have distinct roots of unity corresponding to the point
\theta
in the apartment.

Remark 9.2
.

We observe that for the results of Sections
5
,
6
and
7
, the assumption of algebraic closedness of the residue field
k
is not essential. The existence of a Kawamata cover (see Section
A.1
) is needed only for affine spaces, and this can be achieved by hand under the assumptions of Section
2.2
. We however always assume that
k
is
perfect
so as to be able to apply
[
BT84a
]
. One needs to have extensions
k_{B}
as above with the desired roots of unity for the above extensions of
[
BS15
, Theorem 2.3.1 and Proposition 5.1.2]
. This is possible under the assumptions of Section
2.2
.

9.2. The extension of Theorem
5.7

In this subsection, we denote the uniformizer
z
of
\mathcal{O}
by
x_{0}
. Let
\mathbf{A}_{\mathcal{O}}:=\mathbb{A}^{n}_{\mathcal{O}}=\spec(\mathcal{O}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}])
.
For
0\leq i\leq n
, let
H_{i}
be the coordinate hyperplanes defined by the vanishing of
x_{i}
, and let
\zeta_{i}
be their generic points.

(1)

If we let
(X,D)=({\mathbf{A}_{\mathcal{O}}},H)
, then the analogue of Proposition
5.6
holds without any change for an arbitrary
(n+1)-concave
-function
{\bf f}
.
This statement holds for any characteristic of the residue field
. In the following we denote the Lie algebra bundle by
\mathcal{R}
.

(2)

Observe that since
\mathcal{O}
is assumed to be complete, the formal power series ring
{\mathcal{O}}\llbracket x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rrbracket
is the completion of
\mathcal{O}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
at
\mathfrak m[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
. We begin with a natural extension of the notion of an
(n+1)-bounded group
associated to an
(n+1)-concave function

{\bf f}
on the root system
\Phi
of
G
by defining

(9.5)

{\tt{P}}_{\bf f}:=\left\langle T\left({\mathcal{O}}\llbracket x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rrbracket\right),\,U_{r}\left(\prodop\displaylimits_{0\leq i\leq n}x_{i}^{f_{i}(r)}{\mathcal{O}}\llbracket x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rrbracket\right),r\in\Phi\right\rangle.

(3)

Suppose we are given an ordered
(n+1)
-tuple
(\Sigma_{0},\ldots,\Sigma_{n})
of facets of the apartment
\mathcal{A}_{T}
. Then by Section
2.2
, we may choose a point
\boldsymbol{\Theta}:=(\theta_{0},\ldots,\theta_{n})
in
(\Sigma_{0},\ldots,\Sigma_{n})
such that if
d_{i}
is the minimal integer such that
d_{i}\theta_{i}
lies in
Y(T)
, then each
d_{i}
is coprime to
\charr(k)
and
k
contains primitive
d_{i}^{\mathrm{th}}
roots of unity. More generally, suppose we are given a point
\boldsymbol{\Theta}:=(\theta_{0},\ldots,\theta_{n})\in\mathcal{A}_{T}^{n}
satisfying the assumptions in Section
2.2
. Let
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}
be a complete discrete valuation ring containing
\mathcal{O}
where
x_{0}
ramifies to order a multiple of
d_{0}
and that contains the primitive
d_{i}^{\mathrm{th}}
roots of unity for
0\leq i\leq n
.

(4)

Let
X^{\prime}\subset\mathbf{A}_{\mathcal{O}}
denote the open subset of height
1
prime ideals as in the paragraph after (
5.12
). Taking
{\bf f}
to be the concave function determined by
\theta
in item (
1
), let
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
denote the restriction of
\mathcal{R}
to
X^{\prime}
.

(5)

We are now in the setting of Theorem
5.4
with the only difference that
\mathbf{A}_{\mathcal{O}}
replaces
\mathbb{A}^{n+1}_{k}
.

(6)

Let
\bf{B}_{\tilde{\mathcal{O}}}:=\spec(\tilde{\mathcal{O}}[y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}])
, where
y_{i}^{d_{i}}=x_{i}
. Let
q\colon\mathbf{B}_{\tilde{\mathcal{O}}}\rightarrow\mathbf{A}_{\mathcal{O}}
be the natural projection map with Galois group
\Gamma
. This plays the role of an explicit Kawamata covering.
We set
\mathbf{A}_{0}:=\mathbf{A}_{\mathcal{O}}\setminus H
,
\mathbf{B}_{0}:=q^{-1}(\mathbf{A}_{0})
,
Z^{\prime}:=q^{-1}(X^{\prime})
and
\tilde{X}_{i}:=q^{-1}(X_{i})
. Let
q_{i}\colon\tilde{X}_{i}\rightarrow X_{i}
be the restriction of
q
. Let
\Gamma_{i}
denote the Galois group of
q_{i}
. We are now in the setting of Section
5.3
.

(7)

By applying Section
9.1
to the uniformizer
x_{0}
and the generalization of
[
BS15
, Proposition 5.1.2]
for the rest of the height
1
primes given by the variables
x_{j}
, we get a smooth, affine group scheme
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
on
Z^{\prime}
such that the Weil restrictions gives
q^{\Gamma}_{*}(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})=\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
on
X^{\prime}
.

(8)

Let
V^{\prime}=\Lie(\mathcal{H}^{\prime})
be the Lie algebra bundle on
Z^{\prime}
as in the discussion after (
5.14
). Then,
V^{\prime}
is a Lie algebra bundle with fibre type
\mathfrak g
. Furthermore, the Cartan decomposition on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
lifts to give an equivariant Cartan decomposition of the Lie algebra bundle
V^{\prime}
on
Z^{\prime}
. Hence its reflexive closure
V
also gets an equivariant structure and is also locally free.

(9)

As in the proof of Theorem
5.4
, now that we have the Lie algebra bundle
V
with fibre
\mathfrak g
, we get a group scheme
\mathcal{H}
on
\mathbf{B}_{\tilde{\mathcal{O}}}
and, by taking invariant direct images,
the group scheme
\mathfrak{G}:=q^{\Gamma}_{*}(\mathcal{H})
extending
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
to the whole of
\mathbf{A}_{\mathcal{O}}
. This proves the extension of Theorem
5.7
in the setting of mixed characteristics with the tameness assumptions.

(10)

We also note that as in Proposition
5.5
, the big cell structure also descends to give one for
\mathfrak{G}
on the whole of
\mathbf{A}_{\mathcal{O}}
.

(11)

Finally, we note that the arguments in Theorem
5.7
for the uniqueness of the group scheme carry over to the mixed-characteristic situation as well.

9.3. The main theorem for concave functions

By Section
9.2
(
1
),
we see that there is a Lie algebra bundle on
(\mathbf{A}_{\mathcal{O}},H)
associated to an
(n+1)-concave
function on
\Phi
. The results in Section
6
now work under the tameness assumptions, see Section
1.1
, or more generally under the assumptions on
\charr(k)
of Section
2.2
. We therefore get the extension of Theorem
1.3
to
(\mathbf{A}_{\mathcal{O}},H)
(see Remark
6.11
). Note that this is for the groups
G=\SL(n),G_{2},F_{4},E_{6}
.

For the rest of the groups, by the arguments in Section
7
, we need a faithful representation
G\hookrightarrow\GL(V)
to start with, and so if we choose a faithful representation of minimal dimension
m(G)
, then for these groups, we need to choose
p
to be coprime to
m(G)
and bigger than the Coxeter number
{\tt h}_{G}
of
G
(see Section
7
). The numbers
m(G)
are known and can be obtained from the dimension formulae of the fundamental representations. They are as follows:
m(E_{6})=27
,
m(E_{7})=56
and for
B_{n}
,
C_{n}
and
D_{n}
,
m(G)
is
2n+1
,
2n
and
2n
, respectively. In summary, we have completed the proof of Theorem
1.6
.

9.4. The example of a
2BT
-group scheme in the work of Pappas and Zhu
[
PZ13
]

As we mentioned in the introduction, an example of a
2BT
-group scheme comes up in
[
PZ13
, Theorem 4.1]
.

There are broadly two parts to
[
PZ13
, Theorem 4.1]
, the first being the existence of the
2BT
-group scheme on
\mathbb A^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
and second a proof that the resulting group scheme is
affine
. Although
[
PZ13
, Theorem 4.1]
states the case of a parahoric group scheme along one axis, the proof considers the more general case of the
2
-concave function
{\bf f}=(0,f)
, where
f
is a concave function on the root system
\Phi
. The proof of the affineness follows ideas from
[
Yu15
]
. We are unable to comprehend it when
f
is given by a non-hyperspecial point of the apartment and
G
is split.

We first briefly describe the setting in
[
PZ13
, Section 4]
so as to place their result in our context. We will then indicate how this result can be derived as a special case of our results. We note however that the setting in
[
PZ13
]
is for the general case of a quasi-split group
G
, while we consider only the split case.

In the notation of Section
9.2
above, the setting in
[
PZ13
]
is for the case
n=1
,
i.e.
when
x_{1}=u
. The axes we consider correspond to setting
x_{0}
and
x_{1}
as zero (here by
x_{0}=0
we mean going modulo the uniformizer), but Pappas–Zhu consider the axes given by setting
u
as the uniformizer
\varpi
and by setting
\varpi=0
. The result
[
PZ13
, Theorem 4.1]
is stated for the case when the assignment along the
x_{0}
-axis is the split group (see
[
PZ13
, Section 4.2.2(a)]
), while the assignment in the diagonal
(\varpi=u)
-direction is given by a parahoric group associated to a point
\theta
of the affine apartment. Thus, in our setting this case corresponds to the
2
-concave function which is
{\bf f}=(0,f)
, where
f
is a concave function
f_{\theta}
on the root system
\Phi
along the
u
-direction and the
0
-concave function gives the split group scheme along the
\varpi
-direction. Observe that this gives the concave function
f=0+f
in the
(u=\varpi)
-direction.

The key point is that only
a single
concave function
f
is involved. By
[
BT84a
, Section 4.5.4]
, one obtains a schematic root datum coming from
f
. We now work in the setting of
[
BT84a
, Section 3.9.4]
. Once a faithful representation
G\hookrightarrow\GL(V)
is fixed, the schematic root datum arising out of the choice of
f
entails the choice of a lattice
M\subset V_{K}
. The arguments in
[
BT84a
, Section 3.9.4]
furnishes the group scheme
\mathcal{G}_{\theta_{0},f}
(see
[
PZ13
, p. 175, second paragraph]
). Thus, the
2BT
-group scheme over
\mathbb A^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
which comes up in
[
PZ13
]
is obtained as a direct consequence of
[
BT84a
, Section 3.9.4]
. However, the method of construction of
[
BT84a
, Section 3.9.4 and Proposition 2.2.10]
gives
a priori
only a
quasi-affine group scheme
. So the burden of
[
PZ13
, Section 4]
lies in proving the
affineness
of the group scheme
\mathcal{G}_{\theta_{0},f}
. We emphasize that all this is for a single concave function.

We now compare this with the results of the present paper. Firstly,
[
PZ13
, Theorem 4.1]
as stated is for the
2
-concave function
(0,f_{\theta})
, and this is a special case of Theorem
5.7
as described in Section
9.2
. Thus we derive directly both the
existence and affineness
of the required group scheme and in the more general context of an
(n+1)
-concave function
\bf f
over
\mathbb A^{n}_{\mathcal{O}}
which comes by prescribing
n+1
points in the affine apartment. We note however that the methods in the present paper force certain mild tameness assumptions.

Part 3.  Some applications in the
\boldsymbol{\charr(k)=0}
equicharacteristic case

10.
BT
-group schemes on wonderful embeddings in
\boldsymbol{\charr(k)=0}

10.1. The wonderful compactification
\bf X

Let
{\bf X}:=\overline{G_{\mathrm{ad}}}
be the wonderful compactification of
G_{\mathrm{ad}}
.
Let
\{D_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in S\}
denote the irreducible smooth
divisors
of
{\bf X}
. Let
D:=\cup_{\alpha\in S}D_{\alpha}
. Then
{\bf X}\setminus G_{\mathrm{ad}}=D
. The pair
({\bf X},D)
is the primary example of a
(G_{\mathrm{ad}}\times G_{\mathrm{ad}})
-homogenous pair; see
[
Bri07
]
.
Let
P_{I}
be the standard parabolic subgroup defined by subsets
I\subset S
, the notation being such that the Levi subgroup
L_{I,\mathrm{ad}}
containing
T_{\mathrm{ad}}
has root system with basis
S\setminus I
.
Recall that the
(G_{\mathrm{ad}}\times G_{\mathrm{ad}})
-orbits in
{\bf X}
are indexed by subsets
I\subset S
and have the following description:

Z_{I}=\left(G_{\mathrm{ad}}\times G_{\mathrm{ad}}\right)\times_{P_{I}\times P^{-}_{I}}L_{I,\mathrm{ad}}.

Then by
[
Bri98
, Proposition A1]
, each
Z_{I}
contains a unique base point
z_{I}
such that
(B\times B^{-})\cdot z_{I}
is dense in
Z_{I}
and there is a
1
-PS
\lambda
of
T
satisfying
P_{I}=P(\lambda)
and
\lim_{t\to 0}\lambda(t)=z_{I}
. The closures of these
\lambda
define curves
C_{I}\subset{\bf X}
which meet the strata
Z_{I}
transversally at
z_{I}
. In particular, if
I=\{\alpha\}
is a singleton, then the divisor
D_{\alpha}
is the orbit closure
\bar{Z}_{I}
and the
1
-PS can be taken to be the fundamental co-weight
\omega^{\vee}_{\alpha}
. The closure of the
1
-PS
\omega^{\vee}_{\alpha}\colon\mathbb G_{m}\to G_{\mathrm{ad}}
defines the curve
C_{\alpha}\subset{\bf X}
transversal to the divisor
D_{\alpha}
at the point
z_{\alpha}
. For a non-empty
I\subset S
, the
\lambda
defining

C_{I}
can be taken to be
\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha\in I}\omega^{\vee}_{\alpha}
.

10.2. The
\ell{\tt BT}
-group scheme
{\mathfrak G}_{\bf X}^{\varpi}
on
\bf X

Before stating the main result of this section, we make a few remarks which might help the reader. Let
{\bf Y_{0}}\subset{\bf Y}
the affine toric varieties associated to the Weyl chamber and the fan of Weyl chambers. The basic underlying principle in these constructions
is that the combinatorial data encoded in the triple consisting of the Weyl chamber, the fan of Weyl chambers and the Tits building is geometrically replicated by the inclusion
{\bf Y_{0}}\subset{\bf Y}\subset{\bf X}
. The “wonderful” Bruhat–Tits group scheme which arises on
{\bf X}
has its local Weyl-chamber model on the affine toric variety
{\bf Y}_{0}
. Indeed, in this case, the Kawamata cover is even explicit; see Remark
4.6
. In particular, the theorem below can be executed for
{\bf Y}_{0}
, but this will give the group scheme associated to the data coming from the Weyl chamber alone.

We now state a consequence of our main theorem in the context of the wonderful compactification of
G_{\mathrm{ad}}
which is a primary example of a smooth variety with normal crossing divisors.

Theorem 10.1
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\Theta}=(\theta_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in S)
, where the
\theta_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{A}
are arbitrary points in the apartment of
T
satisfying conditions of Section
2.2
. There exists an affine “wonderful” Bruhat–Tits group scheme
{\mathfrak G}_{\bf X}^{\varpi}
on
{\bf X}
satisfying the following classifying properties:

(1)

At the height
1
primes associated to the canonical boundary divisors associated to the maximal parabolic subgroups
P_{\alpha}\subset G
,
\alpha\in S
, the group scheme
{\mathfrak G}_{\bf X}^{\varpi}
is isomorphic to the parahoric group scheme
\mathfrak{G}_{\theta_{\alpha}}
.

(2)

For
\emptyset\neq I\subset S
, the restriction of
{\mathfrak G}_{\bf X}^{\varpi}
to the formal neighbourhood
U_{I}
of
z_{I}
in the coordinate curves
C_{I}
, see Section
10.1
, is isomorphic to the Bruhat–Tits group scheme associated to the concave function
{\text{\linespread m}_{{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{I}}}\colon\Phi\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
given by
r\mapsto\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha\in I}m_{r}(\theta_{\alpha})
.

(3)

Let
\Phi_{\theta}:=\{r\in\Phi|r(\theta)\in\mathbb{Z}\}
.
The closed fibre of
\mathfrak{G}_{\text{\linespread m}_{{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{I}}}
modulo its unipotent radical has root system generated by the intersection of the
\Phi_{\theta_{\alpha}}
for
\alpha\in I
.

Proof.

By Proposition
5.6
, we have a Lie algebra bundle
\mathcal{R}
on
{\bf X}
prescribed by the first property. Thus by Theorem
5.4
, we get a group scheme
{\mathfrak G}_{\bf X}^{\varpi}
on
{\bf X}
which by construction satisfies the first property. The context of the second property is that of formal neighbourhoods, which is also that of Section
5.8
. As remarked before the theorem, the group scheme constructed on
{\bf X}
has a local Weyl-chamber model on the affine toric variety
{\bf Y}_{0}
. Thus we may restrict to
{\bf Y}_{0}
. Hence the second property follows from Theorem
5.7
. Recall, see
[
BT84a
, Corollaire 4.6.12]
, that the reductive quotient of the closed fibre of
\mathfrak{G}_{f}
has root system given by
\{r\in\Phi\mid\lceil f\rceil(-r)=-\lceil f\rceil(r)\}
. For the case of the concave function
m_{r}(\theta)
, this set is
\Phi_{\theta}
, and for
\text{\linespread m}_{{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{I}}
, it will be the intersection of the
\Phi_{\theta_{\alpha}}
for
\alpha\in I
.
∎

Corollary 10.2
.

Let
\theta_{\alpha}
be the non-zero vertices of the Weyl alcove (
2.1
). Let
L
be the LCM of the coefficients of the highest root and
\ell
the semisimple rank of
G
.
Then setting
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
as
(\theta_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in S)
,
(\theta_{\alpha}/(\ell+1)\mid\alpha\in S)
and

(L\theta_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in S)
gives a group scheme whose
restriction to every coordinate curve
C_{I}
has closed fibre with
root system generated by the highest root
\alpha_{0}
and
S\setminus I
, or simply generated by
S\setminus I
, or equal to that of
G
,
respectively.

Proof.

The structure of the set
\{r\in\Phi\mid\lceil f\rceil(-r)=-\lceil f\rceil(r)\}
and the formula (
2.1
) show that the root system of the reductive quotient of the closed fibre equals the set of roots whose simple root coefficients, coming from the subset
I
of
S
, equal an integral multiple of the corresponding term of the highest root. In the first case, this is the set of roots generated by the highest root
\alpha_{0}
and
S\setminus I
. In the second case, it is the set of roots generated by
S\setminus I
. In the third case, all roots in
\Phi
satisfy this property.
∎

10.3. The wonderful embedding
{\bf X}^{\mathrm{aff}}

We continue to use the notation as in previous sections. Let
G^{\mathrm{aff}}
denote the Kac–Moody group associated to the affine Dynkin diagram of
G
. Recall that
G^{\mathrm{aff}}
is given by a central extension of
L^{\ltimes}G
by
\mathbb{G}_{m}
. Analogously to the wonderful compactification of
G_{\mathrm{ad}}
, Solis in
[
Sol17
]
has constructed a wonderful embedding
{\bf X}^{\mathrm{aff}}
for
G^{\mathrm{aff}}_{\mathrm{ad}}:=G^{\mathrm{aff}}/Z(G^{\mathrm{aff}})=\mathbb{G}_{m}\ltimes\LG/Z(G)
. It is an ind-scheme containing
G^{\mathrm{aff}}_{\mathrm{ad}}
as a dense open ind-scheme and carrying an equivariant action of
L^{\ltimes}G\times L^{\ltimes}G
.

Let
T_{\mathrm{ad}}:=T/Z(G)
and
T^{\ltimes}_{\mathrm{ad}}:=\mathbb{G}_{m}\times T_{\mathrm{ad}}\subset G^{\mathrm{aff}}_{\mathrm{ad}}
, where
\mathbb{G}_{m}
is the rotational torus. In
{\bf X}^{\mathrm{aff}}
, the closure
{\bf Y}^{\mathrm{aff}}:=\overline{T^{\ltimes}_{\mathrm{ad}}}
gives a torus embedding. It is covered by the affine Weyl group
W^{\mathrm{aff}}
-translates of the affine torus embedding
{\bf Y}_{0}^{\mathrm{aff}}:=\overline{T^{\ltimes}_{\mathrm{ad},0}}\simeq\mathbb{A}^{\ell+1}
given by the negative Weyl alcove.

10.4. On the torus embedding
{\bf Y}_{0}^{\mathrm{aff}}

Recall that
Z:={\bf Y}_{0}^{\mathrm{aff}}\setminus T^{\ltimes}_{\mathrm{ad}}
is a union
\cup_{\alpha\in\mathbb S}H_{\alpha}
of
\ell+1
standard coordinate hyperplanes meeting at normal crossings. For
\alpha\in\mathbb S
, let
\zeta_{\alpha}
denote the generic point of the divisor
H_{\alpha}
. Let

(10.1)

A_{\alpha}=\mathcal{O}_{{\bf Y}_{0}^{\mathrm{aff}},\zeta_{\alpha}}

be the DVRs obtained by localizing at the height
1
primes given by the
\zeta_{\alpha}
. Let
K_{\alpha}
be the quotient field of
A_{\alpha}
. Let
Y_{\alpha}:=\spec(A_{\alpha})
. Note that we can identify the open subset
\spec(K_{\alpha})
with
{T^{\ltimes}_{\mathrm{ad}}}\cap Y_{\alpha}
. Let

(10.2)

Y^{\prime}:={T^{\ltimes}_{\mathrm{ad}}}\cup_{\alpha}Y_{\alpha}.

The complement
{\bf Y}_{0}^{\mathrm{aff}}\setminus Y^{\prime}
can again be realized as a colimit of open subsets of
{\bf Y}_{0}^{\mathrm{aff}}
whose codimension is at least
2
in
{\bf Y}_{0}^{\mathrm{aff}}
.

10.5. Construction of a finite-dimensional Lie algebra bundle
J
on
{\bf Y}_{0}^{\mathrm{aff}}
together with parabolic structures

This construction is exactly analogous to the construction of
\mathcal{R}
on
{\bf Y}
in Section
4
. For
\alpha\in\mathbb{S}
, let
\theta_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{A}
be arbitrary points in the apartment satisfying the conditions of Section
2.2
. We let
T^{\ltimes}_{\mathrm{ad}}
,
{\bf Y}_{0}^{\mathrm{aff}}
,
(1,\omega^{\vee}_{\alpha})
and
\mathbb{S}
play the roles of
{\bf A}_{0}
,
{\bf A}
,
\lambda_{i}
and
\{1,\ldots,n\}
. More precisely, we set

(10.3)

\eta_{\alpha}:=\left((0,\ldots,1,\ldots,0),\theta_{\alpha}\right),\quad\alpha\in\mathbb{S}

with the unique
1
in the
\alpha^{\mathrm{th}}
coordinate. Consider the loop rotation action given by (
2.24
) and (
2.25
). We may prove the following theorem exactly like Theorem
4.4
.

Theorem 10.3
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\Theta}:=(\theta_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in\mathbb{S})
, where the
\theta_{\alpha}
are arbitrary points in the apartment
\mathcal{A}
of
T
satisfying the conditions of Section
2.2
. Let
U
be a formal neighbourhood of the origin in
{\bf Y}_{0}^{\mathrm{aff}}
. There exists a canonical Lie algebra bundle
J
on
{\bf Y}_{0}^{\mathrm{aff}}
which extends the trivial bundle with fibre
\mathfrak g
on
{T^{\ltimes}_{\mathrm{ad}}}\subset{\bf Y}^{\mathrm{aff}}_{0}
, and with notation as in (
2.30
), we have the identification of functors from the category of
k
-algebras to
k
-Lie algebras

(10.4)

L^{+}\left(\mathfrak{P}_{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}\right)=L^{+}\left(\mathcal{R}\mid_{U}\right).

We may prove the following corollary like Corollary
4.5
.

Corollary 10.4
.

Let
\lambda=\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha\in\mathbb{S}}k_{\alpha}(1,\theta_{\alpha})
be a non-zero dominant
1
-PS of
T_{\mathrm{ad}}^{\ltimes}
where not all
k_{\alpha}
are zero.
Let
\mathcal{D}
be the formal neighbourhood of origin in
{\bf Y}_{0}^{\mathrm{aff}}
associated to the curve defined by
\lambda
. Let
f\colon\Phi\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be the concave function defined by the assignment
r\mapsto\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha\in\mathbb{S}}k_{\alpha}m_{r}(\theta_{\alpha})
. Then for any
k
-algebra
R
, we have

(10.5)

L^{+}\left(\mathcal{R}\mid_{\mathcal{D}}\right)(R)=\left\langle\mathfrak{t}(R\llbracket t\rrbracket),\,\mathfrak{u}_{r}\left(t^{f(r)}R\llbracket t\rrbracket\right),r\in\Phi\right\rangle.

Corollary 10.5
.

The Lie algebra bundle
J_{{\bf Y}^{\mathrm{aff}}}
gets canonical parabolic structures
(
see the appendix
)
at the generic points
\xi_{\alpha}
of the
W^{\mathrm{aff}}
-translates of the divisors
H_{\alpha}\subset{\bf Y}_{0}^{\mathrm{aff}}
,
\alpha\in\mathbb S
.

Proof.

We prescribe a ramification index
d_{\alpha}
on the divisor
H_{\alpha}
such that
d_{\alpha}\theta_{\alpha}
belongs to
Y(T)
. (Thus in the case when the
d_{\alpha}
are the alcove vertices, see (
2.1
), the
d_{\alpha}
are given by (
2.2
)). Then the identification (
5.10
) of the Lie algebra structures of
J_{{\bf Y}^{\mathrm{aff}}}
and the parahoric Lie algebra structures on the localizations of the generic points of
H_{\alpha}
allows us to endow parabolic structures at the generic points of the divisors.∎

10.6. The parahoric group scheme on the torus embedding
{\bf Y}^{\mathrm{aff}}

Theorem 10.6
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\Theta}=(\theta_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in S)
, where the
\theta_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{A}
are arbitrary points in the apartment of
T
satisfying conditions of Section
2.2
. There exist an affine and smooth “wonderful” Bruhat–Tits group scheme
{\mathfrak G}_{{\bf Y}^{\mathrm{aff}}}^{\varpi}
on
{\bf Y}
together with a canonical isomorphism
\Lie({\mathfrak G}_{{\bf Y}^{\mathrm{aff}}}^{\varpi})\simeq J
. It further satisfies the following classifying property:

For any point
h\in{\bf Y}^{\mathrm{aff}}\setminus T^{\ltimes}_{ad}
, let
\mathbb I\subset\mathbb S
be a subset such that
h\in\cap_{\alpha\in\mathbb I}H_{\alpha}
. Let
C_{\mathbb I}\subset{\bf Y}^{\mathrm{aff}}
be a smooth curve with generic point in
T^{\ltimes}_{\mathrm{ad}}
and closed point
h
. Let
U_{h}\subset C_{\mathbb I}
be a formal neighbourhood of
h
. Then, the restriction
{\mathfrak G}_{{\bf Y}^{\mathrm{aff}}}^{\varpi}|_{U_{h}}
is isomorphic to the Bruhat–Tits group scheme associated to the concave function
{\text{\linespread m}_{{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{I}}}\colon\Phi\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
given by
r\mapsto\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha\in I}m_{r}(\theta_{\alpha})
.

Proof.

Recall that
{{\bf Y}^{\mathrm{aff}}}
is covered by affine spaces
Y_{w}\simeq\mathbb{A}^{\ell+1}
parametrized by the affine Weyl group
W^{\mathrm{aff}}
. Each
Y_{w}
is a translate of
{{\bf Y}_{0}^{\mathrm{aff}}}
. The translates of the divisors
H_{\alpha}
meet each
Y_{w}
in the standard hyperplanes on
\mathbb{A}^{\ell+1}
, and thus, we can prescribe the same ramification data at the hyperplanes on each of the
Y_{w}
. On the other hand, although we have simple normal crossing singularities, we do not have an analogue of the Kawamata covering lemma for schemes such as
{{\bf Y}^{\mathrm{aff}}}
. The lemma is known only in the setting of quasi-projective schemes. So to construct the group scheme, we employ a different approach using the naturality of the constructions for gluing.

We observe firstly that the formalism of Kawamata coverings applies in the setting of the affine spaces
Y_{w}\subset{{\bf Y}^{\mathrm{aff}}}
. Let
p_{w}\colon Z_{w}\rightarrow Y_{w}
be the associated Kawamata cover (see Section
A.1
) with Galois group
\Gamma_{w}
. In Corollary
10.5
we observed that the Lie algebra bundle
J
has a canonical parabolic structure. Letting
Y_{w}
play the role of
{\bf Y_{0}}
and using all arguments in the proof of Theorem
10.1
, we obtain
\mathcal{H}_{w}\rightarrow Z_{w}
, which is a
\Gamma_{w}
-group scheme with fibres isomorphic to
G
whose invariant direct image is a group scheme
\mathfrak{G}_{w}
such that
\Lie(\mathfrak{G}_{w})=J|_{Y_{w}}
. The induced parabolic structure on
J|_{Y_{w}}
is the restriction of the one on
J
. Indeed, by Corollary
10.5
, these parabolic structures are essentially given at the local rings at the generic points
\xi_{\alpha}
of the divisors
H_{\alpha}
, and hence
these parabolic structures on
J
agree on the intersections
Y_{uv}:=Y_{u}\cap Y_{v}
.

Let
Z_{uv}:=p_{u}^{-1}(Y_{uv})
. Let
\tilde{Z}_{uv}
be the normalization of a component of
Z_{uv}\times_{Y_{uv}}Z_{vu}
. Then
\tilde{Z}_{uv}
serves as Kawamata cover (see Section
A.1
) of
Y_{uv}
(see
[
Vie95
, Corollary 2.6]
). We consider the morphisms
\tilde{Z}_{uv}\to Z_{u}
(resp.
\tilde{Z}_{uv}\to Z_{v}
) and let
\mathcal{H}_{u,\tilde{Z}}
(resp.
\mathcal{H}_{v,\tilde{Z}}
) denote the pull-backs of
\mathcal{H}_{u}
(resp.
\mathcal{H}_{v}
) to
\tilde{Z}_{uv}
.

Let
\Gamma
denote the Galois group for
\tilde{Z}_{uv}\rightarrow Y_{uv}
. Then by Lemma
5.1
, the invariant direct images of the equivariant Lie algebra bundles
\Lie(\mathcal{H}_{u,\tilde{Z}})
and
\Lie(\mathcal{H}_{v,\tilde{Z}})
coincide with the Lie algebra structure on
J
restricted to the
{Y_{uv}}
and also as isomorphic parabolic bundles. Therefore, we have a natural isomorphism of equivariant Lie algebra bundles

(10.6)

\Lie\left(\mathcal{H}_{u,\tilde{Z}}\right)\simeq\Lie\left(\mathcal{H}_{v,\tilde{Z}}\right).

As in the proof of Theorem
10.1
, this gives a canonical identification of the equivariant group schemes
\mathcal{H}_{u,\tilde{Z}}
and
\mathcal{H}_{v,\tilde{Z}}
on
\tilde{Z}_{uv}
. Since the invariant direct image of both the group schemes
\mathcal{H}_{u,\tilde{Z}}
and
\mathcal{H}_{v,\tilde{Z}}
are the restrictions

\mathfrak{G}_{u,{Y_{uv}}}
and
\mathfrak{G}_{v,{Y_{uv}}}
, it follows that on
Y_{uv}=Y_{u}\cap Y_{v}
we get a canonical identification of group schemes

(10.7)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{G}_{u,{Y_{uv}}}\simeq\mathfrak{G}_{v,{Y_{uv}}}.

These identifications are canonically induced from the gluing data of the Lie algebra bundle
J
for the cover
Y_{w}
. Therefore, the cocycle conditions are clearly satisfied, and the identifications (
10.7
) glue to give the group scheme
{\mathfrak G}_{{\bf Y}^{\mathrm{aff}}}^{\varpi}

on
{\bf Y}^{\mathrm{aff}}
. The verification of the classifying property follows exactly as in the proof of Theorem
10.1
.
∎

10.7. The
(\ell+1){\tt BT}
-group scheme on
{\bf X}^{\mathrm{aff}}

Let
{\bf X}^{\mathrm{aff}}
be as in Section
10.3
. The situation in this subsection is somewhat distinct from the previously discussed cases. Unlike the scheme
{\bf X}
, the space
{\bf X}^{\mathrm{aff}}
is an
ind-scheme
, and so we give the details of the construction of the group scheme.

We begin with a generality. Let
\mathbb{X}
be an ind-scheme. By an open subscheme
i\colon\mathbb{U}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{X}
, we mean an ind-scheme such that for any
f\colon\spec(A)\rightarrow\mathbb{X}
, the natural morphism
\mathbb{U}\times_{\mathbb{X}}\spec(A)\rightarrow\spec(A)
is an open immersion. For a sheaf
\mathbb{F}
on
\mathbb{U}
, by
i_{*}(\mathbb{F})
we mean the sheaf associated to the pre-sheaf on the “big site” of
\mathbb{X}
, whose sections on
f\colon\spec(A)\rightarrow\mathbb{X}
are given by
\mathbb{F}(\mathbb{U}\times_{\mathbb{X}}\spec(A))
.

The ind-scheme
{\bf X}^{\mathrm{aff}}
has a certain open subset
{\bf X}_{0}
whose precise definition is somewhat technical; see
[
Sol17
, Section 5.1, p. 705]
. Let us mention the properties relevant for us.

Recall that
{\bf X}^{\mathrm{aff}}=(G_{\mathrm{ad}}^{\mathrm{aff}}\times G_{\mathrm{ad}}^{\mathrm{aff}})\penalty\ {\bf X}_{0}
and in fact
{\bf X}^{\mathrm{aff}}=(G_{\mathrm{ad}}^{\mathrm{aff}}\times G_{\mathrm{ad}}^{\mathrm{aff}})\penalty\ {{\bf Y}^{\mathrm{aff}}}
. Further, the torus embedding
{\bf Y}^{\mathrm{aff}}
is covered by
{\bf Y}^{\mathrm{aff}}_{w}\simeq\mathbb A^{\ell+1}
, which are
W^{\mathrm{aff}}
-translates of
{\bf Y}^{\mathrm{aff}}_{0}
, where
{\bf Y}^{\mathrm{aff}}_{0}={{\bf Y}^{\mathrm{aff}}}\cap{\bf X}_{0}
. We remark that, analogously to the case of
{\bf Y}_{0}\subset{\bf Y}\subset{\bf X}
, just as the toric variety
{\bf Y}_{0}
was associated to the negative Weyl chamber, the toric variety
{\bf Y}^{\mathrm{aff}}_{0}
is associated to the negative Weyl alcove.

So let us also denote by
0
the neutral element of
W^{\mathrm{aff}}
. Let
U^{\pm}\subset B^{\pm}
be the unipotent subgroups. Let
\mathcal{U}^{\pm}:=\ev^{-1}(U^{\pm})
, where
\ev\colon G(\mathcal{O})\rightarrow G(k)
is the evaluation map. Further, by
[
Sol17
, Proposition 5.3]
,

(10.8)

{\bf X}_{0}=\mathcal{U}\times{\bf Y}^{\mathrm{aff}}_{0}\times\mathcal{U}^{-}.

Let
{\bf X}_{w}:=\mathcal{U}\times{\bf Y}^{\mathrm{aff}}_{w}\times\mathcal{U}^{-}
. These cover
\mathcal{U}\times{{\bf Y}^{\mathrm{aff}}}\times\mathcal{U}^{-}
. For
g\in G_{\mathrm{ad}}^{\mathrm{aff}}\times G_{\mathrm{ad}}^{\mathrm{aff}}
, let

(10.9)

{\bf X}_{g}:=g{\bf X}_{0},\quad{\bf X}_{g,w}:=g{\bf X}_{w},\quad{\bf Y}^{\mathrm{aff}}_{g,w}:=g{\bf Y}^{\mathrm{aff}}_{w}.

Note that we have the projection
{\bf X}_{g,w}\to{\bf Y}^{\mathrm{aff}}_{g,w}
, which is a
(\mathcal{U}\times\mathcal{U}^{-})
-bundle.

By
[
Sol17
, Theorem 5.1]
, the ind-scheme
{\bf X}^{\mathrm{aff}}
has divisors
D_{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in\mathbb S
such that the complement of their union is
{\bf X}^{\mathrm{aff}}\setminus G_{\mathrm{ad}}^{\mathrm{aff}}
. The next proposition shows the existence of a finite-dimensional Lie algebra bundle on
{\bf X}^{\mathrm{aff}}
which is analogous to the bundle
\mathcal{R}
over
{\bf X}
.

Proposition 10.7
.

There is a finite-dimensional Lie algebra bundle
{\bf R}
on
{\bf X}^{\mathrm{aff}}
which extends the trivial Lie algebra bundle
G_{\mathrm{ad}}^{\mathrm{aff}}\times\mathfrak g
on the open dense subset
G_{\mathrm{ad}}^{\mathrm{aff}}\subset{\bf X}^{\mathrm{aff}}
and whose restriction to
{\bf Y}^{\mathrm{aff}}
is
J
.

Proof.

Since the projection
{\bf X}_{g,w}\to{\bf Y}^{\mathrm{aff}}_{g,w}
is a
(\mathcal{U}\times\mathcal{U}^{-})
-bundle, the transition functions of
J
and its restrictions to tubular neighbourhoods of its divisors may be used to construct a locally free sheaf
J^{\prime}
on an open subset
{\bf X^{\prime}}
containing the union of
G^{\mathrm{aff}}
and the height
1
prime ideals of
{\bf X}^{\mathrm{aff}}
using the transition functions of
J
. Let
{\bf R}
denote its push-forward to
{\bf X}^{\mathrm{aff}}
. To check that the push-forward is locally free, without loss of generality we may consider its restriction to
{\bf X}_{0}
. But on
{\bf X}_{0}
the push-forward of
J^{\prime}
restricts to the pull-back of a Lie algebra bundle
J
on
{\bf Y}^{\mathrm{aff}}
constructed in Theorem
10.3
, which completes the argument. The rest of the properties follow immediately.
∎

Theorem 10.8
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\Theta}=(\theta_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in S)
, where the
\theta_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{A}
are arbitrary points in the apartment of
T
satisfying the conditions of Section
2.2
. There exist an affine and smooth “wonderful” Bruhat–Tits group scheme
{\mathfrak G}_{{\bf X}^{\mathrm{aff}}}^{\varpi}
on
{\bf X}^{\mathrm{aff}}
together with a canonical isomorphism
\Lie({\mathfrak G}_{{\bf X}^{\mathrm{aff}}}^{\varpi})\simeq{\bf R}
. It further satisfies the following classifying property:

For any point
h\in{\bf X}^{\mathrm{aff}}\setminus G_{\mathrm{ad}}^{\mathrm{aff}}
, let
\mathbb I\subset\mathbb S
be defined by the condition
h\in\cap_{\alpha\in\mathbb{I}}D_{\alpha}
. Let
C_{\mathbb I}\subset{\bf X}
be a smooth curve with generic point in
G_{\mathrm{ad}}^{\mathrm{aff}}
with closed point
h
. Let
U_{h}\subset C_{\mathbb I}
be a formal neighbourhood of
h
. Then, the restriction
{\mathfrak G}_{{\bf X}^{\mathrm{aff}}}^{\varpi}|_{U_{h}}
is isomorphic to the Bruhat–Tits group scheme associated to the concave function
{\text{\linespread m}_{{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}_{I}}}\colon\Phi\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
given by
r\mapsto\sumop\displaylimits_{\alpha\in I}m_{r}(\theta_{\alpha})
.

Proof.

We begin by observing that
{\bf X}_{g,w}
and
{\bf R}\rightarrow{\bf X}^{\mathrm{aff}}
play the roles of
{\bf Y}_{g,w}^{\mathrm{aff}}
and
J\rightarrow{\bf Y}^{\mathrm{aff}}
in the proof of Theorem
10.6
. Therefore, the group scheme
\mathfrak{G}_{g,w}
glue together, and we obtain the global group scheme
{\mathfrak G}_{{\bf X}^{\mathrm{aff}}}^{\varpi}
. The verification of the classifying property follows exactly as in the proof of Theorem
10.1
.
∎

11.
2BT
-group schemes and degenerations of torsors

In this section we assume
\charr(k)=0
. Our aim is to revisit certain smooth, affine group schemes on the minimal resolution of normal surface singularities. We work with the complete local rings and in this discussion stick to the
A_{n}
-type singularities alone and satisfy ourselves with a few remarks on the other types in a brief remark. The senior author used these constructions in
[
Bal22
]
, where
\tt 2BT
-group schemes first occur. The purpose was to construct degenerations of the moduli spaces of principal
G
-bundles on smooth projective curves when the curve is made to degenerate to an irreducible nodal curve.

The picture and notation we use are as in
[
Bal22
]
. The novel feature is that the constructions were made using the geometric McKay correspondence of G. González-Sprinberg and J.-L. Verdier (see
[
GSV83
]
), the role of which is intriguing.

Let
N_{d}=\spec(\frac{k\llbracket t\rrbracket\llbracket x,y\rrbracket}{(x\cdot y-t^{d})})
. We recall that
N_{d}
is a
normal
surface with an isolated singularity of type
\text{A}_{d}
. By the generality of
\text{A}_{d}
-type singularities, one can realize
N_{d}
as a quotient
\sigma\colon D\to N_{d}
of
D:=\spec(\frac{k\llbracket t\rrbracket\llbracket u,v\rrbracket}{(u\cdot v-t)})
by the cyclic group
{\sf\mu_{d}}=\langle\gamma\rangle
, where
x=u^{d}
,
y=v^{d}
,
\zeta
is a primitive
d^{\mathrm{th}}
-root of unity and
{\sf\mu_{d}}
acts on
D
as
follows:

(11.1)

\displaystyle\gamma\cdot(u,v)=\left(\zeta\cdot u,\zeta^{d-1}\cdot v\right).

We consider the following basic diagram for all
d>0
(see
[
GSV83
]
):

\textstyle{{D^{(d)}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{{\sf N}^{(d)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{d}}
\textstyle{0\in D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\textstyle{\penalty\ \penalty\ {N_{d}}\owns c\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}}

where
p_{d}\colon{\sf N}^{(d)}\to N_{d}
is the
minimal resolution of singularities
of
N_{d}
obtained by successively blowing up the singularity, with the exceptional divisor
E^{(d)}=p_{d}^{-1}(c)
having
d-1
rational components, and

(11.7)

\displaystyle D^{(d)}:=\left(D\times_{N_{d}}{\sf N}^{(d)}\right)_{\mathrm{red}}.

The
closed fibre

F^{(d)}
of the canonical morphism
{\sf N}^{(d)}\to\spec(k\llbracket t\rrbracket)
looks like

(11.8)

\displaystyle F^{(d)}=E^{(d)}\cup E(1)\cup E(2),

where
E(1)
and
E(2)
are the inverse images of the branches corresponding to
x=0
and
y=0
in
N_{d}
.

Thus,
F^{(d)}\subset{\sf N}^{(d)}
is a normal crossing divisor with
d+1
components.
By
[
GSV83
, Proposition 2.4]
, the morphism

(11.9)

\displaystyle f\colon D^{(d)}\longrightarrow{\sf N}^{(d)}

is
finite and flat, the minimal platificateur in the sense of Grothendieck
; see
[
GSV83
, Corollary 7, p. 448]
. Since
{\sf N}^{(d)}
is smooth, this implies that
f
is ramified at the generic point of each of the
d-1
rational components of the exceptional divisor
E^{(d)}=p_{d}^{-1}(c)\subset F^{(d)}
.

Let

(11.10)

\displaystyle{\mathscr{T}}_{D}\simeq D\times^{\rho}G

be the trivial
({\sf\mu_{d}},G)
-torsor on
D
(see (
11
)) with a
{\sf\mu_{d}}
-structure given by a homomorphism
\rho\colon{\sf\mu_{d}}\to G
. This gives a homomorphism
\rho\colon{\sf\mu_{d}}\to T
into the maximal torus
T
of
G
. We fix once and for all an isomorphism
T\simeq\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\ell}
. Thus
\rho
determines an ordered pair of integers modulo
d
called the
type

\tau=(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{\ell})
. More precisely, we have a
{\sf\mu_{d}}
-action on
D\times G
, given by

(11.11)

\displaystyle\gamma\cdot(u,v,g)=\left(\zeta\cdot u,\zeta^{-1}\cdot v,\rho(\gamma)\cdot g\right).

Let
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
be the two points in
D^{(d)}
above the origin
0\in D
where the normalization of the curve
u\cdot v=0
meets the fibre
q^{-1}(0)
. We observe that the action of
{\sf\mu_{d}}
is
balanced
at these two marked points;
i.e.
the action of a generator
\zeta
on the tangent spaces to each branch are inverses to each other. For the corresponding dual action in the neighbourhood
D^{\prime}_{0}
(the component with local coordinate
v
), the action is by
\zeta^{-1}
. If we begin with a representation
\rho\colon{\sf\mu_{d}}\to G
of local type
\tau
at a point in a branch, then the corresponding local type for the dual action at the point in the second branch is denoted by
\bar{\tau}
.

Consider the adjoint group scheme
{\mathscr{T}}_{D}(G):={\mathscr{T}}_{D}\times^{G,\Ad}G
on
D
, where
G
acts on itself by inner conjugation. We define the equivariant group scheme

(11.12)

\displaystyle E(G,\tau):=q^{*}\left({\mathscr{T}}_{D}(G)\right)

on
D^{(d)}
of local type
\tau
in the sense that it comes with a
{\sf\mu_{d}}
-action via a representation
\rho\colon{\sf\mu_{d}}\to G
.
Since the morphism
f\colon D^{(d)}\to{\sf N}^{(d)}
is also
finite and flat
, we can take the Weil restriction of scalars

(11.13)

\displaystyle f_{*}\left(E(G,\tau)_{D^{(d)}}\right):={\Res}_{{D^{(d)}}/{\sf N}^{(d)}}\left(E(G,\tau\right)_{D^{(d)}}),

and since
E(G,\tau)_{D^{(d)}}\to D^{(d)}
is a smooth (affine) group scheme, the basic properties of Weil restriction of scalars (
cf.

[
Edi92
, Lemma 2.2]
) show that
f_{*}(E(G,\tau))
is a smooth group scheme on
{\sf N}^{(d)}
together with a
{\sf\mu_{d}}
-action. By taking invariants under the action of
{\sf\mu_{d}}
and noting that we are over characteristic zero, by
[
Edi92
, Proposition 3.4]
, we obtain the smooth (affine) group scheme on
{\sf N}^{(d)}
obtained by taking
invariant direct images
:

(11.14)

\displaystyle\tiny\text{\linespread H}^{G}_{\tau,{\sf N}^{(d)}}=(f^{\sf\mu_{d}}_{*})((E(G,\tau)_{D^{(d)}}))

(see
[
BS15
, Definition 4.1.3]
).
The
2BT
-group scheme of type
\tau
with generic fibre
G
of singularity type
\text{A}_{d}
associated to
\theta_{\tau}
is defined to be the affine group scheme
\tiny\text{\linespread H}^{G}_{\tau,{\sf N}^{(d)}}
from (
11.14
) on the regular surface
{\sf N}^{(d)}
. This process defines a distinguished collection of
2BT
-group scheme
\big\{{\tiny\text{\linespread H}^{G}_{\tau,{\sf N}^{(d)}}}\big\}_{\tau}
indexed by the type
\tau
.

11.1. The McKay correspondence revisited

We recall the geometric interpretation of the McKay correspondence given by González-Sprinberg and Verdier (see
[
GSV83
]
). Let
\Irr^{o}({\sf\mu_{d}})\subset\Irr({\sf\mu_{d}})
be the non-trivial irreducible representations of
{\sf\mu_{d}}
, and let
\Irr(E^{(d)})
denote the set of irreducible rational components of the exceptional divisor of the minimal resolution
p_{d}\colon{\sf N}^{(d)}\to N_{d}
from (
11
). Let
\psi
be a non-trivial character of
{\sf\mu_{d}}=\langle\gamma\rangle
. Then
\psi
corresponds to
\zeta\mapsto\zeta^{s}
, where
\zeta
is the primitive
d^{\mathrm{th}}
-root of unity chosen above and
1\leq s\leq d-1
. Let
L_{\psi}
be the equivariant line bundle on
D
where
{\sf\mu_{d}}
acts on
D\times k
as
\gamma\cdot(u,v,a)=(\zeta\cdot u,\zeta^{d-1}\cdot v,\zeta^{s}\cdot a)
,
a\in k
.
A
{\sf\mu_{d}}
-invariant section
h
of this line bundle is given by the relation
\{\gamma\cdot\text{\linespread h}\}(u,v)=\text{\linespread h}(\gamma\cdot(u,v))=\zeta^{s}\text{\linespread h}(u,v)
, and hence the
{\sf\mu_{d}}
-invariant sections are generated by
u^{s}
and
v^{d-s}
.
Let
\mathcal{L}_{\psi}:=f^{\sf\mu_{d}}_{*}(q^{*}(L_{\psi}))
be the induced line bundle on
\sf N^{(d)}
. This is a line bundle since
f
is finite and flat.

Mckay correspondence following González-Sprinberg and Verdier
. There is a bijection
\Irr^{o}({\sf\mu_{d}})\to\Irr(E^{(d)})
,
\psi\mapsto E_{\psi}
, such that for any
E_{j}\in\Irr(E^{(d)})
, we have

(11.15)

\displaystyle c_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{\psi})\cdot E_{j}=\begin{cases}0&\quad\text{if }E_{j}\neq E_{\psi},\\
1&\quad{\text{i}f}E_{j}=E_{\psi}.\end{cases}

The above statement implies that the first Chern class
c_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{\psi})
can be represented by an
effective
divisor
\delta_{\psi}\subset\sf N^{(d)}
which meets
F^{(d)}
transversally at a unique point which lies in
E_{\psi}
.

We interpret this on the side of the surface
D^{(d)}
. Consider the reduced fibre
\tilde{E}:=q^{-1}(0)_{\mathrm{red}}
. The group
{\sf\mu_{d}}
-fixes the divisor
q^{-1}(0)
and hence its reduced subscheme
\tilde{E}
. There is a smooth curve
\delta^{\prime}_{\psi}\in D^{(d)}
which meets the divisor
